# Continuity State

Canon established by the bootstrap phase. This file is the long-term memory for batch writers; keep it compact and update it when drafting changes a fact.

## Setting

Morrowfen, a city on a black peat sink in a salt basin, in **Morrowfen Reckoning Year 412**. The Yearspire stands on Stillstep Island and is the city's bank, courthouse, emergency office, archive, and power plant. The six public sections are the Threshold, the Tally Halls, the Season Vaults, the Witness Rooms, the Hollow Galleries, and the sealed Deep Engine. Principal districts: Tallow Ward, Bell Stair, Rainmarket, Crown Terrace, Hallow Ground (the Unwritten Quarter, missing from the tower's map and rolls), and the Underbank.

## Rules that must never bend

- A **loan** has five parts: need, pledge, scope, witness, settlement. A missing material term makes it false-witnessed, which makes the responsibility chain visible and contestable.
- Four forms: **vigor** (endurance, healing, resistance), **craft** (practiced skill from a possible working life), **sequence** (a favored local event, paid by a counterweight), and **record** (institutional recognition, not physical fact).
- A year is not a coin and the tower shows no numeric balance. Effects are real now and the cost is real later; neither cancels the other.
- A Yearwell cannot bring back the dead, invent impossible outcomes, show the one true future, safely move a living person backward, or erase a settled past.
- A valid loan cannot make a person immortal, resurrect the dead, or promise a victory no plausible future supports.
- **Ledger Sight** reads visible entries, witness chain, and immediate contradiction on physical contact. It never predicts, never gives percentages or verdicts, and costs headaches, tunnel vision, and a recent memory gap after repeated use.
- **Boundary Hold** suspends one named settlement briefly. The delay is stored in Marek's own yearline. When the hold ends, the contract's chain collects: the borrower, then the surety, then the living clerk who certified the entry when the chain is false-witnessed. A Hold adds nobody to that chain, so an unpayable debt still ends with the affected district and Marek carries the toll of the delay as a year scar. He chooses the settlement and the duration, never the destination, and the cost can never reach an uninvolved bystander.
- The **First Account** is a reserve created from the Last Convocation's lost future, pledged under the **Founder's Clause** with no living consent. It is an unappealed founding claim, and every use of it is false-witnessed in the strict sense.
- The crossing was made through a **one-way year seam**: in the country that no longer exists, the founders collapsed their own well inward. It cannot be aimed, reopened, or used to carry a living passenger twice, and its price is the missing year. The oldest surviving copies bear the signature **A-0**. The seam can be disclosed and voided. It cannot be recovered.
- The **Open Account** can exist only after the founding claim is released in the Deep Engine and a quorum of three independent witness rooms records that the Clause has no living consent chain. After that, an effect is funded by present contributions or a narrow pledge made by a living person in person, and a shortfall is paid by the public reserve.
- No new cosmic threat, god, or prophecy enters late. The final enemy is Edren Sable and the First Account system, seeded in Volume 1 through the A-0 signature.

## Cast and starting positions

- **Marek Vale**, 28, third-grade settlement clerk in Bell Stair, lives above a lockkeeper's shop in Tallow Ward. Wants Tomas's debt cleared; fears becoming the official who decides whose future is acceptable to spend. Turns relationships into procedures and delays decisions until he can prove a safe answer.
- **Tomas Vale**, 34, Marek's older brother, a bridgewright who took a vigor loan after a flood wrecked the family workshop. Resents the ledger for making the danger legible after the fact.
- **Lio Vale**, 9, Tomas's daughter. Asks the direct question adults avoid. Not magical, not an oracle.
- **Tamsin Vale**, Marek's mother, a seamstress who signed an old body loan to keep Marek alive through a childhood fever and knows more about his first mark than she says.
- **Neris Kade**, 30, independent field examiner, sensitive to year scars, distrusted by clerks and unromantically opposed to Marek in Volume 1. No rival or second love interest exists.
- **Sera Holt**, 52, chief physician, Marek's mentor, hiding the clinic's own hidden loan.
- **Nim Rook**, 19, night courier and former copy-room assistant, trying to prove her missing sister Ilen existed.
- **Ilya Senn**, 41, senior Lantern Warden who has secretly disabled a settlement valve to save Tallow Ward. The Trust will call it sabotage.
- **Hesper Rann**, 46, Marek's supervisor, falsified a Tallow Ward water entry so a floodgate loan could be sealed in time. Not a cartoon villain; arrested after Volume 1.
- **Hesper's falsified entry is the volume's first legal wrong, and an under-clerk who countersigned it in good faith is the person Boundary Hold injures.**
- **Ovid Merrow**, 47, Sable Compact broker; **Chancellor Ysara Venn**, 58, emergency governor; **Seren Calder**, 40, settlement marshal; **Lyra Dain**, 36, Quiet Choir leader; **Edren Sable**, First Auditor, copied and renewed, present only as A-0 until Volume 11.

### Volume 01 supporting cast (named in `outline/volume-01.md`)

- **Ondra Kelb**, 20, junior counter-clerk in the Chamber, three weeks in the post. Countersigned Hesper's falsified Tallow Ward water entry in good faith. He is the last living name on the chain, he is injured by the volume's Boundary Hold, he lives, and he is not written to forgive Marek quickly.
- **Renata Vole**, 57, Deputy Settlement Steward, Yearspire Trust. Signs emergency settlements and treats an appeal as a scheduling problem. Reassigned, never conceding she was wrong.
- **Aldis Kern**, 44, Tallow Ward water-yard captain. Four yards, about four hundred people. The ward's public voice; also the man who testifies against Ilya.
- **Bohdan Rezek**, 51, fuel-yard foreman. First visible victim of the ward settlement; his hands give out in the copy room in Chapter 4.
- **Nadja Ostrow**, 38, kitchen cook, one of the eleven named households on the ward levy.
- **Marda Senn**, Ilya's older sister, a Warden's ledger clerk who keeps her own pump counts. Her name leaves the warden's roll in Chapter 22; she does not return in this volume.
- **Bel Harrow**, Crown March representative, arrives in Chapter 31 and chairs the emergency appeals board.

### Volume 01 local terms (setting detail, not new world rules)

The Silt (the river), Bell Weir, the Tallow Gate (the ward floodgate and the boundary named in the ward file), the Third Sluice (the settlement valve Ilya holds open), the posting (an announced settlement date on the Threshold board), the levy (a ward's standing repayment obligation, distinct from a single loan), the copy room and public register in the Tally Halls, and the standing reserve (the reserve cited in the falsified entry, which resolves through one footnote to the oldest reference volume).

### Volume 01 locked instruments in the Tallow file (do not blur)

- **The ward settlement entry — the false-witnessed instrument.** Hesper signs it as supervisor on the ninth day; **Ondra Kelb countersigns it at the counter-desk the same afternoon** in good faith. The invented reserve and the omitted material term live on this paper, so this is the instrument the Chapter 10 chain walks to and the instrument suspended in Chapter 8.
- **The clerk's certificate of posting — a separate one-page paper.** A settlement counts as posted rather than provisional only with a third-grade clerk's countersignature on it, and **Marek signs it on the eleventh day** in Chapter 5 after the Sight shows a clean file. He did not write the lie; he made it collectible. He surrenders this paper in Chapter 44, and it is the Chamber's instrument against him in Chapter 32.
- The chain follows the instrument, not the folder, so there is never more than one living certifier where the collection lands. Marek's name is never the chain's terminal name.
- **Levy roll:** eleven named households, the posting names the first four, and **the Vale household is seventh of the eleven** — Marek's own family is on the roll, which is the premise of Chapter 3's central question. This is a separate instrument from Tomas's vigor loan, which is lawful, is not the fraud, and is not on this roll.

### Volume 01 founding clauses (canon, added to the bible during the Volume 01 planning phase)

The **standing hold** is a founding remedy in the margin of the oldest reference volume, in the same hand as the blank first line and the signature **A-0**: no settlement may be taken before the year is ended. It is held by **the clerk of record**, an office retired a few centuries after the opening and held by nobody now, which is why the clause is taught as history, why Marek's use of it in Chapter 8 is unauthorized, and why the delay has no office to sit in and goes into his body instead. It changes a due date and nothing else, and it is used once in the series. The Trust's standing order on the sealed founding page — no clerk may erase the signature, no clerk may copy the page — is live instruction, not oversight; the volume's final chapter breaks it by hand.

## Volume 1 frame

Central pressure: a flood-season water shortage forces Tallow Ward to settle a loan that will draw future recovery from several residents and leave Tomas's bridge debt unresolved. Resolution: Marek exposes the falsified entry, forces a public emergency appeals board, converts the scheduled settlement of Tomas's loan into supervised road work, and brings the valve sabotage into the record. The ward is saved for this season; the old debt is not erased. The unauthorized Boundary Hold injures the junior clerk who countersigned Hesper's entry, and that injury stays in the volume. Next question: who owns the blank first entry, and what does A-0 mean?

Full plan: `outline/series.md` and `outline/ending.md` are written and authoritative. `outline/volume-01.md` and `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0001.md` are written and authoritative as of the Volume 01 planning phase. Batch 0001 owns Chapters 1–10 and its cards specify the batch midpoint (Ch 6), the climax (Ch 10), the panel budget, and the locked new names and local terms.

## Volume 01 chain of cause (locked in the Volume 01 planning)

The falsified Tallow Ward water entry invents an offsetting **standing reserve** so the ward does not appear to pay for the gate loan Hesper needed sealed before its notice period expired. The lie bought speed, not safety: the ward really is short, and the river is the reason. The invented reserve resolves through a single footnote in the oldest reference volume in the Season Vaults case, to that volume's first line, which is blank, and beneath the blank is the signature **A-0**. In the same old hand, in the margin, is the founding remedy this volume uses: a standing hold, by which no settlement may be taken before the year is ended, held by the clerk of record. A hold stores the delay in the holder's own yearline and adds nobody to the responsibility chain. The volume's lesson is that the delay is his and the collection is not his to direct.

## Volume 01 facts established by batch 0001 (Chapters 1–10)

### Instruments and pages in the Tallow folder (do not blur)

- **The ward settlement entry** — false-witnessed. Hesper's own hand, signed the ninth day at the sixth bell; the settlement date filled in and initialled by Hesper on the eleventh day; **Ondra Kelb's pencil countersignature on the totals, dated the ninth day**. Carries the invented standing-reserve recital and the omission of page three from the ward's notice. This is the instrument the Chapter 10 chain walks and the instrument suspended in Chapter 8.
- **The clerk's certificate of posting** — a separate one-page paper, **countersigned by Marek on the eleventh day**, with *Yes* written by Marek on the posting desk's query that records him as a party. It is the paper that made the settlement due at the peak, and it is what he surrenders in Chapter 44.
- **Page three is the labour-pledge schedule.** Eleven lines, four columns: household, days, surety, witness. Eleven sureties named. **All eleven witness cells blank.** It is cited at the foot of the entry's fourth clause, filed in the file, and **never given to the ward**: the ward's copy runs one, two, four, five.
- The chain follows the instrument, not the folder. Marek's name is never the chain's terminal name.

### The fraud's two halves, as established

- **Visible half:** the omitted material term — page three, the eleven unsigned sureties, never disclosed to the parties the levy was posted against.
- **Unauditable half:** the offsetting standing-reserve recital in the fourth clause. It resolves through **one footnote** to the first entry of the oldest reference volume in the Season Vaults case, which is **blank** and signed **A-0**; the marginal standing hold in the same hand is the unauthorized remedy Marek uses in Chapter 8. **Nobody can audit that line at a counter-desk, and that is what a recital is for.**
- The reserve exists so the ward will not appear to pay for the Tallow Gate loan, whose thirty-day notice expired on the twelfth. The lie bought the ward its floodgate; it did not reduce the ward's cost. Hesper did not manufacture the shortage.

### Volume 01 local facts now on the page

- The Tallow yards are the **Gate yard, the Tanner's yard, the Fuel basin and Ropewalk** (a standpipe). The Gate tank was dry on the ninth, half on the twelfth, full by the fifteenth.
- **Tallow Ward's standing schedule is 40 pints a person a day** for a person who works and 30 for one who does not, infants *as the mother is able*. The ward is about four hundred people, so the schedule is **160 hundreds of pints a day**. Every water figure in Movement I is expressed against that number, and the arithmetic closes: the junction meter (what the account reads) certifies at **640 hundreds = 4× the schedule**; the four yard counters (what the ward actually took) show **440 hundreds = 2¾× the schedule**; the difference of 200 hundreds is **a shade under a third of the meter reading** and **closer to half of the true draw**. The standing schedule is therefore a shade over a third of the real draw, and the figure the certification gives the ward is close to half again as much as that. Do not reintroduce absolute figures that break this, and do not call the over-read "forty per cent" — it is not.
- **Six bells a day and six a night**, counted separately and never mixed, roughly an hour apart. The day count runs from the first at the opening of the desks to the **sixth when the copy room closes** and the seventh in the evening; the **eighth day bell is struck in the morning**, which is why every hearing in this volume is "at the eighth bell" in the morning. The night watch runs six bells from the last of the light to the light again: the **fourth of the night is the flood peak** and is the hour in Hesper's posting, and the **sixth of the night is where a watch ends**, which is when the held settlement came due and when the form says the year ends.
- **Petross Oleksen**, 61, senior hand on the Tallow line, holds the junction screen. The screen and the bypass are maintained on **account 4-Crown, irrigation**, serving the Crown Terrace north gardens, which draw from Bell Weir and have never drawn from the Tallow line. Cleared and reset on the thirteenth; the difference column on Marda Senn's slate was ruled out of use. The two days bought did not touch the bill.
- **Rezek's draw:** sixteen days granted against sealed terms of fourteen under a wet-weather rider, granted administratively at the counter-desk by Marek, who wrote himself into the margin. The Witness Room was not sitting. Rezek's hands seized and the record and his body disagreed in public. He is the first visible victim and he lives.
- **The peak-day delivery certification** was never signed. The ward's lawful requirement for the peak is therefore a quantity that exists in no document. The hearing offered on the tenth for the twelfth is **cancelled**; the hearing is **the fifteenth at the eighth bell, the Trust's third floor, Renata Vole presiding**, with the ward's copy and the file's copy to be compared in the room.
- **The hold notice** went onto the posting board with *no office of record* on the line and Marek's name under it as applicant. It is still up.
- Neris Kade got **91 people** out of the low kitchens on the night of the fourteenth onto a list in her own hand tied to her finger with string, and counted **391** in the Sill Road school house that night and the days before it. The ward is about four hundred (Chapter 1 records four hundred and six signatures on a petition and calls it the whole ward), so no number attached to the school house may exceed that.

### Ondra Kelb's injury as established (Chapter 10)

Not paralysis. He can move the hand and will be able to lift a cup at sixty. What is gone is **the years of it** — the fine use of the right hand, which arrives at the end of a life and has been taken out of the middle. It is expected to be permanent and there is nothing in the ledger to put back, because it was not a loan: no pledge, no surety, no settlement, no day on the calendar called the fourteenth. He is not at the fifteenth hearing. Marek must state it in the order **entry, Kelb's name, his own**, because two signed papers from two clerks live in one folder.

### The cost carried by Marek so far

Ledger Sight was used on a live record five times in five days — Chapters 4, 5, 6, 7 and 10 — and the Chapter 7 use is silent: it confirms a payee line at the fortieth row of the ward water account and gives no warning. A lost midday purchase on the tenth — he is carrying it and cannot remember what is in it — a lost quarter hour on the twelfth night, and a **lost hour between the fifth and sixth bells on the fourteenth**. Six bells of hold: nosebleed, tunnel vision, tremor, and a **year scar that has not appeared yet** — Sera Holt told him to be sitting in a chair when it does, and it is not dramatic.

### Terminology note for later batches

The prose term for Marek's Chapter 8 act is **standing hold** (the founding remedy in the margin). The batch cards also call it a **Boundary Hold**; that is the bible's name for the general power stage and it does not appear in the prose, because no character in the building has ever seen one. A later batch must not put the phrase *Boundary Hold* into a clerk's mouth.

The card in `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0001.md` places Chapter 8 on "the thirteenth night into the fourteenth" and Chapter 9 on "the fourteenth night." The prose resolves both to **one night, the night of the fourteenth**, with the sixth bell in the small hours of the fifteenth; the card is the error and the prose is correct. Chapter 8's hold is taken **after the last of the light and before the first bell**, not at the fifth bell. Six bells is not a figure Marek chooses: it is the length of the night watch, and the batch now says so on the page once, at the moment the panel is struck.

### Batch 0001 facts as corrected by the review-repair pass (prose is authoritative)

- **The Third Sluice order is dated the seventh and is still in force on the night of the fourteenth**, so Ilya's breach of *it* is **seven days**, not five, and his breach of his own authority to keep the valve open is **the twelve days since the second**. Chapter 6 no longer says the valve has been open five days. Both numbers are load-bearing for the Chapters 23 and 47 oath tests, so do not restate them loosely.
- **The Chapter 4 panel's two names are the borrower and the surety** on the line Rezek's draw stands on. The prose now says so, which is what makes the Chapter 18 Pella Rusk payoff legible rather than a surprise. The second name is still not produced by the Sight; it is read off the schedule by hand.
- **The Chapter 5 opening now carries the bakery memory cost** from Chapter 4: Marek has eaten and cannot remember eating, only a parcel in his hand. Do not write "he had eaten nothing" again.
- **Chapter 9's standpipe output and Chapter 7's are both in pints** (about seventy an hour), never gallons. The ward schedule is a pints schedule.
- Hesper has **nineteen years** since he last signed for a ward; Kern has been **the captain of Tallow's water for sixteen years**; Sera has been a physician **twenty-six years**. Those are the numbers on the page now.

### Supporting figures added in batch 0001 (all Volume 01, all still live)

- **Pella Rusk**, the ward schoolmistress at the Tallow Gate school, is one of the eleven sureties named in an unsigned cell on page three. Marek finds the name in Chapter 4 and tells nobody, including her. She does not know. This is the seed of her Chapter 18 school-roll chapter and of the public school-roll number argued in Chapter 37; later batches must not make it a surprise that she has a reason to care, and must not have her learn of it before Chapter 18.
- **Sedge**, a third-grade clerk of nine years on the intake floor, refuses to countersign a certification for a district he is not clerk of, and is the clerk Hesper threatens in Chapters 4 and 7.
- **Ferun**, a Tallow yard-man, whose boy of thirteen went into the Underbank for a neighbour's goods and came out with a broken leg. Aldis Kern's rule is that the Underbank damage is named before the sluice in any account of that night, and he wants it said that way permanently. Owned by Chapters 23 and 47.
- The **posting desk's duty-clerk's woman**, twenty-two years in the copy room and the duty clerk's deputy, who is going to the hearing and who has never in twenty-two years had a roll come up she could not place. She is not a source and does not become one.
- Unnamed and functional: the Threshold hearing officer; the ward's fair-fast writing hand that wrote the assessment; Ovsek, the tanner who witnessed a fortnight of recovery; Vollner, a meter reader dismissed twenty-two years ago.

### The ward roll planted at the close of Chapter 10 (owned by Volume 5, not repeated in this volume)

A working ward roll, four fingers thick, rained on, left under a weight on the posting desk by **Nim Rook** at the first bell for the Tallow clerk. Thirty-one entries in a fair small hand, the last dated the ninth day of the rains. The district column reads **four, and a name — Kallow — over and over**, under a head-line reading *assigned*, and the yard or street column was **never filled in**. The tower's four-hundred-page register *Districts and Rolls* has no district numbered four and no Kallow anywhere in it; the districts that *are* in the register (seven Tallow Gate, nine Ropewalk, eleven the Tanner's, thirty-one the Underbank) are the ones the copy-room clerk can name off the top of his head, and the roll's four is the one he cannot. Marek keeps it out of the Tallow file, because a file would make it an exhibit in a levy hearing, and puts it in his coat. **Nobody names the quarter, nobody explains the code, and this plant does not recur in Movements II–V.** It is not mentioned at all in Chapters 11–20.

## Volume 01 facts established by batch 0002 (Chapters 11–20)

Prose is authoritative over the batch cards wherever they disagree. The batch card's dates and the prose's dates agree day-for-day; the only clock change is that **Chapter 11 runs to the sixth bell and Chapters 12–13 straddle the eighth**, because the ward cannot be in the yard and on the causeway and in the Trust's third-floor room inside the same bell.

### The calendar of the batch (now fixed; later batches must not restate it loosely)

The ninth of the rains is a **Wednesday**, and every weekday in the volume follows from that one fact, so do not re-derive it: the tenth Thursday, the eleventh Friday, the twelfth Saturday, the thirteenth Sunday, the fourteenth Monday, the **fifteenth Tuesday**, the sixteenth Wednesday, the seventeenth Thursday, the eighteenth Friday, the **nineteenth Saturday**, the twentieth Sunday, the twenty-first Monday, the twenty-second Tuesday, the twenty-third Wednesday, the **twenty-fourth Thursday**. Every Wednesday reference in Chapters 3, 5, 9, 14, 16 and 17 is the ninth or the sixteenth; every Monday in Chapters 12, 13 and 14 is the fourteenth; the Saturday in Chapters 11 and 19 is the nineteenth; the Tuesday Rusk names in Chapter 18 is the fifteenth. The ward school closes its books on the twenty-third and its attendance is suspended from the twenty-fourth. The **district account's figures came out on the fifteenth** (the copy two men in good coats put on Rusk's step, and the one Kelb read from a schoolhouse window on the sixteenth) and it was **struck in a hand by the water office on the eighteenth**, reason *obligation in course of settlement*. Hesper's schedule offer names the twenty-fourth as the first dry day and never as a weekday. The ward hears the claim in Chapter 13 on the fifteenth, the room is called at the eighth bell and the parties are seated at the ninth.

*Corrected at the batch 0002 review.* Chapter 7 had put the thirteenth on a Wednesday, which made the fourteenth a Thursday and broke every date in batch 0002. The chapter now reads **Sunday**, the two stray "Thursday" references in Chapters 17 and 19 have been moved to the days they actually describe, and Chapter 11's "Friday the twenty-third" is now simply the twenty-third, because the twenty-third is a Wednesday and the twenty-fourth is a Thursday. If a later chapter needs a weekday, count from the ninth, not from Chapter 7.

### Hearing outcome on the fifteenth (Chapter 13) — locked

- The Trust's third-floor room holds seventy; the ward's four hundred are in the corridor and the north stair landing. Vole gives Neris Kade a **courtesy**, not a right, to speak at the end of the table.
- **Vole rules page three was never served on the ward**, so no party may rely on it: all eleven unsigned sureties are set aside for the purposes of the proceeding. Kelb is at the clinic and the ward is told so; he is not in the room and has given no account.
- **The file's stated cause is an operator's failure**: a maintenance failure of the junction screen and the bypass at the second junction, the maintenance responsibility of the district's irrigation contractor **Hask and Rundle** upon **Crown Terrace account 4-Crown**, corrected on the thirteenth at the water office's expense. Every word is true, and it is load-bearing for the reserve recital, because it puts the ward's thirst on a Crown Terrace maintenance contract.
- **Both copies are entered as the same document** and the difference between them is noted at the foot of the record and **not adjudicated**. It is a permanent record. This is Marek's only win and it costs the ward.
- **The settlement is certified lawful.** There is still no signed peak-day delivery certification, and Vole notes the fact; the ward's lawful requirement for the peak night is the standing schedule, **160 hundreds of pints**; the excess drawn at the ordinary wage is a lawful settlement and stands. Vole says the number four hundred and forty out loud when Kade asks, and the officer opens the four yard books on the table to let her.

### Locked numbers (do not reintroduce differently)

- Standing schedule **160** hundreds of pints a day; junction account **640**; the four yard counters **440**; the roll's foot figure of excess **279 hundreds and 40 pints**; Neris Kade's own count of the yards **441** (she counts the Ropewalk standpipe by the hour, the yards by the day). One hundred and sixty is the only figure the Trust will acknowledge. The four hands do not agree and the difference is unclosable.
- **The ward's lawful requirement for the peak night is 160 hundreds of pints and the ward drank 440.** From the fifteenth forward the levy looks deserved in every room that is not that room.
- The **district account is overdrawn by 240 per cent** — that is the locked figure and the only gloss allowed on it is *a shade over three times the lawful draw*. The account's figures came out on the fifteenth and it was struck in a hand by the water office on the eighteenth, reason *obligation in course of settlement*. The levy's days at the ordinary wage are drawn out of the same account. The office balances it by cutting lines in a written order, and **a school year is the cheapest line on the page**.

### The three private documents now in Marek's own book

1. The footnote, the blank line, the knife-cut initials and the marginal standing hold, copied in a vault on the twelfth against a standing order (Chapter 6).
2. Three unofficial renderings of page three: the back page of his own book, the hearing-bundle sheet written in the levy office's hand on the Chair step with Kelb as copyist (Chapter 9), and the wrapped hand-copy of the sixteenth (Chapter 15). **None of the three may be produced in any room.**
3. The cause line of the recount of the twenty-first, in the levy office's hand, bottom margin (Chapter 20).

The rule Chapter 15 establishes, and the batch's real lesson: **a hand-copy is a certification, and a certification is only good against a document the Tower may certify. There is a class of page no clerk may copy, anything carrying its words carries the bar with it, and a man who makes one of those has not made a copy — he has made a second document, which nobody in the room is permitted to produce.** The difference is procedural, not moral. A suspended settlement is also not a state that was ever made, so the Tower will not copy one and **no office funds the copy of a settlement that does not exist**, which is why the work is done for nothing by whoever is nearest.

### Hesper's second certification — DRAFTED IN CHAPTER 20, NOT FILED

Record the drafting and stop there. A recount is a lawful step (fourth clause of the settlement desk's standing practice): a supervisor's re-addition of a certified total, with a third-grade clerk present, entered in the desk's book with the hour, the clerk's name and the reason. The cause line is written on the form **before the arithmetic is checked** and reads, in full: *Cause: the totals of this entry were added at the counter-desk from a defective schedule.* Every word is true; the false-witnessed instrument's counter-desk totals were added from the schedule cited at the foot of the fourth clause, and that makes Kelb's countersignature the cause of the ward's shortage. The witness was **Emil Skar**. Hesper asked after Marek's arm and told him the ward has its water, and meant both. Hesper does not know the page exists and believes the questioner is Neris Kade. He has already decided the ward must be paid and is choosing who. **The recount is not filed, not reported to a third party, not withdrawn, and not resolvable in this batch. The filing belongs to Chapter 39.** Nothing in Chapters 11–20 touches it.

### Hesper's offer of the seventeenth (Chapter 16)

One line on a sheet in the supervisor's room, countersigned, with **Hesper's name and no other**: *W-4 (supervised work, settlement schedule). Vale, T. — north-road span. Schedule written out, named, scheduled; independent witness, third party. First dry day, the 24th.* It is **the settlement schedule only** — the supervised road work, not the years. The draw posted on the fourteenth as scheduled, the vigor is working, and the years already taken are not returned. Hesper offered it a second time at the end of Chapter 20. Marek has not accepted it, has not refused it, and has not destroyed it.

**The hinge beat of Chapter 16, on the Chair step:** Marek works out that **not one of the two of them said the boy's name.** Hesper had gone clean over the counter-desk junior twice in that room — once inside a paragraph about a clinic that would treat nine people free for a fortnight, once inside the four dead people he found on the ninth — and Marek himself had said *I carried him down a lane* and *I know you know about the hand* and had meant both. Two men in a room with a door in it, being entirely reasonable with each other over a sheet with a date on it, and the whole cost of it borne by a third man who was not in the room and was not named in it. His conclusion, and it is the chapter's real ending: **it did not take a villain. It took a supervisor with a schedule in a drawer and a clerk with a reason to want it, and about eleven minutes.** Nobody has been cruel. This is the fact Chapter 25 turns over, and it is why Marek cannot win the argument in Chapter 25 by calling Hesper a monster.

### The three things a surgeon's morning and a Fund's room established

- **Sera Holt: there is no form.** No office owes a yearline mark, no trust and no reserve, only a vault book of marks kept by a man called **Osk** on the second floor, with no fee, because the tower never decided a person should be charged for being damaged. The only question left in Kelb's life is whose the years were.
- **Kelb's injury is not paralysis and not a disability.** He can lift a basket and a cup and will hold a cup at sixty. What is gone is the years of the fine use of the right hand. He was out of the clinic on the afternoon of the fifteenth and lucid throughout the batch.
- **Marek's own year scar surfaced on the fifteenth** and was not understood until the twentieth. It is a ruled line about a finger's width, parallel to and an inch below the old mark on the inside of his forearm, **crossed with four small notches at even intervals — the same four-notch spacing and the same slight drag at the end of the third as the mark Lio asked about in Chapter 5.** It is not a wound and not a disability. He noticed it in a physician's language first ("that is a notching… it is the kind of mark a yearline makes on a person when a delay has been put in the body and left there"), refused to connect it, and did not connect it because the number four on his arm is a number he has been refused twice. He dates it himself as **the fourteenth** in Chapter 19 and writes it into his own book in pencil in Chapter 20. **It is the same four notches at the same intervals in the same place, and nobody in the book has yet said why, and Chapter 34 owns that.** Marek also has a four-inch cut on the same forearm, opened and closed on the fifteenth, which came from holding a page down through the hold.

### New Volume 01 names introduced in batch 0002 (all still live)

- **Zorka Vendl**, 61, secretary of the **Benevolent Fund of the Counter-Clerks of the Chamber of Accounts**, nineteen years. The Fund's one room is over a chandler's shop, eleven feet by nine, and every inch of two walls is shelf. She is the instrument of the scene, not its villain. The form has a box for *injury sustained in the course of duty* and no box for what happened. The Fund pays the clinic, the cot, the medicine and a hand-book; it does not pay for the years. The Fund's levy is a rate on the hall paid out of every clerk's wage and **has run at a deficit every year since the flood years**. The **discharge book** holds forty-one marks in nineteen years: twenty-four single strokes, thirteen double (a hand, an eye, a season's recovery, a hip — the Fund does not ask which), and **four triple strokes, meaning a clerk who never came back to the hall**; one of the four is the brother of the woman who sat in the clinic waiting room, and she asked Vendl to find him and Vendl could not.
- **Milena Kelb**, 43, laundress, one street off Sill Row, eleven years on the street. She asked for the fourth chair in the clinic waiting room specifically. She carries four written facts and wants them said in her order; she is not hostile and that is worse. She asks Marek to stand in the schoolroom on the nineteenth and be a wall, which he does.
- **Jitka Kelb**, eleven, Kelb's sister, at the ward school on Sill Row.
- **Emil Skar**, junior counter-clerk, six weeks in the post on the sixteenth. He was the counter-desk clerk who went to the washroom at the noon bell on the ninth and came back to find the entry already countersigned. He asked the first question anybody has asked in six years about the Fund's form — when the duty in a night watch begins, and the answer is **the day the watch began**, which for Kelb is the night of the fourteenth, four hours before he was carried down a lane. He is the **eleven counter-clerks' mutual's** first recruit and the witness at the Chapter 20 recount.
- **Bohum Ostrek**, 33, north-road hand, six years on the road, raised in the salt country. Took the crew's draw on the sixteenth of the rains on the same vigor form as Tomas — twenty-six months of his own recovery, scope the north-road span and the load path, two witnesses, settlement eleven days a season at the ordinary wage — and both his hands locked in the middle of a lift on the eighteenth. The clause is lawful and stays lawful. Ostrek's witness is called Cveta.
- **Hask and Rundle**, irrigation works, the district's maintenance contractor upon Crown Terrace account 4-Crown. Named only as the party responsible for the junction screen and the bypass in the file's cause line. No individual in the firm is named and none needs to be.
- **Osk**, keeper of the vault book of yearline marks on the second floor, no fee.
- **Master Rill**, a junior counter-clerk in Chapter 14, the one who asks whether a countersignature means somebody checked it.
- **Coutts and Weaver**, the two witnesses on a vigor form read aloud in Chapter 19. Functional.

### New Volume 01 local terms (setting detail, not new world rules)

- **the Counter-Clerks' Fund** and **the Fund's book** (the discharge book) — the mutual the counter-desk clerks keep, which pays a dead or hurt clerk's household and is the only thing in the building that turns an injury into a claim.
- **a recount** — the fourth clause of the settlement desk's standing practice: a supervisor's re-addition of a certified total, with a third-grade clerk present, entered in the desk's book with the hour, the clerk's name and the reason. Lawful.
- **a cause line** — the sentence on a recount's form saying why the recount was done, written before the arithmetic is checked.
- **the fourth chair** — a waiting-room position at the Morrowfen clinic. Only Milena Kelb's use of it is on the page.
- **a notching** — a physician's word for a ruled line crossed with even notches. Osk's book has one line about it and the line is the shortest in the book and it does not say what it is for.

### What Chapter 14 is, structurally

It is the one chapter in the batch with a **deliberate point-of-view change at a section break**: the first two-thirds are close-third on **Ondra Kelb** (with Marek entirely absent from the first half, as the batch card requires), and the last third is close-third on **Marek**, who enters the Fund's room at the last of the light. This is intentional and is not a slip. It is also the chapter where **eleven counter-clerks learn what a counter-signature certifies** and where Marek acquires a debt of a kind he has no procedure for: his name is now in the room of the counter-desk in connection with the ward file, and one of them was in the room in Chapter 20.

### The ward's scar examination, Chapter 19 — the method, which is a ward document

Three piles: **the Silt's years** (nobody's instrument, owed by the river), **the vigor clauses** (form number, date of draw, witnesses; lawful, witnessed, informed; twelve already showing a season's scar and nine of the twelve fuel-yard or bridge hands), and **the hold's years** (the fourteenth, six bells, one clerk, no form number, no instrument, everybody's body). The founding rule: **a scar has to be matched to an instrument and an instrument has to be matched to a clerk; a scar with no instrument behind it is somebody's private business and a scar with a schedule behind it is a line in a case.** The form's four rules, as written on the back of Rusk's second complaint sheet: *examine the body before the instrument; a scar is dated, not counted; where there is no instrument the scar is not recorded and the person is not asked to say; a person may stop at any point and the stopping is recorded as a stopping; an independent witness not paid out of the district account and not living in the yard signs the sheet, and two sheets are never kept by one person.* It was left on a trestle table at the Gate yard, and it is the seed of the individual-consent work in Chapter 35 and the school-roll argument in Chapter 37. **Forty-one people were asked what to call the third pile and gave forty-one answers; the most common was *the sixth to the fourteenth*; there is no agreed heading and a blank space is left at the top of the column.** Marek's own scar is in the third pile, in his own hand, dated the fourteenth, and **nobody remarks on it**.

### School roll (Chapter 18) — locked

Four children are off the ward school's roll: **Wren Ostrow, nine** (Nadja Ostrow's), **Tibb Vray, eight**, **Salle Prent, seven**, **Iven Marsh, ten**. Two are off for two-year arrears; two are off because the seat went. Attendance is suspended from the twenty-fourth, and the suspension is won back for **one week, not for good**, by the standing difference in the Chapter 13 record. Rusk nails two sheets to the Threshold's public complaint board on the nineteenth: the district account and its over-draw, and four names and four dates with the sentence *No child at this school has been asked to do anything. Four places have been removed from an account.* **Pella Rusk's household is one of the eleven on the roll. Her name is in an unsigned surety cell on page three. She asked Marek directly, in the schoolroom, whether page three had her name on it, and he gave her a technically accurate answer about what page three is, which is neither a yes nor a no. That is the chapter's last beat and it belongs to him, not to her.** It is not resolved here and must not be resolved in Chapter 37 by surprise — Chapter 37 argues the school-roll number in public.

### Neris Kade and Marek (Chapter 19) — locked

Their first afternoon of shared work, done badly for three hours and then well, and they still do not like each other. The argument neither of them wins is about what the third pile is called: she will not write *the clerk's* and Marek will not write *the ward's* or *unattributed*, and when he turns her own founding rule against her — a scar with no instrument behind it is somebody's private business — she uses it better than he did and makes him see that the description he is reaching for is the same shape as the invented reserve. Forty-one people were asked; nothing was published; a method was left in the ward. She says she has not forgiven him for **Tuesday** — the fifteenth, the Gate yard, where she read his forearm in one sentence in front of four hundred people and did not stop — and is not going to, and he writes it down. **No warmth, no physical affection, no instant trust, and no panel and no Ledger Sight in the chapter.**

**What Marek loses in Chapter 19** (added at the review; the card requires it and the prose now carries it): **the belief that the record is the territory.** He arrives with sixty-one names in four columns and a clerk's four hours, and the column structure produces a document about a ward rather than an account of anybody — no line of it can be read out in a room to a person who is not a clerk. He states it himself, sitting in the wet with the sheet: a record is the only place the territory is allowed to stand still long enough to be looked at, and it is not the thing itself. This is the Chapter 15 lesson arriving as a body rather than a page, and it is why the batch ends with a method in a yard and three private documents nobody can produce, and why Chapter 21's offer of a clean note is dangerous to him in a way an accusation would not be.

**The numbers in Chapter 19, so nobody tries to close them later:** the list is **sixty-one at the second bell and a hundred and thirty at the fourth** and neither of them totals it in the open, because a total said in a yard is a number somebody repeats in a room with the yard left off it. Of what he wrote out: **forty-one in the vigor pile** and **thirty-seven in the hold's pile**, the rest the Silt's years, and the Silt's pile is deliberately left unnumbered. Of the forty-one people who came past the table with a sentence about a column heading: **eleven said *the sixth to the fourteenth*, nine said *the day the clerk took it up*, four said something much worse in her own words, and seventeen said one of the two that had already been said twice as often** — which is where the four different headings come from, and the heading at the top of the column is left blank.

### Ledger Sight in this batch: zero uses

**Marek did not use the Sight once in Chapters 11–20, and the batch carries no panel of any kind.** In Chapter 16 the file lies open on Hesper's desk and Marek *declines* to touch it, budgeting the fee — five uses in five days, a parcel in his coat he ate and cannot remember, a quarter hour of the twelfth night, and an hour on the night of the fourteenth with a man's voice in it — and reads the column with his own eyes instead, and finds it entirely correct. This is the horror of the batch stated as an administrative fact: he is a clerk with a good eye for mismatched ink, working out what he can afford to spend on the thing that lets him stop using it. **No second hold is available, possible, or suggested anywhere in Chapters 11–20.**

### Costs Marek's body paid in this batch

- A four-inch cut on the inside of the forearm, opened, drained and closed, from holding a page down through six bells of hold.
- A wet pain in the inside of the right elbow on the sixteenth at the fourth bell and again on the twenty-first while copying the cause line, lasting most of an hour, after two hours and ten minutes of longhand.
- The **year scar** described above, which is the hold's toll and is not a wound and will not be dramatic, exactly as Sera Holt promised.
- An hour of sleep he did not get at four in the morning on the twenty-second, which is ordinary and not a Sight cost.

### Bel Harrow and other guardrails

**Bel Harrow does not appear and is not foreshadowed as arriving.** No lender, market, broker, Choir, military body or regional political crisis enters this batch. Renata Vole is a Trust administrator. Hask and Rundle is a municipal maintenance contractor and the Crown Terrace irrigation account 4-Crown is already canon. Nothing here answers the missing year, names the Last Convocation, or explains **A-0**. The clerk of record is not advanced: no one appoints Marek, and he does not call himself that in this batch.

### Small page facts that look like slips and are not (added at the review)

- **Line seven of the levy roll is the Vale household, and it is carried in the name of Marek's grandmother**, who has been dead eight years and whom nobody has ever struck out. Tomas says so himself in Chapter 17 as the reason he has been a line in a book his whole life. That is **one household, not two**, and a later writer must not read it as a second household at line seven, nor as the roll being wrong.
- **Kelb's post.** He had three weeks in the post on the ninth and is in his fourth week on the fifteenth. Both chapters that mention it now say so in the same terms.
- **Bohum Ostrek's money.** He signed the draw at the counter-desk on the sixteenth, the **pay chest takes the first four days of a draw up front**, and both his hands locked on the eighteenth in the middle of a lift — two days after signing, not a fortnight. His own figure for the bill is **two weeks of the hand off the top**. The clause is lawful and stays lawful; the horror is the timing, not the terms.
- **The old mark on Marek's forearm is one he has been carrying since he was seven years old.** In Chapter 20 he dates it that way himself, having asked his mother about it twice and been refused in a way that was a refusal. This is a **fact on the page and not an explanation.** Chapter 34 owns the explanation, and it must not be given earlier: nobody in the book has yet said why the four notches are the same four notches.
- **The ward's arithmetic at the close of Chapter 20 is four hands and one number that is not a hand's.** The file's 640, the four yard books' 440, the Fund board's 279 hundreds and 40 pints of excess, the examiner's 441 — and separately the 160, which only the Trust is allowed to know about. Do not call it five accounts and do not call the 279 hundreds a delivery.

### Proposed canon change, recorded at the batch 0002 review — NOT applied to `outline/volume-01.md`

`outline/volume-01.md` carries **the filing of Hesper's aimed certification in two places that cannot both be true**: the Movement III chapter map at **Chapter 30** ("the aimed certification is filed; the chain has a direction; Marek has days") and the Movement IV chapter map at **Chapter 39** ("Hesper files the aimed certification naming Kelb as a careless operator; a junior clerk's name is destroyed in public"). The escalation-sequence summary at the head of Movement III repeats the Chapter 30 version.

**Resolution, recorded here rather than made silently:** the filing belongs to **Chapter 39**. Three things require it. The batch 0002 card locks it there and forbids it being recorded, foreshadowed as done, or withdrawn in that batch. The volume climax in Chapters 43–45 needs the document to arrive unread and be exposed aloud in a room with the ward's families in it, which is impossible if it was filed nine chapters earlier and read in the interim. And the aimed document's whole design is that it is *lawful, quiet and already done* — a form in a drawer for four days before a room is ready for it — which is what Chapter 39 spends.

**What the writer has done about it:** `workspace/volume-01/batch-0003/PROMPT.md` is corrected. Chapter 30 now ends with the Trust's finding served on the ward, **the chain getting a direction and a clock with a number of days on it**, and the machine lining itself up to write the cause line — the box is still blank. The prompt carries a filing rule that overrides Movement III for the batch, and a writer of Chapters 21–30 must not file it, withdraw it, report it to a third party, or resolve it. `outline/volume-01.md` is not the writer's file to edit; the outline owner should amend the Movement III line at Chapter 30 to read *the chain has a direction; Marek has days* and leave the filing where Movement IV already puts it.

## Volume 01 facts established by batch 0003 (Chapters 21–30)

Prose is authoritative. Movement III runs from the twenty-second to the thirtieth of the rains and is the volume's midpoint: the pressure stops being *who lied* and becomes *who pays when everyone needed something hidden*.

### The calendar of the batch (extends the fixed chain; count from the ninth)

The ninth of the rains is a **Wednesday** and that remains the only anchor. This batch uses: **twenty-second Tuesday, twenty-third Wednesday, twenty-fourth Thursday, twenty-fifth Friday, twenty-sixth Saturday, twenty-seventh Sunday, twenty-eighth Monday, twenty-ninth Tuesday, thirtieth Wednesday.** Every weekday claim in Chapters 21–30 follows from it, and the ones that are stated are the twenty-fifth (Friday, the water office), the twenty-sixth (Saturday, Hesper's room), the twenty-eighth (Monday, Ilya's hearing) and the twenty-ninth (Tuesday, the sitting and the yard). The ward school closes its books on the twenty-third and its attendance is suspended from the twenty-fourth. The rain stopped on the **twenty-second**; the river came up again without rain on the **twenty-seventh**.

**Three forward dates the batch fixes, and the fourth of next month matters:** the **re-listen day at the Gate** is the fourth of next month; the **recount sitting is the twelfth of next month**, with notice on the board on the first and twelve days' notice; the **Hesper W-4 offer's first dry day was the twenty-fourth** and has neither been accepted nor refused. There is no longer an eighth day between the thirtieth and the fourth — do not re-derive them.

### Bells (corrected in this batch — the only bell errors in Chapters 21–30 were fixed)

**Six bells a day and six a night, counted separately and never mixed.** The day count runs from the first at the opening of the desks to the sixth when the copy room closes and the seventh in the evening; the **eighth day bell is struck in the morning**, which is why every hearing in this volume is "at the eighth bell" in the morning. The night watch runs six bells from the last of the light to the light again. **There is no tenth bell and no eleventh bell, and a night bell is never called by a number without the word *night*.** Chapter 22's descent to the removed records now reads *at the sixth of the night* and *before the light*; Chapter 26's high water comes in *about the fifth of the night*. **Chapter 6 carries a pre-existing eleventh-bell error that is not in this batch's ownership — recorded here so a later batch does not propagate it.**

### The locked instruments, restated after this batch — do not blur

Nothing in Chapters 21–30 moved any of them, and the batch is careful about the difference, so the next batch must be too.

- **The ward settlement entry** is the false-witnessed instrument: Hesper signed it on the ninth, **Kelb countersigned it in pencil the same afternoon**. The invented standing-reserve recital and the omitted page three are on it. The collection lands here and only here.
- **The clerk's certificate of posting** is the separate one-page paper Marek signed on the eleventh with *Yes* on the party query. Chapter 21 makes it the *subject* of Vole's offer — she offers to strike **that page** and nothing else, and she says so in terms: *My certification does not depend on your certificate of posting. It never did.* A settlement whose certificate of posting is void is not due, not overdue, and not payable. **Marek's name is still never where the collection lands.** He surrenders this paper in Ch 44; the Chamber holds it over him in Ch 32.
- **The chain follows the instrument, not the folder.** It is referred to once, in half a sentence, by levy clerk Ostrowsky in Ch 30, quoting the hearing officer of Ch 13. Do not re-explain it at length.
- **The recount of the twenty-first** is a separate desk's form with the cause line *the totals of this entry were added at the counter-desk from a defective schedule.* **Drafted, not filed, not withdrawn, not reported, not exposed. It is filed in Chapter 39.** See the frozen-thread entry below.
- **Page three** is the labour-pledge schedule: eleven lines, four columns, eleven sureties named, **all eleven witness cells blank**, never given to the ward, whose copy runs one, two, four, five. Both copies are on the permanent Ch 13 record with the difference **noted and not adjudicated**. **Ch 21 and Ch 30 both list the four private documents in Marek's book and keep them distinct and unusable; the batch adds no fifth rendering.**
- **The levy roll** is unchanged: eleven households, the posting names the first four, the **Vale household is seventh**. **Ch 30 now reads the four payable aloud off the finding and they are Ch 3's four yards in Ch 3's order** — *the fuel basin, of the ward's north side. Ropewalk, of the low kitchens. The Tanner's yard. The Gate yard* — **with only two of the four carrying a household name in the margin, the fuel-yard foreman's and *Ostrow, Nadja, cook, of the low kitchens*.** The outline says the rest are "counted, not named" and the prose now obeys it: **no Prent and no Marsh household is named anywhere in the volume.** Tomas's vigor loan is untouched and is not mentioned once in Chapters 21–30.

### Locked numbers after Chapter 30 — and the ones that were corrected

- **The four hands still do not close, and must not be made to close.** The file's **640**; the four yard books' **440**; the Fund board's **279 hundreds and 40 pints** of excess; **Neris Kade's 441**, which she puts into the hearing record in Ch 27 and the officer enters beside the corrected figure. The Trust certifies **440**. The ward's roll figure differs from the corrected excess by **sixty pints in nine days** (280 hundreds = 28,000 pints; 279h 40p = 27,940). **This was twenty pints in the first draft and was corrected in the repair pass: 280h minus 279h40p is sixty pints, and the figure is inside a sealed finding, so it cannot be wrong.** One hundred and sixty is still the only figure the Trust acknowledges.
- **The district account, now stated in full for the first time** (Ch 29, and carried into the Ch 30 finding). Income for the season **twelve thousand and something**. Obligations as presented **forty thousand eight hundred and something**, which is **two hundred and forty per cent over** — the locked figure, glossed in the office's idiom as *a shade over three times the lawful draw*. A **reserve carry of forty thousand nine hundred**, entered every year since the flood years, unapplied, unclaimable, held against a standing reserve nobody has ever located. True obligations before the carry **eighty-one thousand seven hundred and fourteen**, which is **five hundred and eighty per cent over**, glossed *a shade under seven times what the district may lawfully draw*. The presented figure is **exactly half** the true one, which is Grale's reason for calling it half as bad as it is. **The carry was twenty-eight thousand eight hundred in the first draft and was changed in the repair pass, because it sat within three per cent of the ward's own bill in pints and read as a bug in a book made of numbers.** Do not reintroduce either old figure.
- **The over-read's cause is now settled and must not wobble.** About **two hundred hundreds of pints a night** has gone through an **unmetered take-off above the second-junction ward meter** into the Crown Terrace north gardens for **eleven years**, and **more of it since the second of the rains**. The step appears in the reading book on the second. The take-off is on **Crown Terrace irrigation account 4-Crown**, which carries **nine gardeners with wages against it**, and the ward has been the only body in the basin on which the district account's closing figure could be made to balance. The junction screen **was** genuinely fouled and **was** genuinely cleared on the thirteenth at the district account's expense; that maintenance failure is real, is not the cause of the excess, and Hask and Rundle's fence is down.

### The recitation, the carry, and the collection — the three-part rule fixed in Ch 29–30

This is the institutional spine of the batch and it was made explicit in the repair pass, because a reader could not otherwise tell which charge the recited reserve cancels.

1. **The recited standing reserve is offset in full against the water office's own reserve-carry line.** It is an account of the water office. It is honoured as certified on the face of the finding, and it has been honoured, unapplied, every year since the flood years.
2. **It does not touch the households.** What a yard pays at the ordinary wage is drawn from the households and is not in the district account and does not come through it. Grale says it in the office's own idiom: *if anybody in a room ever tells a ward that those are the same figure, that is a man being kind.* **This is a seed for Ch 46's conversion of the bill to a public levy.**
3. **The collection from the eleven households is a separate instrument** and proceeds in the ordinary course — **and may not be entered before the cause is entered in the box at the head of the form.** That rider is **in the served text of the finding** (it was moved there in the repair pass, from a private line in the levy office, so that the ward's belief that the collection is proceeding is a documented sentence that later proves false). It was written by the Deputy herself at the fourth bell of the thirtieth, on a draft that levy clerk Ostrowsky typed on the twenty-eighth.

### A ward is not a person — the line that carries Ch 21, 26, 28 and 35

Stated once, at the door of the Threshold on the twenty-second, by a hearing officer applying standing orders with no cruelty in it: a ward **cannot petition, cannot appeal, cannot be sworn, and cannot be cross-examined**, because a ward cannot speak; every form in the hall has a box for a person in it and **there is no box for Tallow Ward and there has never been one.** Only two people can speak for the ward: the clerk of the day, who is a party and will be cross-examined alone, or Kern in his own name. The ward's petition of three hundred and eleven signatures was **entered as a document received and not acted upon** — the *not acted upon* is the Deputy Steward's phrasing, not the officer's, and **the words a clerk does not write on a document are the ones read out in a room six months later.** **Do not restate the whole rule again. It has been said.**

### What the batch did to each person

- **Marek.** Acquired a second decision and no more certainty than the first. He is **shown the temptation, does the arithmetic on it, and does not sign** — and the reader watches him not sign: on the twenty-fourth he puts both papers on the *answered* side of Vole's own tray, writes the date and the words *not signed*, and does not look at the tray again on his way out. The reason is not conscience (conscience would have cost the four households and the certificate) but that **the statement is twenty-nine words long and every one of them is true, and a man who signs a true sentence because a superior has told him it costs nobody anything will be told by the next man who has to read it that he did.** He also **uses the finding for the first time**: the no-forum conclusion he has been frightened of since the sixteenth becomes a thing he spends. He is **not appointed clerk of record** and does not call himself that. He still cannot warn the ward, and the chapter states why three separate times: a warning from a clerk to four hundred people is a sentence at a door, and in nine days somebody in a room with a door in it would ask where it came from.
- **Hesper Rann** (46, locked — the first draft of Ch 25 had him at fifty-two, corrected): **human throughout, and monstrous at 25 without a mask dropping.** He names the four dead and does not apologise, and *would have written it in a better hand* is the closest he comes. He refuses to be asked for a second false statement and says plainly what the price of asking would be. He has known about the Gate plate since the ninth. He notices the coat and not the mark, and tells Marek why he has never asked. **He does not explain the cause line, name it, or forgive Marek for it anywhere in Chapters 21–30** — in the repair pass the Ch 30 stair scene was cut back to his praise, his presence in the room, and *bring your book shut*, because the first draft had him describe what a cause is and forgive Marek in the same breath, which spent Ch 38's cruelty and softened Ch 39.
- **Ilya Senn** (41): a rule-breaker the ward is fed by and cannot thank. He refuses the tidying sentence in a room and in front of the party, refuses the ward's gratitude as carefully as he refuses the Trust's terms, and puts his own wages on the table as an **offer made and not accepted**. He names the worse of his two breaches himself: a man who holds a valve open against an order is disobeying, a man who holds one with no order at all is deciding. He is **not** a hero and **not** a martyr, and the line that makes the ward uncomfortable about him is his own: *a Warden's competence taught me a long time ago that I should make the terrible decision before anybody else can get to it, and I have never once been wrong about the water, and I have been wrong about everything else on that platform.*
- **Aldis Kern** (44): refuses Marek a document in order to protect a person, and the refusal is correct. He names the Underbank before the sluice, permanently, on a sheet that cannot go into any room, and adds the condemned-building slate because a yard-man made him. He gives evidence **against** Ilya in order to protect the ward's water, and says he will not enjoy it. He keeps the ward's roll in the yard, in forty hands, and **refuses to let Kade or Marek hold it** — *the day this yard has a copy of a thing that is kept in forty hands is the day the thing belongs to somebody.*
- **Neris Kade:** hostile, competent, not warm, and her Volume 1 arc begins. She has a ward sheet of her own, stops keeping it in her own hand, and **gets ninety-one examined bodies into a Trust file as a document received and not acted upon** — the first time a body has gone into a Trust file before the document did. She names the reading-failure/maintenance-failure distinction before Vey does. She puts 441 into the record. She refuses to keep the ward's roll. She tells Marek to decide on Thursday because he can live in a city with the decision, and she is the only person who can say aloud the sentence that is available in place of *I have a paper I cannot show you.* **No warmth, no physical affection, no first-name familiarity** (the first draft had her say "Marek" once, corrected to "Vale").
- **Marda Senn.** **Her name comes off the warden's roll in Chapter 22 and does not come back in this volume.** She is the person most nearly right about the water and the building cannot hold her name: a roll with one column for a name and one for an hour, no cause, no place, and **no form anywhere in the city on which a person may be entered as not returned.** Ilya asks four questions in a row and the answer to all four is that there is no form to appeal to. **Nobody goes into the Hollow Galleries in this batch. Marek stands outside the door for two hours and eleven minutes and is refused, and says why in terms that are honest and not the whole reason. Nobody should explain the Galleries.**

### New Volume 01 names introduced in batch 0003 (all still live)

- **Sander Vey**, 34, clerk in the Crown Terrace water office, eleven years in it, keeps the **reading record** — a wall book, no copy, certified weekly under a line with his name on it. He has never had a production order for it. He gives the batch's central institutional distinction without understanding that it mattered: *a reading is not a document, a document is something a person signs; a reading is a figure somebody copies off a dial at a certain hour.*
- **Grale**, 51, district accountant, the account desk, bad chest, nineteen years as levy clerk's opposite number and **eleven years taking ward counts down on a tray**. He took Marda Senn's counts on the twenty-third and went into the removed records and came back up. He is the volume's window on the district account and the cut order, he is not a villain, he has cut that order four times in nineteen years, and **he takes the frame off the wall and turns it round when it is about to be rewritten.** The line *it gets cheaper as it goes down* is his, and so is *the day I prove it I have to stop doing it.*
- **Ostrowsky**, about sixty, **levy clerk of the hall, nineteen years**, second floor. He types the findings, he is the clerk present when the Deputy writes the rider, he is sorry about putting Marek's name down as clerk of the day for the recount, and he is the one who quotes *the chain follows the instrument, not the folder* in half a sentence. **He is the volume's picture of a man who can tell exactly what a document does and has no instrument for what it means.**
- **Aldis Kern's yard, named:** **Milan Vray**, 39, rope-maker, whose condemned storehouse held about four hundred of his own stock and who carried pails for twelve days; **Nadja Ostrow**, 38, cook of the low kitchens, one of the eleven on the roll, and **the only household name in the margin of the finding's fourth clause**; **Vasil Ferun**, 13, the broken shin, who does not want it named; **Warden Dettmer**, about sixty, who has struck three names in nine years.
- **Three of the four children's mothers** come up the causeway to the copy-room door in Ch 29 with Rusk. One says a name and Rusk says *not today*.

### New Volume 01 local terms (setting detail, not new world rules)

- **the Hollow Galleries** — the removed records, three floors below the account desk, no lift, cold, shelves a long way back, trays of flat boards with a waxed cord and no labels, a keeper of about seventy with a key on a string. **They keep records removed from official histories. They are not a dungeon, they are not explained, and nobody in Morrowfen goes in during this batch.**
- **a carry** — a district-account line of charges the office has entered against itself and cannot apply, carried forward year on year because nobody can name a district to apply it against. A question asked three hundred times and not answered. **The reserve carry is the batch's discovery and it is three hundred years old.**
- **the cut order** — a written order in a frame, nailed to the wall of the levy office, that holds the district account to its presented figure. **It has no copy**, which is why it is the only instruction in the city nobody can be given. It gets cheaper as it goes down; the north gardens' crew is ninth, the ward clinic's coal and the ward's night lamp are second from the bottom, and the school is last.
- **a reading record / a maintenance record** — two books, two offices, eleven years without ever being on the same desk.
- **the removed records / the counting room** — the water office's room for the wall books.

### Ledger Sight in this batch: zero uses

**The batch carries no panel of any kind, which was the target.** Nothing above Boundary Hold, no second standing hold used, offered, wished for, or hinted to be available. **Marek does not use the Sight once in Chapters 21–30 and does not decline one on stage either** — the budget he started in Ch 16 simply governs, and the chapters are made of documents, people and arithmetic instead. The costs he pays in this batch are ordinary and boring and that is the point: **a wet elbow on the twenty-first** (carried over), **a scar that is not a wound and will not be dramatic**, and **four days of not sleeping.**

### Ch 37 is protected, and this is the note the next batch needs

**Chapter 37 argues the school-roll number in public, argued by people who are not clerks, and it is not paid off as a surprise anywhere.** Chapter 29 gives Rusk the page her school comes off, the fact that the school is the last line of an order with no copy, and a mother's voice asking her to say a name — **and she explicitly declines to say the number**, in terms: *the number they are an argument about is not in this ward and not in this hall, it is on that page, and that page has no copy, and I am not a person who can be given one. So somebody is going to have to write that number in a room one day, and it is not going to be me.* **The first draft of Ch 29 spent Ch 37's argument — the four names, the eleven hours a week, the escalation to eleven children — and it was cut in the repair pass.** Do not restore it. Chapter 30 adds one plant and no more: **Nadja Ostrow is in the margin of the finding's fourth clause and Wren Ostrow is on a sheet in a schoolroom on Sill Row, and it is the same household at both ends of it and neither of them has ever been told.** Pella Rusk's own surety cell is untouched, and her unanswered question to Marek from Ch 18 is still unanswered.

### Deviations from the batch prompt and the outline, recorded rather than made silently

- **The aimed certification is NOT filed in Chapter 30, and the outline now says so.** The conflict inside `outline/volume-01.md` (Movement III Ch 30 versus Movement IV Ch 39) was resolved in favour of **Chapter 39** and **the outline has been amended at the second repair pass**: the Movement III summary and the Chapter 30 map line now read *the aimed certification is drawn and not filed / left in a drawer, to be filed in Movement IV*. **Chapter 30 ends with the machine lining itself up to write the cause line and the box still empty.** Nothing is owed on this point.
- **Chapters 21, 22, 24, 25, 28 and 30 are longer than the prompt's 2,400–3,000 ordinary band** (3,452 · 3,645 · 3,271 · 3,340 · 3,527 · 3,281) and 23 runs long as the prompt permitted. See the length note in `state/current.md`. **No chapter sits under the 2,400 floor**, which was batch 0002's failure mode, and the batch's shape is six long chapters and four ordinary ones rather than four long and six short.
- **Neris Kade's 441 was nearly lost** when the corrected delivery of 440 became certified. It is restored in Ch 27, entered in the hearing record beside the corrected figure, because **the volume's argument is that the figures do not close and Ch 30 must not let them close.**
- **Ch 28's yard scene was moved.** As first drafted it ran at the second to the sixth bells of the twenty-ninth and therefore preceded, rather than followed, the eighth-bell sitting it reports. It now runs at the last of the light on the twenty-ninth, so that **the ward's decision is caused by Ilya's refusal** and Ch 30's finding the following morning lands after it. Check any later reference to "the yard roll going on the board" for the new hour: **before the lamps were lit on the twenty-ninth.**

### Second review-repair pass (against `logs/batch-0003.review.log`) — canon touched, recorded

**No plot beat moved and no new canon invented. What changed is the wording, the arithmetic in one speech, and what the reader is allowed to have seen by the end of Chapter 30.** The following are now the settled readings and must not drift back.

- **Marek's book holds exactly four things, and the inventory is now stated once, in Ch 30, and referred to as "four" everywhere else.** (1) A sheet with a footnote copied out of a page no clerk may copy, carrying *copied from the original* in his own hand, worth nothing at all. (2) Three renderings of page three, the labour-pledge schedule. (3) **The certificate of posting** — the only one of the four that any office in the city would look at. (4) A sentence of nine words in the levy office's own formula for a cause, under a wrapper. **"The third copy" (Ch 21) and "a fifth thing" (Ch 30) are gone; they were wrong.** The private half of the book was made on his own stair at four in the morning (Ch 22). The certificate was in his coat on the twenty-second — the decision text in Ch 21 already said so, and the inventory now agrees with it.
- **The nine words are NOT printed in Chapter 30.** The sentence is established on the page in **Chapter 20**, in Hesper's own square hand, in a box at the top of a form that had no field for causes at all except that box, and it is in Chapter 20 three times because Chapter 20 *is* the chapter about writing it. **Chapter 30 now says only that a sentence of nine words exists under a wrapper and has never been shown to a living soul.** The reader who has read Ch 20 knows the words; the reader is not handed them a second time, and the Ch 30 text no longer ties a cause to a false-witnessed instrument. **Ch 39's job is therefore the exposure in public, not the sentence.**
- **The mechanism sentence is cut from Ch 30 and is now Ch 13's alone.** Ch 13 (the Trust's hearing officer) owns *the chain follows the instrument, not the folder* and the fact that a false-witnessed entry sends the collection to the counter-desk clerk. **Nothing in Chapters 21–30 connects the cause box to that rule.** Ostrowsky's replacement is colder and gives away nothing: on thirty-one recounts in nineteen years he always knew before the sitting **whose paper** he was filling the box against, and on this one he does not, and Marek was in the room on the fifteenth when nobody told the room then either.
- **The served fourth clause's margin line reads:** *Two of the four carry a household name in the margin: the fuel basin's, which is the fuel-yard foreman's, and Ostrow, Nadja, cook, of the low kitchens.* **One of the two is a household identified by its occupation, the other is a woman.** That is what makes Rezek's speech work — his own name is not in the margin, and the fuel basin's is. Do not let a later chapter put a foreman's personal name there.
- **Ch 22's refusal is grounded in the twenty-second, not in the petition book.** Marda will not let Marek on her stair because **he is the clerk the hearing officer offered the ward as its own voice at the door, in front of three hundred and eleven names.** The petition is Kern's, on the back of a fuel-yard tally, and Marek never writes on it. **"Twenty-nine" is Vole's statement word count and is not a signature count anywhere.**
- **Kern's yard percentage is a hundred and fifty per cent**, his own sixteen years of over-signing, and touches none of the locked figures. **It is not 240 and not 580, and it is not a hundred and forty** — a hundred and forty is the number of unnamed carriers and Vray's own money in stock.
- **The water office has one counter for the district and a hatch at the back for the public.** The hatch has been shut since the flood and there is a bench of people who came for it anyway. **There is no second public counter, and the queue at the Threshold in Ch 24 is for the Threshold.**
- **Neris Kade's 441 speech in Ch 27 is now her own.** She counted the Ropewalk standpipe by the hour for a fortnight, the yards counted by the day, and the yard men will call the hundred her arithmetic and she will not argue because they will not be wrong. **The figure and the disagreement are unchanged; the wording no longer borrows Ch 20's.**
- **The two templates the reviewer flagged are thinned, not purged.** "The whole of" is at fourteen in the batch, down from twenty-seven. Kern's *that is not a complaint about the Trust, that is a fact about mouths* is cut. **Kade's *You cannot have the valve and the innocence* is kept** — it is the batch's best line and the finding was that three speakers shared a shape, not that this one was wrong. **Neris Kade's 441 in Ch 20 and her 441 in Ch 27 are the same woman counting the same way; keep the difference between the narration and the speech.**
- **The one structural finding is recorded, not fixed: eight of ten chapters run a document being read aloud into a room that reacts.** Chapters 23, 26 and 28 were all yard-meeting, trestle-table, sheet-read. **Rebuilding eight finished scenes is not a repair and was not attempted.** It is written into `workspace/volume-01/batch-0004/PROMPT.md` as the next batch's hardest craft problem, with the chapters that have other engines named. The one instance that was a true defect — Ch 26 restating Ch 23's ordering rule verbatim in substance — is fixed.

## Volume 01 facts established by batch 0004 (Chapters 31–40)

Prose is authoritative. Movement IV runs from the first to the fourteenth of next month and turns the volume from arithmetic into a question about a room: **whether four hundred people can be a party to a proceeding.** The batch's own climax is Chapter 39, the recount sitting of the twelfth.

### The calendar of the batch (extends the fixed chain; the ninth of the rains is a Wednesday)
