**First Thursday, second Friday, third Saturday, fourth Sunday, fifth Monday, sixth Tuesday, seventh Wednesday, eighth Thursday, ninth Friday, tenth Saturday, eleventh Sunday, twelfth Monday, thirteenth Tuesday, fourteenth Wednesday, sixteenth Thursday.** Weekdays stated in the prose: the second is a Friday (Ch 32, the Fund's secretary's line), the ninth is a Friday (Ch 36), the tenth is a Saturday (Ch 37), the twelfth is a Monday (Ch 39). The **re-listen day at the Gate is the fourth of next month** and Marek is not asked to it. The **ward school reopened on the first for one week and shut again on the sixth** — the week bought by the Ch 13 record — and is shut on the tenth, the thirteenth and the fourteenth.

**Four forward dates the batch fixes.** The **first sitting of the emergency appeals board is the ninth**, at the eighth bell, notice given on the second (seven days). The **recount sitting is the twelfth**, at the eighth bell, as fixed in Ch 30. **Marek's suspension hearing is the seventeenth**, at the eighth bell. **The board's hearing is the sixteenth**, at the eighth bell, with **four days' notice** given on the thirteenth.

### The suspension — the instrument, and what is not in it (Ch 32, LOCKED)

The Chamber posts a slip on the **clerk's column** of the Threshold posting board, on Trust paper, under the Chamber's seal, read out twice by the posting desk's woman. Its terms: **suspended from countersignature, from the certification of copies, and from the assignment of files, from the second until a hearing at the eighth bell on the seventeenth, at which the clerk may be heard.** Grounds: **the certificate of posting of the eleventh of the rains, on which the clerk of the day answered the party query in his own hand.** And at the foot: ***No other matter is suspended.***

- The ground is a **true, twenty-word query and one word of answer**. The certificate's own query reads *clerk of the day is entered as a party to the matter. Does clerk certify? Y/N*, and Marek wrote *Yes*. He certified a matter he was a party to. It is not fraud and it is not the lie.
- **The six bells are not in it** and cannot be: there is no office in the city that could suspend a delay held without authority against an instrument that does not exist, so there is nothing to suspend. **The year's scar is not a matter either.** A yearline mark is not a document, has no form and no entry, and is not capable of suspension by anybody.
- **Marek's name is still never where the collection lands.** The paper that suspends him is not the paper that hurts Kelb, and the batch says so in two characters' mouths and it is still the volume's sharpest wound.
- Practical effects, all on the page: the letter ***V.*** is struck from the Tallow line of the assignment column in a hand that is **not Hesper's**; a clerk of the day assigned no ward is not clerk of the day; he may not countersign, certify a copy, or be given a file; **and he is therefore not the clerk of the day for the recount on the twelfth.** The name that replaces his goes in the parties' notice on the second. **Neither Marek nor the reader is told who it is, and Ostrowsky refuses to say in a corridor.** It is the clerk who witnessed the cause being drafted in Ch 20.
- **Who could speak for him, and what each of them did.** **Sedge** (intake floor) will not countersign anything with him on it — the standing order, the second half of which he asked a man about in the winter, and which is *the practice* until somebody with a seal says otherwise. **Ostrowsky** enters the strike and tells him he is not clerk of the day, in a corridor, and says he has never once said the useful half of a thing first. **Zorka Vendl** refuses to write a suspension rule into the counter-clerks' mutual made by eleven juniors in four days, and says aloud the Fund's real principle: in nineteen years a suspended clerk has never been turned away and it is not kindness, it is that everybody is waiting to see how bad it gets and would rather somebody else went first. **Emil Skar**, the mutual's secretary, writes Marek's name off the sheet on the stairs outside the Fund's room in four seconds, after telling him the thing he is spending his nerve on. **Neris Kade stops asking him questions in a corridor** — not a sulk: an answer from a suspended clerk is an assertion with no instrument behind it, and asking a man who cannot be asked twice is a pattern. She states it as a professional judgement and she is right, and it is the worst thing that happens to him in the batch.
- **Hesper's W-4 is still unaccepted, unrefused and undestroyed**, in a drawer in his room, nineteen days old in Ch 38, first dry day the twenty-fourth. **Hesper warns him against a second thing**: that somebody he trusts will offer him a true statement at a cost that is not money. (This is the Ch 21/Vole offer, and Marek has already refused it; Hesper is speaking generally and the batch does not say so.)
- **The one thing a suspension accidentally makes possible:** a suspended clerk is not paid out of the district account and does not live in the yard, which makes him the only independent witness in Morrowfen available to a boy in Tallow Ward. He signs Kelb's sheets once and is told he will not be a witness again.

### Bel Harrow and the emergency appeals board (Ch 31, 36, 40 — LOCKED)

- **Bel Harrow, about forty, a Crown March representative.** He represents a procedure, not a constituency, and there is nobody in Crown March he answers to but a clerk with a seal. **He can call a meeting and he cannot pay anybody out of it.** Nobody in Morrowfen has met one before. He travels with a handcart and a leather case and no retinue, asks permission before taking anybody's name, and says his whole office out loud in a yard: *we are not clever, we are the calendar.*
- He arrives with **a petition for a public board**, a two-page Crown March instrument: a schedule of **five grounds** and a petition. The five grounds, as stated in a yard: a settlement certified during a declared emergency; a party not served with a material term; a document in the case with no copy; an account bearing figures that do not agree with one another; a question of conduct excluded from a proceeding in which it ought to have been heard. **Four are about figures and one is about a person, and the procedure does not care which is worse.** He is **not permitted to tell the ward which term was not served**, will be asked about it in a room, and will not be able to answer. **He is not foreshadowed anywhere in Chapters 21–30.**
- **The ward's six-line terms sheet**, written in about eleven hands in the Gate yard on the first: that a ward may be in the room and the people in it may sit in it and the attendance is not a matter of grace; that a person may answer or not answer and that not answering is written down as not answering; that anything read into the room may be read aloud to the people it is about first; that the room keeps a record and the record is read to the yard afterwards by a person who is not a clerk; that a member who dissents may have the disagreement written in their own words and kept and the keeping is the board's duty; and that the day is told in advance and the notice is not left at an office. **A petition asks for a decision and the ward does not want a decision — it wants a room — which is how the chapter's argument is made without anybody making a speech.**
- **Vole refuses in procedure with a schedule of reasons, three of them now stated in full**: a certified finding is not appealable and a returnpath requires a living holder of the obligation, which is a schedule with eleven names and no witness that was set aside; the matter was enlarged without asking any party; **and a Crown March representative has standing in a Crown interest, and no Crown interest is pleaded, because the Crown Terrace account in this matter is a district irrigation account — the word Crown is a district and a terrace.** She schedules the sitting anyway and says out loud that she did not want to and could not find a reason to refuse better than the reason to agree.
- **The board's members, locked:** Harrow (presiding, and on the list of members not eligible to be chosen chair), **Renata Vole** for the Trust, **Sedge** for the Chamber, **Aldis Kern** for the ward in his own name, and **Zorka Vendl** for the Benevolent Fund of the Counter-Clerks. **That is the Chair plus four members, and a table of five, and nobody else.** **The Chair does not vote in a money matter**, so a money vote is at most three to one and the Ch 36 recorder's-pay vote **went three to one**. **Kade is NOT a member and cannot vote.** The Chair gives her the second seat and a sheet on the thirteenth because a person who puts the bodies in has to be in the room, and the chapter says out loud that she is not a member and did not pretend otherwise. **Her sheet's one function is to hold the four figures in the order they are called out**, which is the one thing at that table nobody else was doing. **Do not seat a sixth person at this table and do not let a non-member's motion be a vote.**
- **Harrow has no clerk and cannot get one.** No office in the city will serve the record of a body it is a party to, so **he serves the minutes himself, in his own hand, and says so at the table.**
- **The four terms of the ninth, and where each is now owned.** (1) *The chair:* Harrow presides until the board is satisfied it can sit without him, reviewed at each sitting and **written**; Vole's dissent on Crown March standing was withdrawn when he agreed to put the review in. (2) *The recorder's pay:* **the Fund cannot pay a sitting** — its rule is in the second leaf of the book and it pays claims for a clerk's injury, and it has never done otherwise; the Chamber cannot, because a rate on the hall is not a fund; **a ward cannot be assessed a rate at all**, so the Trust's share rose to cover it, and Kern objected and was outvoted **three to one**. **Vendl is the person who declined twice and she now says so in the first person** and asks for it to be minuted that she is still sitting in the room. (3) *Attendance and declining:* put from a bench Kade was not entitled to speak from; Harrow calls it standard March practice; **neither Kade nor Marek ever says where it came from, and it came off the back of a complaint sheet in a yard on the twentieth of the rains.** (4) ***A member who dissents may have the grounds written in their own words, in the finding, and the duty to write them is the board's and not the member's.*** Vole refuses it on the record twice in the same words, and the refusal is in the minutes. **This term is the volume's institutional change and survives to Chapter 46.** **Term 4 also went three to one** (Sedge, Kern, Vendl; Vole's office did not concur and gave no grounds), so **the two Ch 36 votes are both three to one and are not to be conflated: one is a money matter, one is not.**
- **Harrow's own terms, added on the thirteenth and in a different hand: no collection is entered while the board is open**, from service of notice to the making of the finding. He says out loud that it is worth four days and is a queue behind a door and not a stay, and that the collection enters the day after the finding whatever the finding says. **Nobody moves against it. Hesper Rann's not moving against it is the tell, and Marek reads it.**
- **The board cannot strike anything, cannot choose a figure, and cannot serve a ward.** The Trust's motion to strike the cause fails, and Vole offers the honest version: the board may **hear** the cause, in front of the parties, on the Thursday. Sedge's motion to find a common figure fails, and **Kade's motion carries: record all four figures and adjudicate none of them**, which Harrow minutes as the whole of the moral content of a board in its first sitting.

### The aimed certification — FILED IN CHAPTER 39, and it is in the record

**The document is a one-page CERTIFICATION OF CAUSE, and Chapter 39 now says so on the page.** It is the instrument a recount's cause is written on; a certification has to be signed to be a thing and not a conversation, and it is the only one-page instrument in this city that puts a clerk's name at the top of a sentence about his own work. **Drafted on the twenty-first of the rains, filed on the twelfth of next month, exposed in public with the ward's families outside the door.** Nothing in Chapters 31–38 prepares it. **The nine words of the cause are not printed again in the batch.** The reader who read Ch 20 knows the sentence; Chapter 39's work is the room, and **the charge of *careless* reaches the room through Rezek's protest and not through a sentence anybody reads out.**

- **Procedure, all of it lawful and all of it stated in full in the room:** a recount is the fourth clause of the settlement desk's standing practice; any supervisor may require a re-addition of a certified total; it requires a third-grade clerk present; it is entered in the desk's book with the hour, the clerk's name and the reason; **the cause goes on the form before the arithmetic, and Hesper says why out loud** (*because the arithmetic is either going to agree or it is not, and if it agrees then a man who has written a sentence explaining why he did a lawful thing will want to know what he wrote*). The hearing officer states that **a cause must be capable of being acted on**; the clerk confirms the fourth clause of the finding is satisfied; **the collection is unblocked**.
- **The rule, stated once, in the hearing officer's mouth, and referring to the fifteenth:** the collection follows the instrument and not the folder, and an entry carrying a material term the parties were not served with sends the collection to the living clerk whose counter-signature is the institution's act. That clerk is at the counter-desk. **The third-grade clerk reads the foot of the entry and the name is in the room.**
- **The seven in the gallery, all persons in their own names, because a ward cannot be a party to a supervisor's step and cannot be sworn or cross-examined:** Bohdan Rezek of the fuel basin; Nadja Ostrow of the low kitchens; Milan Vray; Pella Rusk; Milena Kelb; a young hand of the Tanner's yard; **Ondra Kelb**, who is named in the room's business and has a hand-book in the bag and a folded schedule he does not produce. **Plus Aldis Kern at the table, plus Marek Vale let in at the door because a suspended clerk is nobody's clerk. Fourteen people are let in in all — the seven, those two, and five at the table (Hesper, a third-grade clerk, Ostrowsky, the hearing officer, Vole).** **It is seven, not nine, and Chapters 41–45 must not say nine.** Marek sits in the last row of the gallery and understands every word; **not one of the seven understands any.** About three hundred and forty people are in the corridor and on the stair.
- **What each of them does.** Rezek refuses the word *careless* and says he could not tell one counter-desk junior from another in twenty-two years. **Kelb says one true thing and sits down: two hundred and seventy-nine hundreds and forty pints, the ward's own figure, added twice with a pencil on the ninth, correct throughout, and the sixty pints he cannot account for** — and the officer confirms both the ward's figure and the finding's 280 are in the record. **Milena Kelb stands up and says her son's name twice and says nobody has looked at the boy.** Hesper is kind to Kelb twice, about the hand-book and about his mother, and means both. Hesper then asks, in front of the table, for the ward's account to be taken from four named households and set against a schedule nobody was ever given, and says he has wanted that since the ninth of the rains and did not put it in a drawer for a month because it is not a matter of a man's private arithmetic.
- **Aftermath, in the room:** the arithmetic is done and the column agrees; the desk's book gains its thirty-second entry in nineteen years; **the suspension of the ninth is not disturbed**; the four households are read out again in the same order with two household names in the margin; **Vole states that nothing in the room disturbs her finding and that this office does not intend to be delayed by a board on the sixteenth.**
- **The recount sitting of the twelfth has been overtaken.** What now runs is the board's hearing on the sixteenth, and the only thing standing between the four households and the collection is a term worth four days.

### The ward's own work, one household at a time (Ch 35 — the institutional turn, from below)

**The asking, the fifth to the eighth: forty-one doors, twenty-nine answers, three no's.** Twenty-six yes, three no, and the twelve who were not there were not the same twelve twice. The rules are copied from the back of the complaint sheet left on a trestle table at the Gate on the twentieth of the rains, and **the yard is not told where they came from and no character in the batch says the words *the Chapter 19 method*.**

- Ask at a door, one household at a time. Ask the household, not the yard. Write the answer in the words it was given in. **A stopping is a stopping.** **Two of you, a keeper's sheet and a reader's sheet, never one keeper and never one reader, and a sheet does not go to a third person's house. Nobody is to be asked why they are refusing, twice.**
- **The record is not two sheets and cannot be.** Each of the **nineteen pairs** carried its own keeper's sheet and reader's sheet. The yard had **two long ruled sheets at the tank** carrying the rules and, at the foot of each, the witness box. **On the fourth day the nineteen pairs came back and read their own answers out in the open at the tank**, one pair after another, and Kern wrote them onto the two long sheets while a woman from the low kitchens called each line back to him. **The twenty-nine answers went onto two lines in the words the households used and the three refusals onto a third with the reasons under them**, and the nineteen working sheets went onto a nail by the tank with their strings on. **Chapters 31–40 and after may not describe the ward's return as two sheets' worth of asking.** The line that is quotable is Kern's reason for the nail: *a record a person can lift off a nail is a record and a record a person has to ask somebody for is a rumour.*
- **The one box the ward cannot fill is the independent witness, and the reason is a power, not a shortage of volunteers.** Vray's premise is now correct: *any one of us would do, and that is the whole reason none of us can* — a witness has to be somebody the ward cannot reach and cannot pay, there is no form on which a yard asks the city for a person, and no office will send a witness to a tank because a yard asked. The yard leaves the box empty on purpose so that the shape of the hole is visible to whoever reads the sheet in six weeks. **Do not let a later chapter say the box was empty because there was no such man in the ward; there were two such people in Morrowfen and one of them was standing in the yard.**
- **Marek is not asked and does not offer.** **Neris Kade refuses a sheet** (the day an examiner keeps a roll is the day a district's roll becomes a return) and stands where the asking is. She says, in front of sixty people, that Marek has not touched the sheets, so that the answer survives in sixty mouths and not in a document.
- **Sera Holt comes once**, to say the rule being borrowed is a rule about a body and a levy is not a pail, and to name what the yard has learned that a hall has not: **a count is a number, and a number can be carried into a room by somebody who was not in the yard.** She signs **one** of the two long sheets and not the other, and says *once, and only once, and it is not because you asked, because a yard cannot ask and every one of you knows it.* **She is the answer to the empty box and she was not summoned: she was already there.**
- **The three no's.** A Tanner's yard household: the work must be in their own yard or they are being somewhere. A Ropewalk household that carried pails for twelve days: *you have come for it a second time in a different coat.* **And Nadja Ostrow, which is the finding of the week and which nobody saw coming: a public levy is days at the ordinary wage out of the district account, and the district account is the account her daughter's school seat comes off, and Wren Ostrow has been off the roll since the twenty-fourth.** She will take the work and not the days, and she asks the yard which of them came up her lane not knowing that. **The refusal is entered in her own words and stands, and nobody comes back to it.**
- **The third pile's blank heading gets a fifth answer in nineteen hands on the eighth and is still blank**, and the yard votes against filling it, on the grounds that a heading is the one thing on a sheet that cannot be put in afterwards by anybody who was not there on the day.

### The cut order and the school's number (Ch 37)

- **The form is refused in public at the Threshold, and the reason is the day's finding: a copy of a cut order would be a second cut order, and the water office is permitted one.** So the instruction that governs the ward cannot be given to the ward, and Milan Vray realises the ward could all along have asked whether anybody else had one.
- **The district account is a record** and a ratepayer may read a rate, and **the ward had never thought to ask** because the water office strikes a line without writing to the people it is about. Kade's one sentence sends them there.
- **The school's line is a sum**, and the yard works out in the open that the office values a school as a fixed thing, so a cut does not remove a school — it removes **the number of children in it. Fourteen places against ninety-one.** **Rusk says the number out loud once, in the yard, at the fourth bell, and refuses to say it again anywhere but a room.** Rezek refuses to write it and says instead that nobody cut his hand — **lines are cut**, by a man with a frame in a room on the last working day of a month, with nobody present, and there is no form for it.
- **Rusk's sentence for the room: a thing that cannot be given a copy is a thing that can be changed with nobody present.** She says she cannot write it in a room because the reason she cannot is on a wall in a frame with a stamp and no copy, and that she will say it in a yard every week until somebody is angry enough to write it down, and that this is not a procedure and she has nothing better.
- **Grale carries his seed and does not resolve it.** Asked to say the line's position in a room he says no, and gives the reason: it gets cheaper as it goes down, somebody worked that out about sixty years ago, he has a horror of something he cannot prove, **and the day he proves it he has to stop.** **Nobody in Morrowfen knows who A-0 is and this batch must not explain it.** The frame stays turned to the wall.
- **Kade's position at the close of the batch:** she has stopped asking Marek things, has said so explicitly, and has said that she is not being kind to him — she is being careful with a record, because a kindness can be taken back. **Marek's unanswered answer to Pella Rusk from Ch 18 is still unanswered and is not paid off here.**

### Ondra Kelb's recovery (Ch 33 — the third and last practice chapter, played as technique)

- **The schedule is his own.** Three rules: a thing is not done again until it has been done clean three days running; a stopping is written down as a stopping, in the hand of the person who stopped; a day that cost nothing and was worse than the day before is a day, and is written down as a day. Four columns and a ruled line: *day, hour, what, what it cost me.*
- **The hand-book works and it is the wrong hand.** Vendl asked for the Ropewalk maker by name — Hesper's kindness of Ch 20, paid, entered in the book without comment. Laced on, it produces a fair fast hand at the speed of a man in his forties. **Kelb's own left hand has taken over the fine work it never did, and it is a nine-year-old's writing, and it is better than the hand-book and it is not his.**
- **The controlled experiment:** twenty lines from the issue ledger in his ordinary hand, no strap, stopped at twenty, with four days off the right hand whatever happens. **It takes four seconds and the figures come out the shape of a child of nine and are all correct.** The cost is four dead days of the right hand, his mother's basket, and one of his own rules broken to make room for a stranger's good figures.
- **A man from the copy room leaves a strip with his own hourly figure on it, unsigned, and Kelb extends his third rule by hand to cover the case, with no authority whatever, and notes that it is the first rule in the business he has broken.**
- **The fifth column has no heading** and reads *what I am for now*, seven words in four days, and he puts his hand over it and says Marek may look again next Saturday and that he does not want the name if it arrives too easily, because he wants to see whether Marek goes and asks anybody.
- **He is not written to forgive Marek quickly and not written to hate him.** In Ch 39 he defends himself with the only number he has, in a room, in public, and his mother then says his name.

### The year scar and the family book (Ch 34 — LOCKED, NOT RESOLVED)

**Tamsin produces a house book** of about the size of a hymn book, in her hand, older than her, with a date and the word *keep* inside the front cover in a hand that is not hers. Five columns, domestic headings: *in*, *out*, a date, a fourth column ruled straight with **nothing at the head of it**, and a fifth headed ***carried*** in the same small level hand. In the fourth column, on the third day of a month with no number written beside it, **four notches**; in the second column, in a different ink, **a name**; and the whole line **struck through in a third ink that has gone brown** by a hand that is neither. There is **no account of the striking anywhere in the book and no signature on it.** She has taken the page out and put it back twice in thirty-one years.

- **She says the number is not an accident, and refuses to say whose it is, and the refusal is not protection.** *You have been asking me about a mark on your arm since you were eight. You have never once asked me a question. I was not hiding anything; I was refusing the question.* And she will not give a name, because a name would be what the mark is for the rest of his life, and because she can see the line was struck out by somebody with no authority and no reason, **and she says out loud that he is not going to be allowed to say so.**
- **He does the arithmetic on his own stair and gets the shape of the answer and not the answer:** four notches are a count; a count is what a man writes when he cannot write a name; the name is struck through; the striking is not the writing hand; the fourth column has no heading. The edge of the answer is the striking.
- **He does not put it in his book.** There was space on that page since the sixteenth of the rains. It is not an instrument, it certifies nothing, and every word of it carries the bar. He makes himself be clear that this is a decision and not an oversight.
- **The mark is no longer hidden under a coat** as of Ch 34; a suspended clerk has nothing to be careful with.
- **The account is Volume 16's and the public testimony is MR 435's.** The number is not explained, no second person decodes it, and Hesper's Chapter 25 line — *a man who hides a mark has decided what it is* — is the thing that hurts at the end of Ch 34, in a variant: **he has not been hiding a mark for a fortnight, he has been carrying a page.**

### Hesper Rann in Movement IV (Ch 38 — locked)

**Kind in Ch 32, near the thing he has made in Ch 33–37 without speaking of it, simply cruel in Ch 38, and pleasant and procedural in Ch 39.** The mask does not drop in Ch 39; it is removed by other people.

- **He has a sister, forty-four, twenty-two years on a copying floor on the fifth floor of the fund building, home at a quarter to six every day.** Eleven weeks ago a man on that floor cut his thumb and sat down on the boards and would not say what had happened to his hand, and the foreman sent him home, and **the wax came off the board and there was a mark on the wood under where the wrist would have been, and she washed the wax off and told nobody, and has not mentioned it to her brother once.** Hesper uses this as a knife and says the price of not asking, and **never says what the mark is and never gives Marek a chance to ask.**
- **The W-4 is nineteen days in the drawer.** Neither produced nor destroyed. He says a form is worth what the man holding it says it is worth on the day he holds it up, that the start date is a man's decision and not his, and that he has had a reason every day and will not give one. He offers it twice more: *if you had been clever enough to make me I should have had to do it and I should have resented you for the rest of my life.*
- **He says Tomas arrived at refusing the schedule himself**, over a fortnight, from a clerk in a corridor, and that Marek has never once asked his brother whether he knows the paper exists.
- **He has read the school register at the Tallow Gate** — the register, not the levy roll, and a Trust administrator may read a public body's register because the Trust is the payee of the account it comes off. *Vale, Lio: off the twenty-fourth, back on the first, off again on the sixth.* He says he has read eleven names and four are the ones a levy can move, that the school is the last line on an order with no copy, that the ward's levy days have not been drawn, and **that when they are drawn that is a line above the school — so his daughter's seat is the cheapest single thing in Tallow Ward.** He says he is not going to be able to do anything about it, that he has not slept properly since he looked, and that he looked on the Friday and wanted Marek to know it cost him something, so that Marek cannot afterwards say he did not know what he was doing.
- **The offer, in the same words as the seventeenth of the rains:** take the schedule and he will enter the Vale household's assessment at the close of the quarter on a figure he chooses, as supervisor of the settlement desk, **four times in nineteen years**, Grale would know, Grale will not be asked. Not in the rules, and in a drawer.
- **He never mentions the twelfth.** He says he has wanted Marek to get to tomorrow for about a week and has not helped him once. He says: *we are both very careful people and neither of us has done a single unkind thing on purpose in this room*, having just named a child from a register in a room with no second person in it and put nothing in writing on purpose, and calls that the first dishonest thing he has done in his office in nineteen years.

### Neris Kade and Marek (Ch 32, 35, 36, 37, 40 — locked)

**Professional respect plus one argument neither of them wins, and no movement in either direction.** **No first names, no warmth, no physical affection, no sulk.** What grows is the thing that is not romance: two competent people who have learned exactly what the other one will refuse to do. She says *Vale* and *Examiner* and nothing else. In Ch 32 she stops asking him questions in a corridor and says why. In Ch 35 she refuses a sheet and stands where the asking is, and says publicly that he has not touched them, so that the answer survives in mouths and not in a document. **In Ch 36 her term becomes the board's term and she never says where it came from.** In Ch 37 she says she has stopped asking him things and that it is not kindness. In Ch 40 she is given the second seat and a sheet by the Chair and her motion — record all four figures and adjudicate none — is minuted as the moral content of a board's first sitting.

### The board's hearing is on the sixteenth, and what is on the table

**The room is the second-floor hearing room, Bell Stair, built for thirty.** On the sixteenth, three hundred and forty-odd ward people will be in a corridor and a stair in the rain, because **a ward is not a person and a room holds thirty, and the first thing said in the room will have to be said by a person with a name in a box.** In the room: the falsified entry with its invented reserve and the footnote; page three and the difference noted and not adjudicated; the four figures (640, 440, 279h 40p, 441) recorded and none adjudicated; the cause as read in Ch 39; the district account at 580 per cent with a 40,900 carry that has been unapplied for three hundred years; the cut order with no copy, in a frame, turned to the wall; the school line and the number fourteen; the certificate of posting, which is in Marek's book and is the paper he surrenders in Ch 44; and Ilya's valve, which is a maintenance account and is Ch 47's.

### Ledger Sight in this batch: zero uses

**No panel, no use, and no refusal on stage either** — except that in Ch 32 Marek stands on a stair and does the arithmetic of not spending one, and works out the second cost for the first time: **a panel in front of somebody is not a headache, it is a reason to be asked questions in a room, and a room takes its answers from men who can sign.** Five uses remain behind him from the tenth to the fourteenth of the rains, plus a parcel in a coat he ate and cannot remember, a quarter of an hour of the twelfth night, and an hour on the night of the fourteenth with a man's voice in it. **No second standing hold is used, offered, wished for, or hinted to be available. Nothing above Boundary Hold. The clerk of record is not advanced and nobody appoints Marek anything.**

### Threads deliberately left open, and the threads frozen

**Frozen and NOT to be touched before their chapters:** the year scar's account (Volume 16) and its public testimony (MR 435) — Ch 34 gives the entry and the refusal and nothing else; **Pella Rusk's unsigned surety cell on page three — Ch 37 argued the school number in public and is not the place, and her Ch 18 question to Marek is still unanswered**; the Fund's discharge book and its four triple strokes; the Hollow Galleries; the reserve carry's origin and Grale's suspicion; the unclaimed take-off; **the ward roll naming district four and the name Kallow, which was not mentioned once in Chapters 11–40 and must not be**; Hesper's unanswered question about the mark; Hesper's four entries into the account desk; the mother's offer that is still on the table in her own kitchen; Hesper's W-4, still in a drawer; and **Hesper's authority to certify, which ends in the room in Ch 44.**

### Proposed canon changes recorded rather than made silently

- **No new world rule was created.** The Crown March representative's office, the emergency appeals board, its four terms, and the suspension are all **institutions and procedure in a volume whose subject is institutions and procedure**, and each is grounded in something already on the page (the ward's six terms, the Chamber's existing duty to record dissent, the Fund's existing rule, the recount, the certificate of posting, the assignment column). **The founding standing hold, the clerk of record and the Trust's no-copy standing order were not restated as new** and do not appear in this batch.
- **One small new date:** the **suspension hearing on the seventeenth** of next month, at the eighth bell. It is referred to in Ch 32 and Ch 33 and is not yet a scene. Ch 44 is where Marek surrenders the certificate; the seventeenth is available to it.
- **One small new rule of practice, not a world rule:** that a suspended clerk may not be a member of a counter-clerks' mutual, and that a Chamber clerk may not countersign a form with a suspended clerk's name on it. Both are stated as *practice* and not as law, in Sedge's and Vendl's mouths, which is the point being made.
- **One thing `outline/volume-01.md` does not yet reflect, recorded as a divergence rather than papered over.** (a) The recount sitting of the twelfth **is held and is overtaken by the board's hearing of the sixteenth**, and the aimed certification is exposed there with **seven** ward families in the gallery and three hundred and forty outside. The outline's Movement IV line for Ch 39 — a certification naming Kelb, a junior clerk's name destroyed in public — **is satisfied in substance and not in wording**: the document is a one-page certification of cause, the nine words are not printed, and the *careless* charge arrives in the room through Rezek's protest. That is the stronger delivery and it is deliberate. (b) **The outline's Ch 40 line is only half delivered, and the state file must not claim otherwise.** The outline says *the board is forced into session for a date and the settlement is postponed to the hearing.* **What Chapter 40 delivers is that the board is forced into session for a date, and that the COLLECTION is held four days by Harrow's own term — the settlement itself is not postponed at all.** The ward's finding of the thirtieth stands, its fourth clause is satisfied, and the bill is payable in the ordinary course the day after the finding whatever the board decides. **A later chapter may not say the settlement was postponed to the hearing, and may not say the board stopped the bill.** `outline/volume-01.md` is not edited; this entry is the record of the difference.
- **The engine correction, taken and recorded — and the honest version of it, which is narrower than the claim first made.** The batch prompt's hardest craft instruction was that a document being read aloud may not be the load-bearing event of more than about half the batch. **Chapters 34, 35, 36, 37 and 40 are carried by a person and a room and not by a sheet** — a kitchen, a storeroom, a yard going out into nineteen lanes, an argument about four terms, a counter queue and a yard, and four failed attempts at informality. Chapters 31, 32 and 38 are also carried by a person. **Only Chapter 39 is a document, and it is the batch's climax and it is the one chapter that has to be a document.** **But the earlier state entry overstated this by claiming the batch had no other reading-out, and that was wrong in two places: Chapter 37's climax is a figure read off the district account and said aloud on a slate in a yard, and Chapter 40's set-piece is the notice read aloud to the yard by Kelb.** Both are *a number or a notice spoken in a yard by a person*, not *a sheet entered into a room's record*, and the structural difference is real, but **a reviewer must not be told this batch ran no yard reading at all. It ran two, and Chapter 35's consolidation is a third, in which nineteen pairs read their own answers out at a tank.**

### Batch 0004 review fix pass — what was repaired and what was deliberately not

A review of the batch-0004 commit found five blocking, three high and six medium findings. All five blocking and all three high are fixed. **The batch was not restarted, no plot was moved, and no scene was replaced; the fixes are local to the sentences and paragraphs that were wrong.**

**Blocking, fixed in prose.**

1. **Ch 35's sheet arithmetic contradicted itself.** The rules sheet said *two sheets, two of you* and two sheets were then asked to hold forty-one doors walked by four hundred people, while a ward whose whole consent record sat with one pair of keepers broke the method's own rule. **Repaired by writing in the consolidation the reviewer asked for and the chapter had already gestured at:** each of the nineteen pairs carried its own keeper's and reader's sheet, and on the fourth day the pairs read their own answers out in the open at the tank while Kern wrote them onto the two long rule sheets and a woman from the low kitchens called each line back. The nineteen working sheets went on a nail. **The two long sheets still carry the twenty-nine answers and the three refusals**, so Chapter 40's *the two sheets of twenty-nine answers with three no's* stays true, and the record on the nail is new and is a better object than the one it replaced.
2. **The empty witness box was contradicted thirty lines later.** Vray's premise (*there is no such man in this ward*) was false in the same chapter, because Sera Holt walked in from outside the ward and signed. **Vray's premise is now a power and not a shortage of volunteers** — any one of them would do, which is why none of them can, and there is no form on which a yard asks the city for a person. **Holt's arrival is now the answer to the empty box rather than a violation of it**, and she says out loud that a yard cannot ask. The repair is also a small improvement: the box was always the chapter's best idea and it now has a reason.
3. **Ch 36's recorder's-pay vote was arithmetically impossible.** Four voting members cannot produce four to one, and the error had been copied into this file and into `open-threads.md`. **The vote is three to one**, Vendl now declines in the first person instead of in the third, and both Ch 36 votes are marked as three to one with the money matter distinguished from the non-money one.
4. **Ch 39's nine were seven.** Nineteen let in, nine named, seven listed. **Fourteen are let in, seven are in the room in their own names**, and Marek sits in the last row rather than a ninth seat that Ondra Kelb was also occupying. **Seven, not nine, everywhere, in the prose and in all three state files.**
5. **The aimed certification existed in four documents and not in the prose.** What Ch 39 filed was a recount form with a cause line. **Ch 39 now names the instrument: a one-page certification of cause**, which is what a recount's cause is written on and the only one-page instrument in the city that puts a clerk's name at the top of a sentence about a man's own work. The outline's line for Ch 39 is therefore satisfied in substance, and **the separate claim that Ch 40 postponed the settlement is recorded above as a half-delivered outline line**, because only the collection is held.

**High, fixed.** The engine claim was overstated in three files and is now stated narrowly and honestly in all of them, with the two yard reading-outs named. **The batch is over the 28,000 ceiling at 28,363 and the state files now say so**, with the per-chapter figures, the reason no further cutting was done, and a reduced budget handed to batch 0005. **`the whole of` is back down to 12 from 26, and `about` from 178 to 146**, undoing the batch-0003 regression the reviewer flagged.

**Medium, fixed.** Ch 40's *Saturday in a room in a fortnight's time* is now *Friday in a room on Bell Stair*, which is the ninth; Marek is at the back of the yard under the tank steps' overhang instead of a corridor, because the meeting is at the tank; Ch 31's *on the third of this month* is now the second, which is when the strike was entered; Vendl's third-person minute is first-person; **and the board-composition trap is closed** — Kade is not a member, cannot vote, and her sheet's function is to hold the four figures in the order they are called.

**Two defects the reviewer did not flag and the fix pass found while verifying the above.** Ch 31 has **"That is four reasons"** spoken over Vole's three, and an unattributed line in the Vole–Harrow exchange in which Renata Vole speaks third-person about Hesper as if she were him. Both are repaired. Ch 38 announces *the second thing and it is about a man I have watched for nineteen years* and then delivers a woman; it now says *a woman on a floor I have watched for nineteen years*, which also breaks the collision between Hesper's nineteen years and the number hanging off his sister.

**Nothing in the guardrails moved.** Kallow, district four, A-0, the Last Convocation, the missing year, Ledger Sight, the second hold, the clerk of record and Pella Rusk's surety cell are all exactly where they were, and **the review confirmed that Chapters 31–40 leak nothing about the twelfth, that no paragraph is duplicated within the batch or against Chapters 1–30, and that Ch 38 still gives the mechanism away nowhere.**

## Volume 01 facts established by batch 0005 (Chapters 41–50) — the last batch of the volume

Prose is authoritative. Movement V runs from the sixteenth of next month to the last evening of the rains and closes the volume. **The board's hearing, the arrest, the finding, the conversion, the oath, the road, the four rooms and the copy room are the movement; there is no sixth movement and there is no further chapter batch.**

### The calendar of the batch (extends the fixed chain; the ninth of the rains is a Wednesday)

**Sixteenth Thursday, seventeenth Friday, eighteenth Saturday, nineteenth Sunday, twentieth Monday, twenty-first Tuesday, twenty-second Wednesday.** No weekday is stated anywhere in Chapters 41–50 and none needs to be. **The hearing is the sixteenth at the eighth bell with four days' notice; the finding is made in the Gate yard on the seventeenth at the sixth bell; Ilya's account is closed on the twentieth at the second bell; the W-4 comes out of custody on the twenty-second; Hesper's Crown March hearing is the third of next month; Vole's notice and the sealed Trust notice are the fourth of next month; the copy is made on the eighteenth of next month, which is the last evening of the rains.**

**Three institutional dates the batch fixes:** the sealed Trust notice is nailed to the Tally Hall door on the fourth and is still there on the eighteenth, **fourteen days**; the Chamber's suspension slip comes down off the clerk's column on the **morning of the eighteenth**, footed *grounded in a paper produced to the board*; **Marek's suspension hearing of the seventeenth never sits**, because the instrument it was grounded in went into a finding on the sixteenth.

### The instruments, unchanged, and the fourth chapter of the movement

Nothing in Chapters 41–50 moved any of the locked instruments and the batch is careful about the difference.

- **The ward settlement entry** is the false-witnessed one. Hesper signed it on the ninth, **Kelb countersigned it in pencil the same afternoon**, and it was read aloud in full and in public in **Chapter 43**, including the third clause's standing-reserve recital, the plate and its date, the schedule cited at the foot of the fourth clause, and the omission of page three. **The collection landed on it in Chapter 39 and the board's finding on the seventeenth struck it from the public copy and not from the desk's book** — the board cannot strike an entry out of a desk's book and says so; the margin of the entry now carries *struck from the public copy by the emergency appeals board, seventeenth of next month, and not replaced*.
- **The certificate of posting** is the separate one-page paper. **Marek surrenders it in Chapter 44** and it is kept with the finding. It is not the lie's signature. **The nine words of the aimed cause are never printed in this batch** — Chapter 44 has Hesper give the *shape* of the certification at Harrow's express instruction, and the reader who has Chapter 20 has the sentence.
- **The aimed certification** — the one-page **certification of cause** of the twenty-first, filed on the twelfth — is **exposed as an aimed chain in Chapter 44, and the exposure is done by Hesper Rann answering a procedural question correctly and by Renata Vole naming the action, not by a confession.** A cause must be capable of being acted on; the action is the collection; the collection lands at the counter-desk; the counter-signature is Kelb's. **The chain follows the instrument, not the folder is not re-explained anywhere in Chapters 41–50**, and Marek's line in Ch 44 is only *my name is not on the page and has never been on the page*.
- **Page three** is read in **Chapter 43 as a shape and a count**: eleven lines, four columns, eleven sureties named, **the witness column empty on all eleven**, counted aloud by the Chair in the room. **The cell is not resolved. Pella Rusk says the sentence a reader can check — *a surety on that page is a person who has not been asked to stand anything* — and Marek gives her a technically accurate silence for the second time in the volume. She still does not know.**
- **The levy roll** is unchanged: eleven households, the four payable named in Ch 41 and Ch 44 in the same order (fuel basin, Ropewalk, the Tanner's yard, the Gate yard), two carrying a household name in the margin, **the Vale household still seventh and paying later**. No Prent and no Marsh household is named. **Tomas's vigor loan is never mentioned in Chapters 41–47 and is its own chapter in 48**, and the chapter says in the first person that it is not a refund.

### The board — what it is, on the day it sat, and what it cannot do

**Five. The Chair plus four members, and nobody else at that table.** Harrow presiding and on the list of members ineligible to be chair; Vole for the Trust; Sedge for the Chamber; Kern for the ward in his own name; Vendl for the Fund. **The Chair does not vote in a money matter, so a money vote is at most three to one, and no money vote is taken in this batch.** **Neris Kade is not a member and cannot vote** and is given the second seat and a sheet; her sheet holds the four figures in the order they were called, and in Ch 46 she stands up to have **the terms tested** and is thanked by nobody, including, explicitly, by the Chair. **Harrow serves the minutes in his own hand.** **The office's three officers stand at the back** — Vole's war, won by her.

- **The hearing opens on two rulings that are now canon for the batch.** Vole's three points: a certified finding is not appealable and a returnpath needs a living holder of the obligation; the matter was enlarged on the thirtieth without asking a party; a ward cannot be sworn. **Harrow rules that she is right on the law and wrong about the consequence: nobody in the room is sworn, every person is heard as a person in their own name, a declining is written as a declining, and no answer binds anybody but the person who gave it.** **This is the engine of Chapters 41–47 and it is the ward's one procedural gain.**
- **The Trust produces one item and refuses the rest of its own file** — the order is the Deputy's, and her reason is that an instrument read before an account is a document read to a room that has not been told what it is about.
- **The board's four terms are confirmed on the seventeenth, not restated from nothing**, each member saying *stands* in a yard, and Vendl says in the first person that she has been asked twice to take money for a sitting and has declined twice and is still in the room.
- **The duty to record dissent is used against the person who proposed it, and this is the volume's institutional proof.** **Aldis Kern dissents in public** on the ground that a levy on four hundred and six named people out of twenty-nine answers is a thing the ward has not agreed to and cannot be made true without days the yard has not been given; **Harrow has to write a ward captain's objection into a finding in longhand, in the open, because he proposed the term and there is no clerk.** **Renata Vole uses it in Chapter 44** by dissenting on the word *aimed* and having her grounds written in her own words. The Chair says out loud that if it only ever came out in his favour it would not be worth the nine minutes it took to write.
- **A duty to record dissent is what the Deputy's own office is already living with**, and she says so in Ch 49: since the seventeenth her office has twice had to record the grounds of a member of the board in a board's finding, in that member's own words, unasked and unpaid. **This is NOT the year-end reading of Ilya's open account, which is a separate instrument with a separate clock: Ilya puts it eleven months out in Ch 47 and the finding of the twentieth is the instrument that made it a question about a board rather than a question about a man. Do not conflate the two.**
- **The board still cannot strike an entry out of a desk's book, cannot choose between the four figures, and cannot serve a ward.** **The four hands stay open.** 640, 440, 279h 40p and 441 are on a table against a wall in Ch 43 and none is adjudicated; the Trust certifying 440 is not the figures agreeing.

### What the ward's bill became, and what it cost

**The conversion: not days, not four hundred unasked names — supervised labour, named, at the ordinary wage, in the ward's own yards, on a schedule written out and hung on the board at the Gate tank, with a household that will not work not having another's share put on it.** The schedule takes the yard forty-one doors already asked and gives it the days to ask the rest.

**It is charged to the reserve carry.** This is the volume's institutional turn at its deepest: forty thousand nine hundred, entered every year since the flood years, unapplied because no form asks whether the fund exists, and **a board is a body and a body can name a district, which no office can.** The recited reserve leaned on exactly this line, so naming it is the volume's own argument turned round. **Grale states the cost in a yard and it is not resolved:** it is not money in a box, it is a hole with a number on it, and the moment the word *Tallow* is beside it the hole is a debt, and in about nine months this yard will be back at that tank and the office will want it back and there will be nothing on the line to give it. **The carry's origin stays frozen and Grale's suspicion is not resolved and nobody is named.**

**A thing with no copy, and the institutional answer to it.** Pella Rusk's sentence goes into the finding in her own words: *a thing that cannot be given a copy is a thing that can be changed with nobody present.* The answer is a procedure and not a name: **any cut order is read, in the words of the office, to every party it names, before it is entered; it is not entered at the close of a month; it carries the day and not the month; and it carries the name of the officer who enters it.** The water office is still permitted one such order and still permitted no copy. **Grale signs the first one that has ever carried his name.** **The cost, which the yard works out in the finding:** the school is named as a party and that is the whole of the protection, the north gardens' crew is ninth and the school is last, and a yard which knows a line is coming will spend the quarter before it lands.

**Nadja Ostrow's refusal is answered in the yard, in public, and it costs.** She is asked in a room on the sixteenth, after the school number is in the record, and she refuses the days a second time and names the cost — a man in a good coat has come up her lane twice and asked her to take money out of her own daughter's seat. On the seventeenth she says what she got and what she did not: **the days are gone for good and that is the shape of a thing that is gone, not the shape of a seat coming back; Wren Ostrow is still off the roll; the four children off since the twenty-fourth are still off; nobody in the yard has put one of them back; and the wage is coming out of a line nobody has ever drawn, so in nine months somebody will tell her she has had a gift.** **She does not thank the Crown March, does not thank the man in the cell, and does not thank the woman who read the finding out in the rain, and says she wants that in the yard's memory and not in a document.**

### Ilya's record, and the oath that is not an oath

**The account is closed on the twentieth on a finding of fact with no penalty and no settlement, and the finding is one paragraph** carrying the breach, the rescue, the ward's damage, the offer and the entry of the make-good together. Harrow says he had it wrong since Tuesday and asks a man who is not a Crown March representative to tell him so; Ilya says it is four sentences too long and that it is correct.

- **The two breaches are now on the record as two.** Seven days against the sealed order of the seventh; **twelve days of his own authority, which he names as the worse one**, because a man who holds a valve against a written order is disobeying and a man who holds one with no order at all is deciding.
- **The Underbank is item one on the ward's sheet and permanently.** Vasil Ferun, thirteen, with **the boy's own fault on the sheet as well as the water**, in Kern's words, *because an account that leaves out the boy's fault is a handbill.* Then the storehouse and the condemned tar, the hundred and forty carriers whose names were never taken by anybody, and the tank and the two hundred children.
- **Ilya refuses to be the first name on the account**, because a thing with no witness is a thing the room believes the person in front of it about, and the day the finding gives the room a reason to stop believing him is the day a sluice is shut on a rising river with nobody holding a lamp.
- **The offer is on the record twice, in the same words, refused twice**: a yard and a half a year, twenty-two years, this hand — *put it in as an offer made and not accepted, because it is not a sacrifice and I will not have it read in a year as a man who gave something.*
- **Ilya's warning about the year-end reading is the batch's sharpest institutional beat**: four days after the board named a district against the carry and protected a line at the bottom of a page, the open account is a question about a board and not a question about a man. Nobody in the room had thought of it and three of the five at the table get there at once.
- **The oath.** Harrow's rule from the sixteenth is that nobody can be sworn. **Kern asks for his statement to be sworn, says plainly that he cannot be, and asks for the word anyway; Harrow writes it and tells the yard he has written a word on a sheet that is not true so that the table can be right about a large thing in a record.** The sheet goes to the Gate yard. **Kern's rule is permanent: an account that leaves out the fault is a handbill.**
- **Kern pays, does not forgive, and does not blame, and means every clause of it.**

### Tomas's schedule — a schedule and not a refund

**The W-4 comes out of custody in a Trust officer's bundle, produced by Renata Vole unasked and with the board unable to ask for it**, and that is the last thing she does in the volume and it is the reason the reader is left in no doubt she was partly right. The form names the north-road span, **eleven days a season for three seasons**, and an independent witness.

- **Tomas refuses it a third time**, and what he refuses is the framing: nobody is giving him a schedule, he signed for it in front of two witnesses, the draw posted on the fourteenth as scheduled, **twenty-six months is twenty-six months**, and a man who takes the help for the schedule and lets anybody call it a rescue has misunderstood what a schedule is. **He is never written as a man somebody saved.**
- **The one sentence he puts in the yard's mouth**: his mother has a mark on his brother's arm with four notches in it and has never said where it came from, and he is saying now that nobody was helped, so that in four years there is one sentence from somebody who was there. **The year scar is not explained and nobody decodes it.**
- **The independent witness is Sera Holt**, and she rules Marek out in the same breath — *he is the ward's own clerk of the day and his brother is the man on the form* — and signs one side and not the other, as she signed one long sheet and not the other. **This is the answer to the empty witness box of Chapter 35 and it is not a favour.**
- **The draw is never cancelled and the years come back to nobody.** Renata Vole reads the whole of the loan out in a yard in the first person and says the sentence the chapter exists for: **it is not a refund; a refund gives something back, and this gives nothing back, and there is nobody in Bell Stair able to give anything back to anybody and she has looked.**
- **The crew is named**: six, headed by Tomas; **Bohum Ostrek with two weeks of the hand gone lawfully**; and a Tanner's-yard household that said on the fifth it would take the work in its own yard or not at all, and was heard. The residual days in the ward yards go onto the Gate board in a fourth column.
- **Harrow cannot promise it.** The Crown March countersign is on a road between here and the coast and until it comes he does not know the schedule is lawful, and neither does the Deputy, and neither does the man who wrote the form in a room with a leg on it.

### Kelb, Hesper, Vole — the three accounts

- **Kelb's restitution does not cover it.** The Fund pays a clinic, a cot, medicine, and the Ropewalk maker **by name**, which is Hesper's kindness of the twentieth of the rains, entered in the record because it was correct and cost him nothing. The Chamber pays a clerk's injury out of a levy on the hall after nine years. The new sum comes out of the carry. **Vendl says four things about *enough* in the first person: it does not buy a hand, it does not buy the years, and the fourth thing is that she does not know what the fourth thing is** — and there are four marks in her discharge book meaning a clerk who never came back, and she is not opening that in a room with a mother in it. **The Fund's discharge book is not resolved and is not decoded.**
- **Kelb's only question is the name in the finding**, and the answer is yes and no: the finding says *Ondra Kelb, junior counter-clerk, who countersigned the totals on the ninth with a pencil, correctly* — and the word *careless* is in the finding too, because it is in the certification of the twelfth and cannot be taken out of the record, with the finding saying beside it that no witness said it. **Both, on one page, in that order.** He does not forgive Marek quickly and he does not hate him.
- **Hesper Rann is in custody pending trial and offers evidence.** He says in Chapter 44 that he has never said he was wrong about the gate; **in Chapter 45 that he is not going to say it and will not let anybody write it down for him, and that if it is in the finding he will appeal it, and there will not be anything in the finding to appeal.** He is arrested in the room on the fifth ground of Harrow's own instrument — *a question of conduct excluded from a proceeding in which it ought to have been heard* — by two men of the Crown March's administrative staff, sent for at the fourth-bell recess, and **he is not marched out of a corridor; it happens at a table in a room built for thirty with the ward's people in it.** **His last act is a question: does the ward have water.** Nadja Ostrow answers it and refuses to thank him in the room, in front of the men taking him.
- **In Chapter 49 he offers four dates** on which he made an entry in the account desk that was not a levy, a schedule, a posting or a return, and there is no box for the fourth kind. **Grale stands up at the fourth date and gives three of the four and keeps the fourth**, because there is a line in that account standing since before the flood years and if he says the date in a room it is in a record in Crown March — *the day I prove it, I have to stop.* **This is a seed opened, not a thread closed: Hesper's four entries into the account desk, and the line in the account, both remain live.**
- **Vole is reassigned to the coastal returns** and does not accept the characterisation, in the first person, and never concedes she was wrong. She says her finding stands, the ward will still pay, and that she was argued out of her own opinion in a yard. She says the other thing: the duty to record dissent is now in her building and her office is already living with it, and it was put there by a Crown March representative she refused for nine years on a point of form, and she does not know what to do with that. **The reader is left in no doubt she was partly right.**

### The Trust's order, and the copy

**The sealed notice, nailed to the Tally Hall door on the fourth of next month and still there on the eighteenth:** the public copy of the first page is to be replaced; **the founding hand is not to be reproduced in it**; the original is not to be removed from the case; **and the work is to be carried out by the officer of the record and by no other hand.** The duty clerk reads it out twice and **nobody will admit to reading it.**

**The office of the clerk of record has been vacant for three centuries and there is no form for filling it**, so the order **cannot be carried out at all**, and the consequence is the volume's last mechanism: the public register will simply have an empty page where the oldest page should be, **and an empty page with nothing under it is not a blank line with a signature on it — it is a page anybody at all may write in, and in nine months somebody will, for a good reason, with no idea whatever.** This is the volume's argument about the recited reserve turned on its own instrument.

**Chapter 50 is the last image and it is a completed act.** Marek copies the first page of the oldest register into the public register **by hand**, breaking the standing order's second clause and obeying its spirit; **he writes the blank line and leaves it blank** — he starts a four, stops, and goes over it so the line is blank and clean; **and beneath it he writes the signature in full, letter by letter, as a clerk is taught to do when the signature is not his, with *written in full*, the date, and *M. Vale, clerk, no office*, which he reads and does not change.** **A-0, written out in full.** The copy goes into the public register on a table by the window, where anybody may read it, and about forty people did. **Lio asks whose year the empty one is and he tells her the only true thing he has** — somebody has to write down what the tower owes, the taking is always written down before anybody has looked at the giving, and a man who invents a year is doing in one stroke what the district account has been doing for three hundred years — **and then he says that the line is not going to be closed while he is the clerk of record.** He is not the clerk of record. **Nobody appoints him, and he has no office, and the sentence is in nobody's record because there is no form in which he could file one.** Tamsin is not asked anything. **The Trust's notice stays nailed to the door, unsigned by anybody who will admit to reading it.**

### The volume's concrete resolutions, recorded as achieved or not achieved

| Resolution | Status |
|---|---|
| The falsified entry struck and published with its correction, not quietly replaced | **ACHIEVED (Ch 46).** Struck from the public copy, not from the desk's book, with the words *and not replaced* in a district accountant's hand. |
| An emergency appeals board exists afterward, with a public duty to record dissent | **ACHIEVED (Ch 44, Ch 46).** Confirmed on the seventeenth; the duty is used against its own author twice and the Deputy's office is already living with it in Ch 49. |
| The ward's bill moved to a public levy plus supervised labour, about four hundred named people, paid work, nothing already drawn returned | **ACHIEVED IN PART, HONESTLY (Ch 46).** Not days and not the unasked; the yard has to ask again, and the school line is named as a party but no child goes back. |
| Ilya's valve in the record as sabotage and rescue in the same paragraph, the ward's share named under oath, the Underbank before the sluice permanently | **ACHIEVED (Ch 47).** One paragraph, no penalty, offer recorded twice and refused twice. |
| Tomas's settlement schedule converted into supervised road work with named workers and an independent witness; the draw never cancelled; the years returned to nobody | **ACHIEVED (Ch 48).** Six named workers, Sera Holt as witness, Marek ruled out, and Vole says in a yard that it is not a refund. |
| Ondra Kelb keeps the loss in his hand, receives restitution that does not cover it, is not written to forgive quickly | **ACHIEVED (Ch 49).** The hand-book works and is the wrong hand; *careless* stays in the record beside its refutation. |
| Hesper Rann in custody pending trial, offering evidence, never saying he was wrong about the gate | **ACHIEVED (Ch 45, Ch 49).** Arrested in the room; four dates offered; three given, one kept. |
| Renata Vole reassigned without conceding she was wrong, and the reader sure she was partly right | **ACHIEVED (Ch 49).** |
| The Trust's sealed order to strike A-0 from the public copy arrives and Marek refuses it in writing | **PARTIALLY ACHIEVED, DELIBERATELY (Ch 49, Ch 50).** The order is to *replace* the page and *not reproduce the founding hand*; Marek does not write a refusal, because a suspended-then-unassigned clerk with no office has no instrument to refuse it with, and there is no form on which he could. He refuses it by **doing the opposite in longhand in the public register, in his own hand, with his own name and no office under it**, and the notice stays on the door unsigned. This is recorded as a divergence from the outline's wording, and the reason is that a refusal in writing would require him to be an officer. |
| The clerk of record | **NOT ADVANCED.** He says the line is not going to be closed while he is the clerk of record, to a nine-year-old on a bench, and nobody appoints him. |

### Deliberately NOT resolved in this volume, and the frozen list

**Untouched and to be untouched by Volume 02:** Marek's year scar and the house book's struck line (**Ch 48 puts one sentence about it in Tomas's mouth and decodes nothing**); **Pella Rusk's unsigned surety cell on page three** (read as a count in Ch 43, unresolvable by the reader, and she still does not know); Zorka Vendl's discharge book and its four triple strokes; the Hollow Galleries and Marda Senn; **the origin of the reserve carry and Grale's suspicion, which is carried a step further and not one step resolved**; the unclaimed take-off above the second-junction ward meter and the nine gardeners on account 4-Crown; **Hesper's four entries into the account desk and the line in that account standing since before the flood years**; **the ward roll naming district four and the name Kallow, which was not mentioned once in Chapters 11–50**; the mother's refusal about the mark and the fifth thing Vendl does not know; Hesper's W-4, which was produced on the twenty-second and is a schedule rather than a rescue; the duty clerk's unplaced roll of 1994; Nim Rook and Ilen Rook; the third pile's blank column heading; Ostrow's residual days, the yard's second round of doors, and the fourteen places.

**Also still open and handed to Volume 02 (*The Sable Market*):** the ward must ask the remaining doors; the carry is now a debt and the office will come for it in nine months; the district account will be cut again and this time every party is named and the author's name is on it; the school will find out before the north gardens' crew does; a suspended-then-unassigned clerk has a certificate in a finding and no standing; **a Crown March instrument has a fifth ground that was used, and there is more where it came from.**

### Ledger Sight in this batch: zero uses, and zero on stage

**Five batches, ten chapters, no panel, and no refusal staged either.** The second cost of the power — *a panel in front of somebody is a reason to be asked questions in a room, and a room takes its answers from men who can sign* — is inherited from Ch 32 and is paid in Ch 44 without the Sight being used: **Marek breaks his own silence and says a true thing that cannot be produced, and is asked nothing in return, because what he has is a wall and not an instrument.** **No second standing hold is used, offered, wished for, or hinted at. Nothing above Boundary Hold.** The standing hold and the clerk of record are canon and are not restated as new — the clerk of record is named three times in Ch 50 and once is the office nobody holds.

### Deviations from the batch prompt and the outline, recorded rather than made silently

- **The nine words of the cause are not printed in the batch.** Ch 44 has Hesper give the *shape* of the certification at Harrow's express instruction and has Harrow refuse to have it read aloud, so that what happens on the sixteenth is a board's finding and not a Crown March representative's sentence. This is a deliberate strengthening of the brief, not a departure from it.
- **Chapter 46 is a yard and a finding, and the finding is not read aloud in it.** The finding is *made* in the open, in sentences, one at a time, over two hours, with nobody holding a page; it is read in the same yard the next morning by a person who is not a clerk (Neris Kade, for the finding of the seventeenth) and on the twenty-first by another (Zorka Vendl, for Ilya's). **As corrected in the repair pass, Ch 46 no longer narrates the morning reading as already happened** — Ch 46 is set on the evening of the seventeenth and Nadja Ostrow now speaks the reading forward, in the moment. **The reading still happens on the eighteenth; the chapter simply does not spend it before it arrives.**
- **The chapter-type distribution of the batch:** Ch 41 a room and a procedural war; **Ch 42 five people talking, and the ward's evidence in the order it happened rather than the order it was certified**; **Ch 43 the document, the only one**; Ch 44 a surrender and a mask taken off by a question; Ch 45 an arrest and one sincere question; **Ch 46 a yard, a finding and a woman who has said no**; **Ch 47 an oath that is not an oath**; Ch 48 a form and then a road — six named men standing on the north-road span in the rain, and a rope-maker saying *that is a span*; Ch 49 four rooms; Ch 50 one man, a lamp and a page. **No chapter ends on a figure or a notice being read out to a crowd.**
- **The Trust's order is not refused in writing in the terms the outline words it.** See the table above. The prose delivers the refusal and the reason.
- **One small new rule of practice, stated as practice and not as law, and it is the volume's last institutional act:** *a clerk reproducing a signature that is not the clerk's writes it out in full, letter by letter, with the words* written in full, *the date, and the clerk's own name and no office — and the reason is that a shorthand mark on a page is a thing a person can put on a page.* It is taught in the first three weeks of a post and it is the one rule Marek obeys in Ch 50 while breaking another.
- **One small new institutional act:** the cut order procedure of Ch 46 — read to the parties named, not entered at the close of a month, carrying the day and the name of the officer who enters it — and **one consequence, which is that the school now finds out before the ninth line does, and a yard which knows a line is coming will spend the quarter before it lands.**
- **One small new date-pair:** the Crown March hearing of the third of next month and the sealed notice of the fourth, both inside Volume 02's reach.
- **No new world rule and no new institution.** Harrow's office, the board, its terms, the suspension, the Crown March fifth ground and the Crown March administrative staff are all either already canon or already on the page. **No lender, market, broker, Choir, military body or regional political crisis has entered. Renata Vole is a Trust administrator and has been reassigned inside it. The founding standing hold is not used, offered or hinted at. Nobody in Morrowfen knows who A-0 is.**
- **A reader must be left unable to believe the board saved the ward from its bill, and Chapters 41–45 say so four times**: Vole on the sixteenth (*nothing this board does disturbs it*), Hesper in Chapter 45 (four households will be asked for two hundred and seventy-nine hundreds and forty pints), Marek in the same chapter (the delay is his and stays as a year scar, and the collection was never his to direct), and Harrow's own four days, which he called a queue behind a door in Chapter 40 and never once revises.


### Batch 0005 review pass — what was found and what was fixed

A review of Chapters 42–50 found one blocking, eight high and fourteen medium and low findings. **All of the canon findings were checked and all passed except one**, which was fixed. **The prose pass found two blocking instances of meta language and they are fixed.** The chapter map, the required character changes, the romance guardrail and Chapter 50's status as a completed act were all confirmed.

**Blocking, fixed.** Two sentences narrated *"the whole of what this volume did to the city"* and *"the whole of the tower's arithmetic in this volume"*, both of which break the fourth wall. Now *the whole of what a month of this did to the city* and *the whole of the tower's arithmetic*. **A grep of Chapters 41–50 for *this volume* now returns nothing.**

**High, fixed.**
- **The room was being counted as fourteen ward people.** The Chair's ruling in Ch 41 puts **seven** in the room, and six chapters said fourteen. The side walls hold seven; the back of the room holds Marek, Rann, Grale and the office's officers. **CORRECTED AT THE batch-0005 REPAIR: the claim below was false when this file was written.** The earlier pass recorded that *every instance is now seven and the count is consistent across Ch 42, 43, 44 and 45*, and Chapter 44 still read **Fourteen people along two side walls** at that moment. It is now seven. **A writer must not treat a verification claim in this file as a substitute for reading the line: a self-reported pass caught the instance in Chapter 42 and reported the whole batch clean.**
- **Lio was called Marek's sister's daughter.** Lio is **Tomas's** daughter and Marek has no sister. Corrected, and **Lio is now named in the exchange and she asks the outline's question directly: *Whose year is it.***
- **"Nine words" was attached to the wrong document.** Ch 43 had the Deputy counting the *footnote* at nine words, which both miscounts it and hangs the volume's protected phrase on a document that must stay clean. The count is cut; the footnote is now *that is the whole of it, and a full stop, and I have read it four times.*
- **Ch 49 contradicted Ch 47 on the year-end reading.** Ilya puts it eleven months out; Vole claimed her office had already done one twice since the seventeenth. Corrected — **her office has twice had to record a member's grounds in a board's finding, unasked**, which is what term 4 actually produced and is a better sentence. The two instruments are now kept apart in this file.
- **Ch 49's reassignment took effect on the first of next month and was signed on the fourth.** Now *the first of the month after*.
- **Ch 48 dated the schedule to the fourth of next month**, which is Vole's notice and the Trust's sealed notice. Now the twenty-second.
- **Ch 45 and Ch 49 ended on the same sentence** — no form for a claim against a thing you saw and could not read, and none for three hundred years. **Ch 45 keeps it and it earns the volume's thesis at the climax; Ch 49 now ends on the posting-desk woman letting Marek in without asking him for anything, which is a different instrument and a quieter one.**
- **Hesper was a cartoon in one line.** He refused to be sorry about the gate three times in two chapters. **The third refusal in Ch 44 and the *"I was not sorry about it and I would do it again tomorrow in exactly the same words"* in Ch 45 are both cut.** Two refusals survive: the one about the four households, which is about who pays and not about remorse, and the cold one on the way out — *I am not going to say I was wrong about the gate and I am not going to let anybody write it down for me either, and if it is in the finding I shall appeal it, and there will not be anything in the finding to appeal.*
