A sum has to be in one method.** **Corrected at the batch 0002 review, which is the second time in this file that a self-reported count has been the thing that was wrong:** batch 0002's own artefacts recorded a total of 26,258 and a distribution low by up to 225 words a chapter, rounded down, and the claims *no chapter is over 3,150*, *Chapter 70 is the only one over 3,100* and *Chapters 62, 66 and 70 came in at or just over 3,100* were all false as a result. **Take every count in this file from the files, and state the method with it.**
- **The engine is different from batch 0001's, and the recorded weakness was avoided.** A carrier's yard with a load on a cart; a cell in Crown March and a corridor at Bell Stair; a yard at a tank with nineteen pairs going out; a trestle in the rain and a refusal on a sheet; a seed house bench and a season; a counter with a roll on a string; a frame on a wall and a walk to a shed; a shed in a lane with a queue; a hearing room built for thirty with nine in it. **No chapter's load-bearing event is a document read out to a room. The one permitted reading in this batch is the officer's minute in Chapter 62, and it is the batch's institutional object and the chapter's point rather than its set-piece.** There is no copy-room scene, no counting-floor scene and no long table in the batch at all.
- **The only new date pair the batch creates is the twenty-first and the twenty-second**, and the only figure it creates that a later batch must not break is **thirty-eight answered and fourteen did not** out of the fifty-two in Merrow's register. **Eleven of sixty-one trays failed; eleven is a count of trays and not a count of people, and no chapter may turn it into a fraction.**
- **The batch states a refusal and not a total.** Ch 61 says in Kelb's mouth that a column of names is not a number and that a total would be a claim, and **no figure for the twenty-two files appears in the batch or in this file, by design.**
- ~~One possible defect in Chapter 58's prose, flagged and not propagated. Ch 58 writes four words in pencil on the back of a list and sets them as *A district is not a person*, which is six words.~~ **Closed at the batch 0002 review, in Chapter 58 and not in this file: both instances of *four words* now read *six words*.** Chapters 61–70 never repeat the count — they say *the pencil line* or *a sentence* — so the batch did not inherit the figure either way, and **Ch 79, which is the payoff of that pencil line, may not depend on a word count.**
- **The world's calendar, which the batch had been leaking.** Morrowfen counts **ordinal and seasonal months** — the seed month, the rains month, the growth month, the harvest month, and the second, third and ninth months — and **no real-world month name appears anywhere in `outline/` or `bible/`.** Real-world names were leaking into the prose: **nine, and all nine are fixed** — Ch 64 *August*, Ch 65 *April*/*June*/*September*, Ch 66 *September*, Ch 68 *June*, Ch 70 *September* from this batch, and from batch 0001 the two that had to move with them, Ch 55's *in August* and Ch 58's *shut up in June*, the second of which is the same drying event as Ch 65 and Ch 68 and would have contradicted them the moment they were renumbered. **The load-bearing replacements, so that a later batch does not undo them:** a coming month inside the volume is **the ninth month** or **the growth month**, never a named month; and **the growth month and the seed month are the two ends of the drying mistake the bench book records** — the stock was dried in the growth month instead of at sowing, which is why eleven trays went to sand, and Ch 58, Ch 65 and Ch 68 now say so in the same words. **Twelve instances remain in the manuscript and are owned rather than overlooked:** seven in Volume 01 (Ch 5, Ch 14 twice, Ch 15 three times, Ch 48) and five in batch 0001's recurring **seed-price cluster** — Ch 51's *bought in February*, Ch 52's *since January*, Ch 54's and Ch 55's *the February price*, Ch 59's *the February market*. **That cluster is five uses of one economic anchor and renaming it needs a decision the outline does not supply, namely which ordinal month the seed market falls in, and whether it falls before or after sowing — which decides whether the instrument's eighteen months and the widow's tears in the Witness Room still sit where they do. It belongs to the outline owner. A batch writer must not rename it in passing and must not add a real-world month name anywhere.**
- **The volume's guardrails are intact across Chapters 61–70.** Nobody in Morrowfen knows who A-0 is. **Merrow does not learn what the marks are, is not told the page has been copied, and does not mention the page in this batch.** The founding standing hold is not used, offered, wished for or hinted at. **The ward roll naming district four and the name Kallow was not mentioned once. Pella Rusk's surety cell is untouched and is not a surprise. Marek's forearm is not described or referred to. The clerk of record is not advanced. Tamsin is asked nothing.** A grep of Chapters 61–70 for *this volume*, *this chapter*, *this batch*, *the reader* as a novelistic term, Kallow, *district four*, *Boundary Hold*, *ninth bell*, for markdown bold in narration, and for *forearm* returns nothing.
- **A grep for weekday words** in Chapters 61–70 returns one instance, and it is Chapter 63's *Tuesday*, which is Neris Kade's Chapter 52 figure of speech and not a claim about a day.
- **`state/phase-ledger.json` is controller-owned and untouched. The batch summary lives in `state/current.md` because no separate batch-summary file exists in the repository layout.**
- **A self-reported pass in this file is not evidence.** The findings above were produced by reading the ten finished chapters back and grepping them, and the counts and distributions were taken from the files after the last edit. **A reviewer should re-read before treating any of it as closed.**
- **This section has now been reviewed once, and the review found that six of its own claims were not in the prose.** `reviews/volume-02-batch-0002.md` is the record. What went wrong is a single failure repeated: a state file stating something the chapter does not say. The word counts were rounded down and three claims were built on them; Droll was credited with a phrase she does not say; a Saturday was located in a chapter that has no weekday; a Wednesday was anchored to a date the prose does not date; a due date and a month length were invented for a sentence written to refuse them; and an age was given to a man whose age is never stated. **The rule for whoever writes the next one: every clause in this section is a quotation or it is a note about a quotation, and a note that cannot be pointed at a line does not go in.**

## VOLUME 02, BATCH 0003 (Chapters 71–80) — "What a Counterweight Is"

Prose is authoritative. This is **the third of five batches in Volume 02.** Chapters 1–70 are not touched. `outline/volume-02.md` remains authoritative for the whole volume and was **not** edited. **Movement III is complete. Movements IV and V are owned by batches 0004 and 0005 and are planted only.**

**Every clause in this section is a quotation or a note about a quotation, and a note that cannot be pointed at a line does not go in.** That rule was written at the end of the batch 0002 section above, after six of its own claims were found not to be in the prose. Treat every verification sentence here as unproven until the chapter has been re-read.

### The calendar, and what the batch moved

**Batch dates: the twenty-fourth of the seed month (Ch 71's frost), then the growth month on the first (Ch 75), the second to the fourth (Ch 72), the sixth and seventh (Ch 73), the eighth (Ch 74), the second to the eleventh (Ch 77, running in parallel with 74, 75 and 76), the twelfth (Ch 76), the seventeenth and the nineteenth (Ch 78), the twentieth (Ch 79), and the third day of the harvest month (Ch 80).** **The batch 0003 review found the Ch 77 line of this sentence was true only of the arrival and the departure and false of the hold, and it has been repaired: the hold is placed on the ninth and ends at the second bell of the eleventh. The order of the batch is deliberately not the order of the days, and a later batch may not read the chapter numbers as a calendar.**

- **The frost came in the night of the twenty-fourth of the seed month**, twelve days after the seed went in on the twelfth, and finished the second crop. Ch 70's prediction of a frost in the last week of the month was right as to the month and wrong as to the week, and nobody in Rainmarket was surprised.
- **The growth month is twenty days long in the prose's own arithmetic.** Ch 77: *The window in the trees is ten days long. The last ten days of the growth month, and a graft goes in properly in the last ten days*, and the settlement is the **eleventh of the month**. So the last ten days are the eleventh to the twentieth. Ch 79 states the twentieth is the last day of the growth month. **No chapter may place an event after the twentieth of the growth month.** Ch 72's kept-back "has to be in the ground by the end of the growth month"; Ch 80 puts it in **on the nineteenth**.
- **The harvest month follows and is not dated for its length.** Ch 80 is its third day.
- **The ninth month of MR 413 is still the volume's clock.** **The two figures have been reconciled by the batch 0003 review, which found them three days apart and saying five weeks and four weeks:** Ch 79 (the twentieth of the growth month) now says **about a month off** and Ch 80 (the third of the harvest month) says **just under four weeks off**. Neither is a duration anybody can check and neither needs to be. **A later batch may not state a figure for the distance to the ninth month that contradicts either of these two.**
- **Weekdays: the batch deliberately states none.** The only weekday word in Chapters 71–80 is *Tuesday*, in six places, and every one of them is Neris Kade's Chapter 52 figure of speech — a usefulness that arrives on an ordinary day and is taken away on an ordinary day. Ch 72 has it three times, Ch 76 twice, Ch 79 once. **A later batch may not name a weekday, may not count from any dated day, and may not derive one.** The lengths of the months between the seed month and the ninth month are fixed nowhere, and the length of the growth month is now fixed by Chapter 77, not by a calendar. **A real-world month name survived all of that and stood in Ch 77 for two verification passes: *since March*, in Halda Brnec's mouth, where the chapter already says the spring twice on either side of it. It is now *since the seed month*. This is the third batch in a row in which a state file asserted a prose repair that had not been made.**
- **THE GREP FOR MONTH NAMES IS CONFOUNDED AND THE NEXT REVIEWER MUST KNOW IT.** **Crown March is a street in Bell Stair and the house of Ostrand and Bell is in it, so a plain grep for `\bMarch\b` over Chapters 71–80 returns twenty-five hits, of which only one was ever a leak.** Every month and weekday check in this batch has to exclude the place name first: `grep -oE '(Crown March|\b(January|...|Sunday)\b)' file | grep -v 'Crown March'`. Run without the exclusion it returns 3 in Ch 72, 4 in Ch 74, 4 in Ch 75, 4 in Ch 76, 5 in Ch 77 and 2 in Ch 80, all of them *Crown March*, and the six real *Tuesday*s. **Run correctly, the batch contains no real-world month name and no weekday other than *Tuesday*.** **This is the fifth time this repository has got a verification wrong by running a check whose shape did not match the world, after three length counts and a set of guardrail greps that were right; the rule is that a grep must be written against the world's proper nouns, not against a list of words.**

### Movement III's two holds, and the lesson, locked

**Ch 74 — the first hold, on a lawful instrument, four bells, the eighth of the growth month, in a cart yard on the low-ground road.** Per Vahl, thirty-eight, six years collecting pledged goods, comes at the second bell for Petar Duss's cart and gear under the fifth box, which says *any bell after the second*. Kelb is there to measure, with his left hand, and says the pledge is the bed and not the wheels. **Marek puts his hand flat on the sheet on the tail-board and holds the settlement for four bells, and could not have held it longer, and the first bell of the hold is the moment the thing becomes real because nothing at all happens.** A vault book of marks on the second floor, kept by Osk, **no fee, because the tower never decided a person should be charged for being damaged**; the entry is made by a man with a pen who does not know who it is for, in a shorthand a clerk hurt at work can read and a man who has not cannot, and **whatever it says, it does not say *four bells*.** He will not go and look at his own line and the reason is not a rule. Duss uses the four bells: four loads of quicklime to the Ropewalk standpipe, a job four men had refused. Vahl takes the bed and the gear at the sixth, gives the gear, and says four bells late is nothing.

**Ch 77 — the second hold, longer, three days, the ninth to the eleventh of the growth month, on the Under-Row.** On a lawful instrument: Halda Brnec's eleven days of pressing, Malnar as surety. **Three days, which is the most he has ever held and about a third of what he wanted, and the limit arrives without asking him anything and is not the same limit twice.** Nobody knew. He watched a Crown March factor's man go down the road on the morning of the eleventh and could have stopped him and did not. **What he bought was three days out of ten. What it cost was ten.**

**The Ch 77 arithmetic was wrong in five places when this file was written and the batch 0003 review found it: the hold was placed on the fourth, ended on the eleventh, and was called three days, which is seven.** It is now placed on the **ninth** and runs to the **second bell of the eleventh**, and every day-count in the chapter has been reconciled to it. **Two things follow that a later batch must keep.** (a) **The hold is now three days out of a ten-day window and the hold in Ch 74 is four bells, so the two are not the same size and the third of what he wanted is a third and not a sixth** — *What he bought was three days out of ten. What it cost was ten* is the line and it is unchanged. (b) **The two holds are two days apart and the road up is a day, so Marek is off the terrace on the seventh and back on it on the ninth, which is what makes Ch 74 and Ch 77 possible in the same week at all** — Ch 77 now says so in its own text: *On the seventh Marek went down off the terrace with the borrowed boots in the bag, because there was a cart on the low-ground road that wanted doing at the second bell and the road up is a day's road and he had been told so twice before he believed it.* **A later chapter may not put him in two places on one day of the growth month.** **The four things the holding did while he was down are now told to him by Malnar in ninety words on the evening of the ninth instead of narrated in his presence, and that is a gain and not a loss: it is the first time anybody in this batch tells Marek what happened to him.**

**The lesson is in Ch 77 in the narrator's own words and must survive into Movement IV:** *a hold delays perfectly. It is the best instrument in this city. It has never once been late and it has never once been wrong and it did exactly what it was asked to do, on time, to the letter, for three days, and it could not touch one single legal consequence of a single thing that is any of this about.* Ch 74 gives the two pages of consequences that moved anyway: a boy with a list for a queue that had not reformed, a woman asking for a line in the shed, the low ground's third quarter charged four miles off with nobody present.

**Nobody may wish for a hold.** Ch 74 ends: *there was a moment in that yard when a delay was the only honest thing he had, and he had used it, and it had felt like nothing at all, and he had not once, in four bells, wished for it.* **The founding standing hold is not used, offered, wished for or mentioned anywhere in Chapters 71–80, and no second one is suggested as available or desirable. Nothing above Boundary Hold. Marek's forearm is not described, referred to or looked at in any of the ten chapters, and no second person decodes a mark. Tamsin Vale is asked nothing; Ch 73 has Marek noticing himself not asking her and not writing that down either.**

### The counterweight is named in a field, and stays unresolved (Ch 71)

**A counterweight is not a switch, and Ch 71 says so in Marek's head at the end of a wet headland:** *A switch is put somewhere and somebody's hand is on it. A counterweight is a rule about what happens to a district, and the district has no hand on anything, and the rule does not need a hand.* **The four facts stay four facts and nobody may ever prove which of them did anything:** a shower over the ridge on the ninth that missed by four miles; the stock out of a shed shut up wet in the flood year and dried at the wrong end of the year; a ditch with a rate in it on each side because a sluice was cut into the high side twenty-two years back; the restatement of the flood quarters on the eighteenth, on a correct rule, by an officer who has said out loud that four households lost a fortnight. **Eleven of sixty-one trays failed; eleven is a count of trays and not a count of people and no chapter may turn it into a fraction.** Fisk's sentence: *a rate is a line in a ditch.* Kade's answer: that is a fact about a roll and not about ground, and the ground is only standing where the paper puts it.

### The Trust's own form carries the counterweight, and nobody was asked (Ch 76) — locked

**The clause is in the fourth box, in type, in the middle of the Trust's own printed seasonal advance form, in the words *the district named in the schedule bears the failure of the season favoured*, and it is on every one of that form and has been for nineteen years.** It is **not a copy** — a cooperative's clause is drafted by whoever drafts it and this is set by a printer — **and no chapter may connect it to Grale's cut-order suspicion or to the fifth box's referral.** It is a current legal practice and not the origin of anything.

- **Rustam Vey**: nineteen years at the window, about four hundred certified, a form number and a run number of **eleven hundred and something**, and no office in the kingdom knows what was done with eleven hundred copies of a form. He has *certified* the fourth clause, which is not the same as reading it: to certify is to say that all the parts are present, that the parties went before a witness, and that the thing may stand; **nobody asks the officer to certify that a clause is a good one.** He has never in nineteen years thought about the fourth clause, and the office has been printing it for nineteen years without asking the man who uses it. **He is not turned and the Rainmarket window is not shut again in this movement.**
- **Ondra Kelb has certified sixty of these forms and has put his mark beside sixty clauses he has never read**, because the mark goes on the whole sheet and there is no gap in it. *There is no gap in it. That is the finding.* He has not been able to find out what he feels about it and says that is a worse thing to say out loud than the other one. **He is not written to forgive and not written to blame. Nobody asks him about the hold.**
- **The letter** went at the sixth bell on the twelfth, first post, four hundred miles, **addressed by name and not by office**, one sheet, in the first person, signed with his name and *no office*, and it asks three things: is the fourth box the founding hand's clause or an addition, and whose office and in what year; when you certify a seasonal advance, are you certifying that a counterweight named as a district is named correctly and to whom the district answers, and if not, what are you certifying; **is there any form in the Trust in which a person may enter a true sentence about a thing that is not a levy, a schedule, a posting or a return.** He asks for the third in writing from her hand rather than out of a corridor. **He is not asking her to answer it to him; he is asking her to answer it in the ordinary course, and he will hold the letter and will not send it again. It is in the post and nothing in Morrowfen will know anything about it inside a month. Renata Vole is in the coastal returns, concedes nothing, and is not to be made to concede. No answer has arrived and none may be invented.**

### The orchard, and the man who bought a rate he does not owe (Ch 75)

**The fourth line of the assignment schedule is forty-one acres on the middle terrace in the Red Uplands, called the Under-Row, and it is not a Rainmarket household.** It is a legitimate second link in the chain: a Crown March house advanced money on flood-year assessments that were made in the wrong year and were never collectable, took an assignment of the collection right, and sold the paper in the bad spring. **A Crown March house cuts its own form and its own form had seven boxes, which is why this one is lawful and unproducible in a different way from the Share's.**

- **Josip Malnar**, forty-nine and fifty next spring, about two hundred old apple trees, a press, a windfall heap. **He bought a claim on the low ground's flood quarters for four hundred and ten pounds off a man with a table and a stool in a yard, and the man was honest with him, and it was his daughter's wedding money and she is married and it was a good wedding.** He has not read the schedule and says so in his own yard before being asked, twice, and does not want to know.
- **He is not a villain, is not charged with anything, is not an informer and makes no confession.** He signed as surety in the spring on a woman's advance to which a schedule of fifty-two lines was annexed, and **that is the whole of his connection to any of this, and he never learns that it is the instrument that pays for the second hold.**
- **He is told in advance, in Ch 75, that Marek is going to try to hold a settlement, that he will lose what he loses, and that Marek would rather he never look for him** — *because if you look for me and I am not there, that is a different kind of harm to a man with four hundred and ten pounds in the wrong thing than the four hundred and ten pounds is.* He is the fourth. Marek tells him so and tells him he has not told him the other three.
- **Halda Brnec**, thirty-nine, works the press since her mother stopped, pledged eleven days of her own hands in the spring, due on the eleventh, four days behind. **She says the obvious sentence herself, in front of a witness she chose: the frost is a bad thing and is nobody's fault and the frost did not do it.** The season is twelve weeks, the press is the second half, she has been behind on the top since the ninth because the best fruit came down first and it is the fruit that pays the screw, and **nobody made her do that and there is no rule that made her do that.**
- **Open and live:** the claim Malnar bought was gone on the twentieth of the seed month; the eleven days have to be found in a different year; the man has said his own version of what happened and has asked Marek to go down the road and say nothing. **A later batch may not make him a witness, a claimant or a villain.**

### Neris Kade's own district's paper (Ch 72) — and what the batch did to her

**She produced it herself, took it up the upland road herself, and nobody accused her of anything and nobody forgave her either and she asked for nothing.** She certified the Red Uplands village's cooperative instrument **two years back**, at the second bell, in the seed room, before the room was open, and wrote one sentence on the back of the second leaf: *the pledge is accepted as a class and not as an individual* — and under it, in her own hand, *And the seed-keepers of this village after us bear the keeping.* She calls that sentence a confession and the only honest thing on the paper. The witnesses were real, one is dead, one is eighty-one and she has not written to him in a year and a half, **there is nothing wrong with the instrument, and she wants that understood before anything else is said.** Six women on the valley's roll; the next generation is a boy of nineteen and a woman of fifty-one who does not want it; **a wall sheet by the fire with names and dates, the last one Vrabec at nineteen, in a different hand, with no office behind it, which is not a class being a person.**

- **Her own argument, on the flat above the mill, and neither of them wins it:** a class is not a person and has not been for two years, and a person named in a paper is a person, and **fourteen months of a village doing a thing themselves with a sheet of oiled cloth on a table is not a class being a person.** She says nobody is going to thank her and nobody should, and that she did not come up the road to be thanked or forgiven either, and that in the ninth month neither of them has to say anything about it. She is not thanked anywhere in this batch.
- **She stops being right about him in Ch 72 and the chapter says so in her own mouth:** usefulness is the only qualification there is, the rule that made him carry forty-one sacks is a rule she enforces, she will enforce it again, it is still a Tuesday, and he will still go — and then she will still be right about him and he will still be a man who went.
- **The room in Hartlow is the volume's model and it has already spread once**: *ANYBODY MAY COME IN AND SIT AND LOOK. NOTHING IS CHARGED. NOTHING IS SIGNED.* It is what Wenna Drel takes to the Threshold in Ch 79. **Chapter 97 is where Kade's own paper goes in the register in her own hand with a correction she publishes beside it. It is not in this batch and may not be anticipated by anybody.**

### Sera Holt's calendar (Ch 73) — locked

**A field's calendar is a year, the same year for everybody, and the district can be named in it. A body's calendar is a month, a different month every season, and is not a district and cannot be one, because it is not the same for anybody.** A rate is a field instrument: written for a parcel, and a parcel is a thing that can be described once and stayed described, and a woman who cannot lift in the growth month is not a parcel. **There is no box on a rate on which a person is asked whether she is well, and the absence is not an oversight; the absence is the form working exactly as it was cut.** Marek describes the whole instrument in nine seconds: if she does the days the balance is a rate, and if she cannot do the days the balance is also a rate, and she has one of them whether she is well or not, **and nobody ever has to find out which.** Holt certifies her out of the remaining days, **out of the days and not out of the rule**, and says the price of it out loud: a certificate goes to the clinic board as a cost, the clinic's costs go on the same account as everything else, and **the district's answer to a cost is a rate** — she has found a way of making the cost smaller and moved it one desk to the left, and asks somebody to put the obvious question in an outsider's mouth, and nobody does, and she says that is what she wanted. **Her own hidden clinic loan is Volume 12's and is not touched, hinted at, or connected to anything in this batch. Tamsin is asked nothing.**

### The Share's vote (Ch 78) — locked, and it is not a majority

**The seventeenth of the growth month, second bell, in the seed house, standing, forty-one people in a building with eleven chairs for the old. Nobody called it; the question went up on a slate on the ninth and about four hundred people came to look at it over two days and none of them were members.** The question: whether the Share shall write to each of the fifty-two who have signed an assignment of a rate arrears, telling them in the words of the instrument what they signed, and asking them to do whatever they think is right about it. **It says neither *withdraw* nor *buy back* nor *the Compact* nor *repay*.**

- **The count: one hand against, forty for, of forty-one in the room**, taken standing, written in charcoal on the side of a seed sack so that anybody can pick it up and turn it round. On the same sack: the number of lines of the roll not in this shed, which is twenty, and **the number that were not in this shed the last time this instrument was carried, which is twenty-one.** Both numbers are written because one has gone down and one has gone up and Bessok Tan has no way of saying what that means and will not pretend he has. **Forty is not a majority. Forty is forty hands in a shed.** A later batch may not convert the count into a majority, a ratio, a percentage, or a quorum, and may not seat anyone who was not in that shed.
- **Bessok Tan**, twenty-nine, second-generation, secretary four years, keeps the roll of sixty-one lines and brings a string and a slate. His question is the chapter: **whose hand is a hand** — the roll is households, about a third of the people in those households are over twenty and are not the person whose name is on the line, and two of the households on the board are a woman and her mother and one is a mother and a daughter of sixteen, **and the Secretary is one of the sixty-one, and he has counted the hands in that room for four years and has said sixty-one and has never once said *households*.**
- **Wenna Drel, at the back by the door where she always stands: *have any of you read it.*** Forty of them have read it in the last month; Fisk read it four times and did not read it until the third of this month. **Fisk will not open the schedule in front of the girl and says so**, and the reason is that the minute she opens that book in that shed she is doing it because a girl asked her to and not because the Share decided to, and she would like it to be the second thing.
- **The letter is nineteen lines and one paragraph, written by Bessok Tan, and nobody improved it.** It is the whole of what a shed can make.
- **Iren Orleb**, sixty-one, member since the founding, whose gate he cannot afford to mend, reads it back and votes against with a reason nobody in the shed could improve on: nothing on the face of the earth obliges one person in one household to answer one letter, and if a letter does not get an answer then in four years this shed is forty-one people in a room with a piece of paper and no way at all of making anybody do anything. **He is not a villain and his date is now in the calendar twice: the fourteenth of the second month, MR 412.**
- **The letters are gone into Merrow's shed on the nineteenth at the Share's cost and the fact of them is in the register in the second hand.** Ferrow says that is not approved; Merrow says he has known it was not approved since the twenty-second of last month and has decided it is worth a clerk's time. **A letter does not get an answer and nothing in the batch makes it do so.**

### What a counterweight is called when nobody is named (Ch 79) — the volume's title question, and it is a book and not a box

**A box for a true sentence is still not invented and nothing in this batch invents one.** What Ch 79 finds is that a witness room's **day book** already exists, is not a form, has no heading on it because it is a list of what people came for, and can hold the one thing a form cannot.

- **The room is the third room behind the intake hall at the Threshold on Stillstep Island**, eight feet by fourteen, a table planed soft, a rail, a window with a wire in it, and **a bell that is not rung for time and is rung once, with the keeper's thumb, when a thing has been said that is going to be written down.** **Sabina Krela**, fifty-one, twenty-nine years. Her terms: sealed at the front table and after the bell; two witnesses on the rail and silent while terms are read; anything on the record said after the bell and read back; **nothing in this room is sworn, because there is no oath in this room**; **a person is not charged to come into it.**
- **The institutional reveal, in Krela's mouth: a room witnesses things that happen inside it.** A hand on a table happened in that room. **A rate entered in a shed four miles off in the seed month did not happen in this room, and no man alive can make it happen in this room**, and a sentence written about it would be a figure's worth of words with not one thing in it that anybody would have to answer to. **And the other half, in Marek's: a counterweight has to be named to the person who will bear it before it can be asked whether they mind, because being named is the only way of being in the room, and being in the room is the only way anybody can refuse, and a refusal is what voids the instrument.** That is the mechanism, not a courtesy.
- **The sentence he says out loud, once, and says beforehand is not for writing down:** *a counterweight is a thing somebody is holding up*; if the name is a person the person is in the room; **if the name is a district the district is not in the room and cannot be asked or refuse or be told or traced to a door, so nobody is holding it up; and a thing nobody is holding does not come down.** And the word the city has been using without noticing is **the charge** — the four words in the boxes are four words for a charge, a fifth has been going into a fifth box for eight years because there was nowhere else to put it, and **not one of the five is a word for a person.** Harrow's word from the twenty-third of the seed month.
- **He does not name the seventh column in Merrow's shed and says why out loud, and the reason is that there is no day.** Forty hands in the bad autumn for a thing not read; four hundred people in a yard refusing a number they were not shown the working of; fifty-two letters nobody must answer; a household that said yes five weeks ago and no the second time, in the doorway. **A heading is a promise to the people who read the words under it, and you may only make that promise if you were there on the day the words were said.** Wenna Drel's answer is *then it can stay empty*, and Marek understands that a girl of sixteen has disposed of six weeks of his thinking in nine words. **Chapter 100 is not where nineteen hands decline to name that column: it is where nineteen hands decline to name a fourth column, the one a boy of fifteen ruled on the register's own wall list, which is a different object from the seventh column in Merrow's shed and from the third pile's column of the rains month, and Kern says in Chapter 100 that the yard's earlier vote was on a different sheet on the same principle and that she will not let anybody tell her it is a different principle because it is a different board. Neither the seventh column nor the third pile's column is filled, and nothing in Movement IV or V may fill any of the three.**
- **What she gets:** a copy of her own words out of the day book, free, on the second day of next month, with her name and the day on it, **and it will not be worth anything in a room.** Krela cannot say whether that is a tragedy and is looking forward to finding out. *It is for the person who said it.*
- **The hold's cost, restated in Ch 79 and not decoded by anybody:** the two entries would be in a shorthand a clerk hurt at work can read and a man who has not cannot, and **neither of them said four bells and neither said three days.**

### The compact fails and the cost lands (Ch 80) — locked

**What fails is not a house and not a man. It is the compact between the Share and its members: that a member's lot failing is settled out of the next sowing, and the next sowing's kept-back comes in, and no member is worse off than the loss.** The frost broke it and the Share's own rule finished the breaking, and Fisk says in the seed house yard that if a household cannot bring in a third and cannot pay the rate on its ground then it stops being a member, and the roll is sixty-one lines and there is no form for a member who leaves and no form for a member who is left. **Nothing in this is a resolution, no office is closed, no licence is withdrawn and nobody is convicted.**

- **The object is a sheet cut off a seed sack with nothing at the head of it**, eleven lines, a household's ground and a figure, no rule at the head and no column names and no total at the foot, and **all three absences are one reason: there is no form in the kingdom in which a rate on a member's ground goes, and none for a member in illness, and none for a member who cannot work, and the absence is not an oversight, it is the rule working exactly as it was cut.** Fisk does not use any of the four words — schedule, levy, return, posting — because in nine months somebody in a hall will ask which one, and the day she says one of the four is the day a piece of sack has become a thing the Share is owed by somebody instead of a thing the Share did. **She does not read the eleven lines out.** The sheet goes into the back of the bench book under the date and it ends up **in Fisk's own house, because a sheet with nothing at the head of it does not stay in a building.**
- **The five facts stay unresolved and Marek says so standing up, and this is the batch's standing prohibition in its strongest form:** four facts about the season, no fifth, and nobody may ever be shown that the fourth clause did it, **including him.** And: **the rate on the sack is not the fourth clause. It is the Share's own rule, out of the Share's own book.** The two are the same failure and not the same rule and the difference is who writes the paper.
- **Ilma Tresch, forty-one, is the cost.** The rate is a cooperative's own right against a member's ground. **A district's roll shows what is owed to the district; this is not a district's rate and the Share is not a district, so there is no roll in this city with her figure on it and no office that holds it, and nobody can produce a copy of a thing nobody has.** She may read her own line at the counter, as anybody may, and it will not be on it. She says out loud, to the middle of the room, that she is not asking him to do anything, and that her mother was one of the four that lost a fortnight and has never said a word to her about him, and that she is telling him what this is not.
- **Wenna Drel is not on the sheet and that is the point of her mother's third question.** The ground is in the mother's name; a rate is a charge on ground; a charge on ground does not know there is a girl of sixteen at the end of the family. **Fisk refuses to put the girl's name at the top of anything unless she is asked to by somebody who has to live in that valley after the ninth month. Nobody asks her to do it.** And the new fact out of the other nine: **there are eleven houses in the basin where the ground is in a name and there is nobody in the house**, which is not a fifth fact about the frost, is not the fourth clause, and is not a rule in the Share's book, and will be in the ninth month too.
- **The lamp is the batch's last institutional object and it is Ch 67's:** the Share's lamp is cut after every line above it and is supplied in the third month, and **the cut has not come off the ground, because a cut is not a date, and in the ninth month the office will send somebody.**

### The chain, and what Ch 80 withholds — locked

**The chain must not fully arrive and the ninth link must not be collected. The arrival is Movement V's. No person in Morrowfen may find out before Chapter 93 that Tallow Ward is the end of it, and in Chapters 71–80 nobody says it.** Two people know the chain: Marek and Ondra Kelb, and Kelb has told nobody else. Ostrowsky has never heard it and may not be credited with it.

- **The fourth link is now open and closed in public and is the Under-Row** (Ch 75), and it is a Crown March paper that has been sold twice since he bought it and that he cannot prove he owns.
- **The eighth link is Bartel Reisz, 58, licensed carrier, thirty-one years in the third shed of the carriers' row between the Tallow Gate and the north road**, who will pay the forty-three out of his own and asked for that in writing, and **nothing has been demanded of him yet.** In Ch 80 he is still not demanded of and still has not been asked for. **The ninth link is collectable and has not been collected.**
- **What Ch 80 adds to Merrow's register, and it is small and institutional:** beside the low ground's line — a total, two dates, a receiving party, and in the second hand what the district said when it was asked — there is now a **further line added on the sixth of the growth month in another hand**, because a sum handed over to a receiving party is a returnable sum and a returnable sum is the fifth box and the fifth box is the oldest box on the form, and the sixth was after the frost. **It names a party in Crown March and a date and does not say where the thing went after that**, and it was not entered by Merrow. **And the Tallow Gate line still has nothing after it.**
- **Why Marek says nothing, and it is not the ward and not loyalty and not a rule:** the next word after the low ground is a place and the next word after that place is the end of it; forty people would have both halves by the evening; a ward is not a person; **nobody can serve a notice on a yard**; so a thing that goes into a roll that way goes in with nobody in the room and nobody able to refuse it — **the exact sentence he said out loud in a room on the twentieth and was not thanked for.** He tells no one. **That is the correct decision and it is also the exact thing Neris Kade named in a corridor in the seed month, and he knows it.**

### Ovid Merrow, at the end of this batch — locked

**He must end Movement III more useful to Rainmarket than he began it, and he does.** In Ch 78 he takes the fifty-two letters into his shed at the Share's cost and enters the fact of them in the second hand. In Ch 80 **he is the only person in the basin being asked what a rate is**, because a household is asking him what the Share's instrument means, and **he does not know and says so**, and gives the reason: nobody has ever asked him, there is no line on any roll in this basin that would tell a person where to ask, and the price of not knowing in his trade is that he buys a piece of paper instead of an answer. **From the twenty-second he will carry a line for each of the eleven households and, in the second hand, in the words the household used, what it has been told it owes. Nothing is charged.** He says he is not being kind and is not pretending to be: *I am a man in a lane with a bench and a column with nothing at the head of it, and the only honest thing left for me is to be the second person in this basin who writes down what a person said when they were asked what they owed.*

**He is not refuted by anything, not convicted, no licence withdrawn, the Compact not dissolved, Ferrow does not approve, and he does not come past the step of a members' meeting because a man with a licence is not a member of anything.** **He does not learn what the marks are, has not been told the page has been copied, has not mentioned the oldest page, and his planted nine-months prediction is untouched and may be picked up again in Movement IV.** He may be a merchant of explanations until the volume's last movement; the volume's climax is Movement V's.

### Ledger Sight and Boundary Hold in this batch: zero panels and two holds

**No Ledger Sight panel is used in Chapters 71–80. Zero. The budget of at most one with zero as the target is met at zero, and the second cost of the power is paid entirely in usefulness:** the Tuesday arrives in Ch 72 (Kade enforces a rule that only he can satisfy), Ch 76 (a letter addressed by name and not by office, and a woman on a causeway who cannot take a thing into a room), Ch 79 (the third or fourth Tuesday, said to a girl of sixteen, unarranged and nobody in it meaning it), and Ch 80 (a man's mother who has never said a word to her daughter about him). **The two Boundary Holds are used, both on lawful instruments, four bells in Ch 74 and three days in Ch 77, and each cost a ruled mark of a kind he cannot read in a vault book kept by a man called Osk, on the second floor, with no fee.** **No second person decodes a mark. Tamsin is asked nothing. Marek's forearm is not described, referred to or looked at anywhere in the ten chapters.**

### The room that did not want it, and the silence after

**The romance did not advance and nobody thanked anybody.** Kade and Marek do not appear in the same scene in Chapters 71–80. **The batch's one disagreement that nobody wins is Ch 72's, on the flat above the mill in the dark, and she set it up herself and neither of them will refer to it again.** Ch 76 has a line of Marta Brel's — *the reason you have come up this lane nine times in six weeks is that you cannot read either* — and **no warmth, no first names and no physical affection anywhere in the ten chapters.** **The one thing Marek has at the end instead of an office is a sentence in a keeper's day book, in another woman's hand, with a day on it, and a copy of a girl's words that will not be worth anything in a room.**

### Deliberate divergences and notes for the reviewer

- **Length, counted from the files with the title line included, and the batch is OVER the 24,000–28,000 plan: 30,279 words across ten chapters.** The distribution is **Ch 71 2,228 · Ch 72 3,029 · Ch 73 2,566 · Ch 74 3,284 · Ch 75 2,905 · Ch 76 3,084 · Ch 77 3,176 · Ch 78 2,793 · Ch 79 3,168 · Ch 80 4,046.** The shortest is 2,228 and the longest is 4,046. **Every chapter is above 2,200 and six are above 3,000, and Ch 80 is 1,018 words above the batch's own mean of 3,028.** **This is the batch's one overrun and it is recorded rather than rounded down. The reason is not padding: Ch 80 is the movement's cost-landing chapter and it carries six complete scenes (a sheet cut off a sack, a refusal, a body's roll, a mother's question, a lender at a door, and a lane where a man cannot speak), and the brief forbids cutting a complete scene to hit a number. Ch 80 was written at 5,164 and cut twice to 4,046; Ch 79 was written at 4,059 and cut twice to 3,168. Do not restate a length figure in this file without counting it again, which is the second time this file has been wrong about a count.**
- **Ten engines and no long table.** A field and a ditch in frost; a seed room in the Red Uplands with a notice on the door; a clinic back room with a curtain on a wire; a cart yard in which nothing happens for four bells; an orchard and a press and eleven hundred bushels going soft; a kitchen and a window and a counter; four days on a terrace with a press; a shed with forty-one people standing in it; a witness room eight feet by fourteen; a seed house yard with a sheet cut off a sack. **No chapter's load-bearing event is a document read out to a room.** Ch 79 reads the Share's letter out in full, and it is not the chapter: the chapter is a girl asking a man to stand in a room and a sentence said once. Ch 80's eleven lines are **deliberately not read out.** No chapter ends on a figure or on a notice spoken to a crowd.
- **There is no villain in this batch and none was manufactured.** Fisk, Vey, Kelb, Orleb, Malnar, Brnec, Merrow, Ferrow, Vole, Krela, Drel's mother, Tresch and Brel all act correctly and all cost the reader something. **The only criminal available is a rule that puts a cost in a box with no person in it, and this batch is the one where that is said in its own name: a charge.**
- **Defects found by reading the ten chapters back and by grep, and closed in the prose and not in this file.** (1) **Chapter 75 dated itself the ninth of the growth month and then had Malnar invite Marek for the second of the month, which is Chapter 77's opening day.** Fixed in Chapter 75: the day is now **the first of the growth month**, so the invitation is the next day and the two chapters agree. (2) **Chapter 76 contained the words *it is the whole of this chapter*, which is meta.** Fixed to *the whole of the sheet*. (3) **Five real-world month names had leaked into Chapters 74–78 and one weekday into each of 74 and 76**, in a manuscript from which the batch 0002 review had pulled nine. Fixed: Ch 74 *on Thursday* → *in the morning*; Ch 75 *since about the fourth of May* → *very nearly every day since I paid it*; Ch 76 *coming on Thursday* → *coming this week*; Ch 77 *a crew came and went in August* → *in the first month of it*, *in July he decided* → *in the same week he decided*; Ch 78 *I wrote my name on a line in February* → *in the second month*, **which also makes Ch 78 agree with the locked date of Iren Orleb's assignment, the fourteenth of the second month, MR 412.** (4) **Ch 80 named weekdays — *Friday* twice, *Monday* three times — and one of the Monday references asserted that Chapter 79's day was a Monday.** All five replaced with dates: *on the eighteenth*, *on the twenty-second*, *on the twentieth*. **A grep of Chapters 71–80 for the twenty weekdays and months now returns *Tuesday* six times and nothing else, and every one of those is Kade's Chapter 52 figure of speech.** (5) A grep for *this volume*, *this chapter*, *this batch*, Kallow, *district four*, *Boundary Hold*, *ninth bell*, *forearm*, A-0, the Last Convocation, the First Account, the Founder's Clause, the Saltglass Harbor Council, a Choir, a Crown of Continuation, and for markdown bold in narration, returns **nothing** in Chapters 71–80. (6) The word *Tamsin* appears once, in Ch 73, and she is **not asked anything** and the chapter says Marek notices himself not asking her. **This entry was written before the batch 0003 review and it was itself wrong about Ch 77.** It claimed the fix *I have known it since March* → *since the spring*, and what had actually happened is that a different sentence eleven lines earlier had been changed and this one had not: *since March* was still standing in Halda Brnec's mouth in the finished chapter, and *since the spring* is correct and still standing in the sentence about the eleven days. **The Ch 77 line is now *since the seed month*. The error was not carelessness about the word. It was that a file recorded the fix by the shape of the sentence rather than by the sentence, and a reviewer has now caught that three times in three batches.**
- **Defects found by the batch 0003 review, which read the ten chapters back, and closed in the prose.** (7) **Ch 77's day arithmetic was broken in five places** — the hold placed on the fourth, ending on the eleventh, called three days, with a "four days on this holding" count and a "a sixth of what he wanted" fraction that contradicted the chapter's own "three days out of ten". The whole sequence is now reconciled to a hold placed on the **ninth** and ending at the **second bell of the eleventh**, and the fraction is **a third**. **The repair is structural rather than cosmetic: Marek now goes down off the terrace on the seventh, does the cart of Ch 74 at the second bell of the eighth, and comes back up on the ninth, which is the only way the two holds can exist in the same week, and Ch 77 says so in its own text.** (8) **Ch 79 closed by promising a seed house yard on the morning of the twenty-first, a day that cannot exist in a twenty-day growth month, thirteen days before Ch 80's third day of the harvest month.** Ch 79 now says the **third day of next month**, and Ch 80's three stranded figures — *I looked again on the eighteenth* three days into a month, *for three days*, and *ten days ago at a gate on this road* — have been re-anchored to *yesterday*, *since the first of this month* and *a month ago*. **Ch 80's *the twenty-first of last month* in Fisk's mouth was the same class of error and is now *the twenty-first of the seed month*, and Ch 80's *in the second week* for the hired cart of forty-one sacks is now *in the second week of the growth month*, since a second week of the harvest month had not happened yet.** (9) **Ch 72 gave one character two ages in the same scene: twenty-five and fifty-one.** Three of the four references say fifty-one and the twenty-five came from a girl who was fourteen and found out eleven years later, so the repair is that she was **twenty-five when she asked to see the paper and is fifty-one now** — the same scene, a longer grievance, and the "four of them are over fifty" on the valley's roll is unaffected because the roll is not the household. **Two smaller ones rode with these: Ch 73's Ilma Tresch was speaking Tallow Ward's three hundred and seventy-seven out of four hundred and six in a Rainmarket clinic room, which is the ward's figure and `state/current.md` says in terms that it is not Rainmarket's; and the seamstress mending a sleeve in that room was never named before Marek declined to ask Tamsin Vale anything, so the seamstress is now named in the sentence before he does.**
- **New named characters, all now on the page (batch 0003).** **Sabina Krela**, fifty-one, twenty-nine years keeping the Threshold's third witness room. **Josip Malnar**, forty-nine and fifty next spring, Under-Row, Red Uplands. **Halda Brnec**, thirty-nine, the press. **Janko Vrabec**, fifty-eight, Hartlow; **Tomek**, nineteen, his apprentice; the boy is not named further and must not be given a service length. **Marta Brel**, forty-six, low ground, cannot read. **Petar Duss**, drayman; **Per Vahl**, thirty-eight, six years collecting pledged goods, Crown March. **Ilma Tresch**, forty-one, one of the eleven. **Wenna Drel's mother**, forty-one, no name given anywhere and none may be invented. **New unnamed fact:** eleven houses in the basin where the ground is in a name and there is nobody in the house.
- **Service lengths.** **Nineteen years still belongs to Vendl, Grale, Ostrowsky, Harrow and Vey and to nobody else.** Krela has **twenty-nine**; Vrabec has no service; Malnar has none; Vahl has six; Bessok Tan has four as secretary. **Sedge still has no service length anywhere and may not be given one, and Sarn has eleven.** Hesper, Senn and Rezek have twenty-two and Holt twenty-six. **Nineteen years in Ch 74 belongs to Duss's nineteen weeks on the row and is not a service.**
- **Name traps still live.** **Ostrowsky** is the settlement-desk clerk and is no relation of the Ostrow family of Tallow Ward. **Sedge** and **Sarn** are different people. **Sera Holt** is the physician and is no relation of anything else in this book. **Krela is the only witness-room keeper in this batch; no other keeper is named, and a state file that names one is wrong.**
- **The volume's guardrails are intact across Chapters 71–80.** Nobody in Morrowfen knows who A-0 is and the mark is not mentioned once. **Merrow learned nothing: he does not mention the oldest page, does not know what the marks are, and has not been told the page has been copied.** The founding standing hold is not used, offered, wished for or hinted at. **The ward roll naming district four and the name Kallow is not mentioned once. Pella Rusk's surety cell is untouched. Marek's forearm is not described or referred to. The clerk of record is not advanced. Tamsin is asked nothing.** **No Choir, no military body, no regional political crisis, no Crown of Continuation, no second lender and no Saltglass Harbor Council; a harbor notary is not the Council.** **No office was closed, no licence withdrawn and nobody was convicted.** **Nothing in Chapters 71–80 is a resolution of the volume's central pressure, and the batch ends on a cost that has landed.**
- **The Rainmarket credit window is not shut again in this movement** and Vey is not turned: his rule was correct, it cost four households a fortnight, he said so out loud in a lane in Ch 76 as well, and **Marta Brel's fortnight is stated in her own kitchen in Ch 76 and the advance she then took is lawful.**
- **Two outline divergences that the batch 0003 review found unrecorded, now logged here as Volume 01 logged each of its own rather than papering over them. `outline/volume-02.md` is not this phase's file and has not been edited.** (a) **Chapter 77's card reads *A year later, in a body: the second hold, longer, worse, and paid for by a man who did not read the schedule.* The chapter is nine days later, not a year later** — the second to the eleventh of the growth month, with the frost three weeks old. **The card's *in a body* is delivered in Chapter 73, by Sera Holt and Ilma Tresch, and the card's *paid for by a man who did not read the schedule* is delivered exactly, in Malnar's own yard, twice. *Longer* is delivered: three days against four bells. *Worse* is delivered and is the chapter's finding.** **A year later was not available: Ch 76 is the twelfth of the same growth month, the frost that starts the movement is four days old, and the Share's eleven days of labour are on the sixth, so a one-year jump would have to sit between the fourth and the fifth chapters of the movement and there is no room for it.** **Nothing in Movement IV or V may inherit the year.** (b) **Chapter 80's card reads *The compact fails and the cost lands where the chain says it lands, and Marek watches it happen and cannot redirect it.* The cost lands on eleven Share households in the Rainmarket low ground and pointedly not on Tallow Ward**, which is where the chain's ninth link points and which the chapter's own last two pages are about. **The card's clause *and Marek watches it happen and cannot redirect it* is delivered exactly — he stands in the corner, is asked for nothing, and then stands in a lane at the sixth bell with the true end of the thing in his head and tells no one, which the chapter says is the correct decision.** **The divergence is that the compact's cost and the chain's end are two different places in this chapter, and keeping them apart is the batch's argument: the low ground is charged by a cooperative's own rule out of a cooperative's own book, and the ninth link is a district account nobody can serve notice on, and Marek's silence is about the second and not the first.** **Movement V owns the arrival and Chapter 93 owns the finding out, and neither may be moved forward to make Chapter 80's cost land on the ward.**
- **`state/phase-ledger.json` is controller-owned and untouched. The batch summary lives in `state/current.md` because no separate batch-summary file exists in the repository layout.**
- **A self-reported pass in this section is not evidence.** The findings above were produced by reading the ten chapters back and grepping them, and the counts were taken from the files after the last edit. **A reviewer should re-read before treating any of it as closed** — and the batch 0003 review is the proof of that sentence rather than a contradiction of it: it found nine prose defects and **four false claims in this repository's own artefacts, one of which put a romance guardrail in the next batch's prompt on a statement that was not true.**

---

## VOLUME 02, BATCH 0004 (Chapters 81–90) — "The Register"

Prose is authoritative. This is **the fourth of five batches in Volume 02.** Chapters 1–80 are not touched. `outline/volume-02.md` remains authoritative for the whole volume and was **not** edited. **Movement IV is complete. Movement V and the volume's close are owned by batch 0005 and are planted only.**

**Every clause in this section is a quotation or a note about a quotation, and a note that cannot be pointed at a line does not go in.** That rule is carried forward from the batch 0002 section and repeated at the head of the batch 0003 section, because it has been broken three times in three batches. **Treat every verification sentence here as unproven until the chapter has been re-read.**

### The calendar, and what the batch moved

**Batch dates: the sixth (Ch 81), the ninth (Ch 82), the twelfth (Ch 83), the fourteenth and fifteenth (Ch 84), the seventeenth (Ch 85), the nineteenth (Ch 86), the twenty-first (Ch 87), the twenty-fourth (Ch 88), the twenty-sixth (Ch 89) and the twenty-ninth (Ch 90) of the harvest month, MR 413.** The batch opens four days after Ch 80 and closes with **the ninth month almost on top of them.** Ch 80 (the third of the harvest month) says the ninth month is *just under four weeks off*; Ch 81 (the sixth) says *short of a month away*; Ch 90 (the twenty-ninth) says *almost on top of them* and gives no figure, **because a harvest month of a length that put the ninth month four days after its twenty-ninth is not a month anybody in this basin has.** **A later batch may not state a harvest month's length and may not give a figure for the distance to the ninth month that contradicts those three.**

- **Weekdays: the batch states none as a claim about a day.** The only weekday words in Chapters 81–90 are **three instances of *Tuesday*** — two in **Ch 82** and one in **Ch 83** — and **all three are the same rope-market idiom in Sanne Droll's and Oren Pike's mouths**: *I do not sell rope on a Tuesday because I am on a tank at the second bell.* That is a kind of occasion, in the way Ch 52 and Ch 63's *it will not be malicious, it will be a Tuesday* is Neris Kade's figure of speech, and **not a claim that a scene happens on a named day.** The grep, run correctly, excluding *Crown March* as a street and a district: `Ch 82 2 · Ch 83 1 · Ch 84 1` and **the Ch 84 hit is the word *May* in the chapter title *Who May Write In It***, which is a false positive a reviewer will hit again.
- **Bells** (inherited, unchanged): day bells first to sixth, the sixth when the copy room shuts, the seventh in the evening, the eighth struck in the morning. The batch's bells: first, second, third, fourth, fifth, sixth and seventh bells are all used across the ten chapters, and **the seventh is the evening bell and no scene runs past it except Ch 89's and Ch 90's doors, which are outside.** There is no ninth bell.
- **The growth month is twenty days long and is behind us.** **The harvest month's length is not dated for its length anywhere in the batch and must not be dated by batch 0005.**

### What a register of risk is, and who pays for it — the movement's institutional spine, and it is all new

- **The proposal (Ch 81).** A room, with hours on it, anybody may ask, nothing charged, nobody asked to sign anything to come in. **One rule: a bundle may not be entered as one line. It goes in as its parts, with the sum, the date, the discount and the receiving party on each line, and the counterweight named to the people who will bear it and the number of people who will bear it, in the words those people used.** And the rule's first consequence, stated in the yard in the first ten minutes: **if a counterweight is named as a district, there is nobody to name it to, and the line does not go in at all** — so **the Compact's paper cannot be entered in the register the register was built to beat.** That is the volume's standing cruelty and it is on the page in Movement IV, not only in Movement V.
- **Five offices refuse it in five correct sentences, and a yard refuses it in nine seconds (Ch 81).** The refusals are refusals **of form**, none of principle, and none of them is a villain's. (1) **The Chamber, Sedge**: the Chamber enters what it is handed; there is no form on which a bundle is entered in parts; the fifty-two assignments are fifty-two instruments and a bundle is not an instrument; bring them and he will enter them, and that is a file behind two doors and not a register. (2) **The Trust's Rainmarket window, Rustam Vey**: the fourth box already names a counterweight in type and has for nineteen years, and a room beside it means the same clause in two places and the office certifying it twice, and **a term certified twice is certified by nobody** — and *it will not be you, Mr Vale, because you have no office and nothing to lose, and I have nineteen years*. **Vey is not turned and the Rainmarket window is not shut again.** (3) **The water office, Berta Lund**: a rate may be read and a copy of a rate is a return and a return is the office's own document; she could not issue one out of that counter **if there were a form for it**; and the entry she has offered eleven times is a total and two dates and a receiving party, which is not a name. (4) **The Fund, Zorka Vendl**: a register is a list of things that have not happened yet to anybody, there is no box for it, she has declined twice to pay for a sitting, and **the reason the register has no money is that there is no box anywhere in the kingdom for a man who reads, and the only two kinds of man in this basin who can be paid for reading a thing to other people are a clerk with a wage and a factor with a margin.** (5) **Harrow's board, in his own hand on paper**: a board may not maintain a register, has no fund, no date and one seat standing empty, and he has asked twice for a person and been refused twice.
- **The yard's refusal (Ch 81) is the movement's best sentence and it is a reason nobody had thought of:** the yard's own way of saying no is **the only piece of paper in the basin that nobody can be shown to be the seller of**, and handing it to a room spends it, because in the ninth month a man in a good coat will come up that road and ask for the yard's own method applied to his instrument. **A yard cannot petition, appeal, be sworn or be cross-examined, and that rule earns a second and a third consequence in this batch without being restated in a mouth** — the second is Ch 86, and the third is the paragraph below.
- **Sedge's counter-offer, refused on the yard's own sentence (Ch 81), is the chapter's turn and the volume's hinge:** the **third box** will take the parts, as a schedule, and *a schedule does not have to be anything anybody agreed to, it only has to be administered* — **and a register of parts is a schedule, and the yard said that sentence on the sixteenth of the seed month and spent four hours refusing to be one, and Marek will not spend the yard's best sentence like money.** **Nobody in Movements IV or V may resolve this: the register is not a Chamber schedule and the Chamber has not agreed to keep one.**

### Tallow Ward's method, its date, its owner, and its price (Ch 82) — locked

- **A sheet went up on the Gate tank on the ninth of the harvest month** carrying the yard's four rules in type, **with nothing above them, and at the foot, in the same type and with nothing above it either: *Tallow Ward. Gate yard. The twentieth day of the rains month.*** Beneath the four rules the **empty independent-witness box is printed**, and printing it is the loudest thing anybody has done in Tallow Ward in four months. **It is a ward document. It is not credited to Marek, and the yard is not grateful, not thanked, not forgiven and not a party.**
- **Nobody can be traced who printed it and the yard decides not to try, on purpose**, because about a hundred and forty people in the ward have lifted a working sheet off that nail in four months, and *the day a yard starts naming the hands on its own sheets is the day the sheets are a thing a yard keeps, and a thing a yard keeps can be produced at it.* **Kern names the origin once and refuses the pen: the twentieth of the rains month, this tank, four lines on the back of a complaint sheet off a trestle table.** **The pen is never named in the batch and a later batch may not name it.**
- **The yard's three conditions** (Ch 82): the four lines are copied in its words with its date and nothing else, and **no name at the foot, ever, or the yard nails the twentieth of the rains month up beside the offending sheet the same day**; **the empty witness box stays empty, on a new and worse reason** — *a levy is agreed by two bodies and an independent witness has to be neither of them, and a person paid out of a levy is one of the two bodies* — and **Droll says in the yard that she voted for the old humiliating reason and not the new decent one, because the decent one is a rate**; and **the yard's second person at the register is a person of the yard and is to be asked the same four questions like anybody else.** That person is **Sanne Droll**, thirty-nine, a rope-maker of Ropewalk, one of the nineteen pairs of both rounds, and she says the cost in her own words: rope she does not sell, four days of a wage in the second round with no total ever written down, and being asked about her own household with her daughter fifteen and on no roll.
- **The yard's third condition is paid for in Ch 87 and nobody thanks her for it.** The reader, Teodor Lask, nineteen, says the whole morning went out of the room while the girl of sixteen stood in it and came back in as strong, which he has seen once before, at a gate in the rains. **An earlier version of this line said *twice*, which cannot be supported: she stands in the room once, at about four feet off the counter, for about a quarter of an hour, and nothing in the scene goes out and comes back two times. It is once, and the *once before, at a gate in the rains* is now the only second thing in the sentence.**

### The form, and who may read and who may write in it (Ch 84) — locked

- **One sheet, cut in the copy room by Sedge, six ruled lines and no boxes**, with the heading **the tank and the date**. **Lines, not boxes**, on his reasoning and it is to be kept: *a box is a thing a person can be put in; a line is a thing a person has to write in; every bad instrument I have ever filed got filed in a box, and every bad instrument anybody put in front of me this summer had a box with a word at the top of it and the word was a kind chosen by whoever cut the form.* **Sedge has no service length anywhere in the manuscript and may not be given one** — his own line is *long enough that twice in it*, and Ch 84's is a second use of the same idiom and no more.
- **The six lines.** One: the thing. Two: **what the person behind it said, in their words** — and *the person* is singular, Sedge chose the word himself in nine seconds, and he says on the page that he is not sure of it. Three: who holds the other end and since when. Four: anything anybody else has to say about it, which may be nothing and that is correct. Five: **the hours — four a day — which somebody is going to have to pay for and for which Sedge has not got a figure and the man in the seed trade has not got one either, and nothing has ever gone on it because the hours are nobody's paper.** Six: **the days a person loses by standing in a room, ruled and permanently empty because there is no box in the kingdom for the days.** **Sedge says in Ch 84 that the sixth line will be the biggest hole in the thing and that every person in it will find it inside a week.** **CORRECTED at the batch 0004 review: this line of the file used to say the fifth line was *nothing has ever gone on it and nobody can say what it is for*, which contradicted Ch 84:107, where Sedge names it on the page as **Hours**. The false version had propagated into Ch 87, Ch 90, `current.md`, `open-threads.md` and the batch 0005 prompt. Ch 87 and Ch 90 now say the fifth line is the hours and is empty because the hours are nobody's paper. Treat it as the hours.**
- **Who may read: anybody, four hours a day, nothing charged, nobody asked to sign anything to come in.** Agreed in forty minutes in a yard. **A person may have their own line read out as many times as they like, and nobody may take a paper out of the room** — *a copy of a rate is a return, a return is the office's own document, and if I put a paper in your hand it would be a second document and nobody in any room would be permitted to produce it* (Ch 90, in Marek's mouth, in the words the water office uses).
- **Who may write: anybody may enter a line about their own paper, and no person may enter a line about anybody else's** — not a factor, a house, an office, a man with no office, and not Sedge, **and he says he wants to and cannot.** An office may enter its own lines; a house may enter its own.
- **A second person may come into the room with a person who cannot read, is named at the door, does not get a hand on anything, and if that person is lying the reader cannot find out and never could.** This is **Marta Brel's** ruling in Ch 87, given from the step of the shed, out of the yard's own rules, and Sedge enters that **the form is not the whole of the thing and the man who cut it does not know all of what it is.**
- **The word *household* (Ch 84, Bessok Tan, and again in Ch 89 in Merrow's mouth) is the volume's quietest finding and it may not be turned into a number:** the Share's roll is households, the instrument was carried on a count of hands in a shed at forty up and one down out of forty-one, and **forty of forty-one is not a majority and Merrow says so out loud in a shed on the low-ground road.**
- **The form is the first thing written in this volume and it is written by a man who is not a hero and who says on the page that he is not sure it is right** (Ch 84) — *a form has to be cut by a man who does not know whether it is right or there has never been one.*

### The levy, and what a levy is (Chs 83, 88, 90) — locked, and the money the batch spends

- **Pike's three figures, priced on his counting floor at the second bell on the twelfth of the harvest month and given in a hand, on a sheet, unsigned, and not in the room.** A market pays **two a reading**; it has been two for eleven years; **sixty to ninety a week**; two thirds of them houses that would pay eight and one third not houses, who have never paid, some of whom have put a coin in a drawer he would not show anybody. A levy carries about **four a week**, which is Signe Alve's wage and which Pike said he would not pay his own daughter less than. A season comes to **between one hundred and forty and one hundred and sixty, and the figure he will stand behind in a room is one hundred and forty.** **One hundred and forty on sixty-one households is a little over two pounds each** — a day of a carter at his gate, and in Tallow Ward the rope Droll did not sell on a Tuesday, twice over. **Pike has not got a figure for what a room is worth and says so in a lane, and that difference is the whole of his trade.**
- **The levy is agreed at one hundred and forty a season (Ch 88), collected at a trestle in the Gate yard by two people of the Share — Bessok Tan and an unnamed woman of the low ground — in coin and in seed, and it is collected in the open because a levy is not a secret.** **There is no total on the slate and the reason is the yard's: a total read in a room in six months is a number of people who agreed with something.** A man asking what the day has come to is told *I can tell you what you paid and I cannot tell you what the day has come to.* **No figure exists anywhere for what the day came to and none may be invented.**
- **A ward cannot be assessed a rate at all**, so the ward contributes **the shed, the notice and the four lines, at no stated value**, and is not a party. **The Share carries the whole of the money.**
- **The cap (Ch 88, Zorka Vendl's dissent, in her own words, in a finding with her name at the foot of it, on a nail in a yard):** a member who cannot pay in money may pay in kind, and **a member who has nothing pays nothing and is not entered.** **The cap is right and it is not enough, and Neris Kade says so in the same room and does not thank anybody:** a member who pays in kind pays in seed, and a member who is not entered is a member who has not been asked, **and the rule the board has just written produces a person who does not exist, and somebody in a month should look for her, and Kade cannot do it and has already said the same shape of sentence in a Crown March record this year and it cannot be taken out.**
- **A levy, defined for the first time in the basin and it is a definition and not a complaint:** *a figure a body puts on itself, in a room, on a day, in the words of the body, without an office, without a seal and without anybody able to produce it afterwards against anybody.* **A cap is the only part of a levy anybody in this basin can recognise as one, because a rate has never had one.** **The finding goes on a nail and nothing in the room paid for it.**

### The counter-move fails once, on a rule about a ward (Ch 86) — locked

- **The yard votes six minutes to enter its own refusal in the register**, on Vray's recorded objection that a single thing sent once to a building is a schedule with a nicer coat on, and it carries without a count written anywhere. **It fails in ninety seconds** on four correct attempts, all refused by the room: a yard has no words because it is not a person and the yard's own first rule is *ask the household, not the yard*; a name is required and a ward is not one; a keeper's signature would put a man's name on the only untraceable paper in the basin and **Droll refuses it in four words and the yard believes her**; and an entry with nothing at the head of it is the piece of sack in Fisk's house, which **Marta Brel names as worse in a shed than in a house** and which cost her, in front of about eighty people, the only thing she came for.
- **The cost, and it is the cost of a reader too:** the yard's best sentence in a raincoat wrote a shield over the ward's most valuable paper and is still wrong. **The register is to live with a hole in it and the hole is the shape of the yard's own refusal. Movement V may not fill it, may not enter the sheet in any form, and may not let any office hold it either** — the first half of the volume's conclusion is that the sheet stays on a nail in a shed and is a public document that cannot be produced, and the second half is that nothing can enter it because a yard is not a person. **Both are the chapter.**
- **Neris Kade declines in four words to be the one who explains it to him**, because that is exactly why she can, and because she has said it in a Crown March record already. On the causeway at the sixth bell she tells Marek not to put it in a room: **the rule did not stop him, the room stopped him, and the room had a form in it with one word in the second line that a clerk cut in nine seconds and said he was not sure of.** **That is the batch's fourth and smallest Tuesday. It is smaller than the mill, it is a professional instruction, there is no warmth in it, nobody was thanked, and neither of them will refer to the rest of it again.** The batch's three scenes with Kade and Marek in them are Ch 84 (a yard, no exchange), Ch 86 (a causeway in the dark, four lines each) and Ch 88 (a hearing room, where he says nothing and does not look at her). **No first names, no physical affection, no sulk.**

### The register's first entries, and the person who does not know what to say (Ch 87, Ch 90) — locked

- **Ch 87, the twenty-first**, the Gate shed, eleven feet by nine and built for a seed bin, a counter of two trestles and a board, sixty in the lane, and **nobody is paid, because the levy is not agreed for three more days.** **Pike's house goes in first and he insists on it for a market reason and states it without embarrassment: *I go first because I am the one who can afford to be first*, and *anybody can be second in a room where the first has made a fool of himself*.** It goes in as its parts, read in the voice of a man reading a price list; the second line is his clerk's words and the clerk is twenty-six and standing there while it is read, and the second person asks the three questions and he answers all three.
