# VOLUME 03, BATCH 0001 (Chapters 101–110) — "The Abundant Harvest"

Prose is authoritative. This is **the first of the five batches in Volume 03.** Chapters 1–100 are not touched. `outline/volume-03.md` and `outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0001.md` were written by this phase as its planning artefacts and were not edited after drafting began.

**The rule from the previous five sections carries: every clause below is a quotation or a note about a quotation, and a note that cannot be pointed at a line does not go in. Treat every verification sentence here as unproven until the chapter has been re-read. That is not politeness: this file has now been wrong about a length figure, a date, a word count, an attribution and a batch's own guardrail across four consecutive volumes, and the batch 0005 review of Volume 02 found ten blocking defects in a batch that had described itself as clean.**

### The calendar, and what the batch moved

**Batch dates: the fourth (Ch 101), the eighth to the tenth (Ch 102), the twelfth (Ch 103), the fifteenth (Ch 104), the nineteenth (Ch 105), the twenty-first (Ch 106), the twenty-fourth (Ch 107), the twenty-eighth (Ch 108) of the tenth month, MR 413; then the second (Ch 109) and the fifth (Ch 110) of the second month, MR 414. The batch crosses the year boundary and the year turning marks nothing, which is the point of the second bell of Ch 109.** The register went live on the eighteenth of the ninth month of MR 413, so it is **sixteen days** old at Ch 101, about three weeks at Ch 103, five weeks at Ch 107 and forty days at Ch 108. **Chapters 101–110 use the register's age in those terms and in no other terms, and a later batch may not restate it loosely.**

- **No real-world month name and no weekday appears anywhere in the ten chapters.** A case-sensitive sweep for the twelve month names and the seven weekdays returns only `March` (twenty-five instances, **every one of them the region or the street in Bell Stair**) and the verb `may`. A second sweep excluding *Crown March* first returns nothing. This is the first batch in two volumes to be clean, and it is clean because **nine errors were written and caught during drafting and are recorded here so that the next writer knows they are possible**: a *Tuesday* in Ch 101 that was a correction of a *Tuesday*; *on Monday* in Ch 102; *in November* in Ch 109; **six instances of *Thursday*** across Chs 109 and 110, which were a day somebody picked; and *struck in March* in Ch 107.
- **The harvest month's length is not dated anywhere and the tenth month's length is not dated anywhere.** No chapter counts days across a month boundary. **The growth month is twenty days long, is behind us, and is referred to as a past season twice (Chs 102, 106) and never as a place on a calendar.** The second month of MR 414 is two chapters and no length is claimed for it.
- **Bells** (inherited, unchanged): day bells first to sixth, the sixth when the copy room shuts, the seventh in the evening, the eighth struck in the morning. First through seventh are all used. **There is no ninth bell and none is used.**

### The abundant harvest (Chs 101, 105, 110) — locked

- **The low ground's barley rate has fallen by about a third in nine days and no other figure for it exists anywhere in the batch.** The fall is documented from the twenty-fourth of the ninth month (Kuez's bridge book) and by the fourth of the tenth month. **No price figure for barley is stated in any chapter and the batch card forbids one; the first draft had two and both contradicted the third and were removed.** Ottmar Kuez reads the rate out and **will not say the figure aloud**, because it is not his to say and it is written in a bridge book with a day against every line and nobody has ever come to look at the book.
- **The cause is grain coming off the Red Uplands early and in quantity, down a road that is open on a Crown March paper**, waved through by a man with a lantern who has been *told* and does not know by whom. **Forty carts at the weighbridge on the fourth of the tenth month, thirty-one the night before, about twenty the night before that, and about eleven left at the sixth bell.** Petar Duss has four of the nine carts of the same make, and **twenty-six carts off that bridge since the twenty-second of the ninth month carried a mark, nine of them his or were.**
- **Petar Duss is the volume's opening and closing character and is the best thing in the batch.** He is a haulier, thirty-one years on the road, four children, a house with a new roof on one side, a rope round his wrist, a lame near hind. He paid **four shillings** for a mark in the growth month from a man in two rooms above a chandler's shop, and he has never been told by anybody where his paper came from except the man he paid. He has a crate-load of things he wants said and none of them is a complaint: *I do not care whose mark it is. I care that it goes.* And, in Chapter 110, the volume's thesis in a haulier's mouth: **a market full of grain it did not grow is not a good year — it is a good year somewhere else and a bill here, and the bill has a name on it, and the name is not a market.**
- **A price that stops falling is a price somebody is holding** (Sefa, the boatwoman, Ch 110). The rate stands for four days from about the first of the second month. **Marek tells Sefa the price is falling because the barley is coming down fast and he does not know why it is coming down fast**, which is the whole of what he knows and the whole of what he is honest about.
- **The town has been eating since before the harvest month and the granary offices of the basin are content.** The four hundred and six people of Tallow Ward were removed from the river-stair scene in this batch's review, because **no figure of Tallow's may appear outside a Tallow scene**, and the review was right.

### The four terraces and the season works (Ch 102) — locked

- **Four terraces. Hartlow is the fourth and the top, four miles above a stone bridge.** The road has eleven switchbacks and takes a day. The second terrace is a day's walk down and a half day's walk back. **Of thirty-nine lots sown on the second terrace, nine have failed.** The third terrace **has stopped reporting** — it has sent a folded sheet with a figure on it to an office in the hills once a year for nineteen years and has not sent it this year, and **there is no form in this kingdom in which anybody may be sent to ask why.**
- **The season works: nine lines, made by the four terraces themselves, in the second month, out of what they can see from their own doors. It is not an office list and it belongs to no office.** Cutting is one, a drain is two, the shared threshing floor is three, a road is four, a water is five, two store rooms are six and seven, a seed store is eight, **and the ninth is the fourth-terrace store, which is the biggest and the only one with a key.** **The ninth line is ruled and empty.** Janko Vrabec has held the key for eleven years, was told in the growth month of MR 413 that he is going to die this winter, has not taken the key off the nail, and **has left the ninth line ruled and empty rather than write his own name in it, because in the eleventh month somebody would have to strike a man's name off a list and there is no box for that.** *The works has nine lines and the ninth one is empty.* **The first draft had eight lines and a hole and it was corrected: the lock is nine lines with one of them empty.**
- **Janko Vrabec, fifty-eight, the keeper of the Hartlow cooperative's roll, refuses to be the story, out loud, to Marek's face, on his own ground:** *I am not going to be the thing you came up here about.* He makes Marek say it once to his face and then leave it. **He is not a villain and the chapter says so in his own words:** *I asked for it. It arrived. I have been up a road and I have got nine lots of sand out of thirty-nine and I would do it again this year for the same reason and I would rather it had gone to somebody who was going to be able to use it.* He ends the chapter **sitting up in his own yard with a lamp** and says the sentence the volume is built on: *I am the ninth line of a list of nine, and I am going to sit up in my own yard, and if I am not stood up in the eleventh month then somebody can come up this road and ask me whether I was right, and if I am stood up then nobody can come, and that is the whole of what a dying man can do with a form.*
- **Ruta Vrabec, fifty-one, and Tomek, nineteen, are not in Chapter 102 except as the household the ninth line stands behind.** The card for 102 says Tomek is named once and enters nothing, and the review caught the batch's first draft breaking that by putting him and Ruta on a *second*-terrace paper in Chapter 108; **they are in Hartlow, the number of persons who will bear the Hartlow counterweight is two, and that number is Ch 97's and is not restated here.**
- **The Hartlow roll is six lines: four women over fifty, one a child, and the sixth line carrying a season examiner's name.** The first draft said "six women, four over fifty, one a child, and the sixth line is the examiner" and was corrected; **the sixth line is the examiner and there is no sixth woman.**
- **The sequence instrument was requested in the second month of MR 412, sealed in the hills in about nine days, and the sowing was done in the second month of MR 413, ten months later, because that is when the ground was ready.** **The nine lots that came up as sand are this season's and are the harvest just gathered; a first draft dated the sowing to MR 412 and made the harvest a year out of date, and it was corrected.** Nobody may find a defect in the instrument.

### The cellar of forms (Ch 104) — locked

- **A cellar under a chandler's yard in Bell Stair, eleven steps down, leased by Vesna Duss, sixty-one, for nineteen years, in which she has never read a document.** Nine crates, about forty sheets standing on edge in the open one. **Sedge counts twice: two hundred and eleven sheets of a Trust seasonal advance form.** The printer's ledger gives the run as *eleven hundred and something* — **the phrase is locked and the figure may never be fixed** — and about a thousand sheets are unaccounted for, which is an ordinary commercial fact: a printer sells paper and the season offices of a kingdom use more than eleven hundred forms a year. **Sedge is a clerk with no service length anywhere and may not be given one; the first draft gave him eleven years in the building and the sentence now reads *long enough that twice in it*, which is his own line.**
- **The crate came off a road in the flood year.** A driver asked for a dry room for the night, was given the cellar, and in the morning said the load was paper for the Crown March depot at Verrow, that the account had gone on to somebody else, and that he would leave it in the lane. **She took it. It has not moved. It came in the flood year and it has been here since the flood year — the first draft dated it three incompatible ways (before the flood year, in 411, and six years) and all three were wrong, and there is no version of this crate that is six years old.**
- **Vesna Duss excludes herself before anybody asks, in the yard's and the register's own idiom:** *I am not going to be thanked for telling you, and I am not going to be the woman in a yard with a cellar of Crown March forms.* **Nobody asks her anything.**
- **Sedge's finding on the form, which is a canon addition and not a repair:** the Trust seasonal advance form's **fifth box at the foot is a return** — the oldest box on the form, the only one not dated in this century, and the box a district may hand a returnable sum over in at a discount under a rule older than anybody in the building. **The counterweight in the fourth box is in type and has been for nineteen years, and it is stock and not a crime.** The first draft recited the four-words-for-a-charge list from Ch 79 two volumes late in a new mouth; that was cut.
- **The ninth crate's lid carries a Tide Reckoning mark** — a month, a tide phase, a harbour seal stamped in wax, and a translator's name in a small hand with a space before it and none after. **It is not a forgery, not a mystery, and not a clue: it is a shipping mark put on by a man in a port for a crate going to a depot in the hills.** **Marek sees it, does not go back to it, does not say so for eleven minutes, and then tells Sedge and not Vesna Duss. Sedge says no, and gives the reason, and goes back down the eleven steps to put a board on the crate, which is the only thing about it that is anybody's business.**

### Rell, the projection, and the advance (Chs 105, 107) — locked

- **Sabin Rell, fifty-four, Crown March granary official, twenty-nine years, the depot at Verrow.** He comes down the river on the nineteenth of the tenth month because the basin feeds three regions and the harvest is good and the board at Verrow must certify an export. **He is right about all of it, he says so himself first, and he is not stupid and not cruel.** He comes down because four people told him the Gate shed's sentence wrong and one of them asked a question, and he has come to see whether the room thinks.
- **The projection, defined on the page for the first time:** a granary's forward estimate of a district's yield, entered in a ledger **in the year before the yield.** It is a number, not a measurement, and it is entered in advance, and that is the ordinary practice of a book that has to balance. **The four terraces are entered at the ten-year mean. The ten-year mean is not in the book Rell is carrying, not in any book in Morrowfen, and he is not permitted to produce it — because the moment a depot can produce its own estimate the estimate is an offer instead of a measurement, and an offer can be sued.** Rell knows the figure and has read the table once and cannot swear to it, and he says so on a stair, unasked, in about forty words, before he has signed anything.
- **The advance is eleven thousand and some hundreds of pounds and the figure MAY NOT BE FIXED IN THIS VOLUME BY ANY CHAPTER IN ANY MOUTH.** It is the volume's only money and it is already in the four terraces. **The underwriting clause is the fourth clause of the standard granary form, is a matter of public print, and is correct.** There is no fraud anywhere in it and no villain in it.
- **The clause is signed on the twenty-fourth, in a room over a chandler's shop on the low-ground road in Morrowfen**, in a room hired for the day with one table and four chairs, in front of Marek (standing, because the chairs were for the four people in the room), a young man from Verrow with the ledger who is called nothing else, and Ilse Ferrow. **The sealed original goes back to Verrow in a locked box with the ledger.** The first draft had Rell signing at Verrow and Chapter 106 referring to Verrow; both are corrected, and **the distance and the travel time are no longer stated as figures.**
- **The sheet is sealed before anybody writes a word on it**, and the seal and the counterseal were struck in the hills and came down in a locked box. **Every instrument in this kingdom that matters is sealed before somebody signs it, and the signing is not the sealing, and there is a form for the sealing and there is no form for the signing.** Rell dates the sheet twice — once in his hand, once in his own column — and says he started doing that a year ago and the day he stops is the day he should be moved to a shed.
- **The counterseal on the clause is Halvard Sunn's and Rell paid him four pounds for a day and Sunn does not know Rell is in the building.**
- **The schedule is eleven lines, eleven names, four terrace holdings, and the fourth column is the same nine words on all eleven lines: *the seed-keepers of the four terraces after this season*.** **The first draft called them eleven words, which is the holdings and not the phrase; it is nine.** **And the phrase is on a printed form, and the *and* before it is on the printed form too, and only the number is filled in by hand — so the words are not a sentence a valley wrote and they are the same nine words as the Hartlow clause Neris Kade confessed to in Ch 97, with the number in front of them changed. Marek sees it, has the sentence ready, and does not say it.** That is the first time in the volume that he has a piece of information he is going to sit on.
- **Rell refuses the number of people in the eleven holdings and it is Volume 02's finding in a fifth mouth:** *There is no figure. A holding is a holding and a household is not a holding, and I could give you the number of holdings and I could not give you the number of people in them and neither could the man at the depot, and we are not going to try, because if you count the people in a district then the people in a district become a figure, and a figure can be entered, and a figure that can be entered can be added up, and the sum of the people in four terraces is the sort of number that a house in a good coat could take to a house in another good coat and turn into a yield.*
- **Ilse Ferrow asks one question and it is the true thing in the room before the fourth bell:** *Do you know what you have just certified.* Rell says yes. She does not approve of him, says so in two words, is not thanked, and says that if anybody asks her in a year what she said in that room she will say that she asked him whether he knew and he said yes and then he signed it, **and that it will not help him and is not intended to.** She is a lapsed licence holder and is not a party and is not forgiven.
- **Rell asks Marek, as a courtesy between two men who have been of use to one another, whether he intends to do anything, and Marek says no, and means it.** The chapter's last beat is Rell saying *Go and look for the room that signed the paper, not the room that is keeping the number.* The number will be in a book and there is no form in this kingdom that lets a man in a good coat out of a book, and **Rell has been doing this for twenty-nine years and has never once known where the number is kept, and has known all along where the paper is.** That is a defence and it is going to work.

### Four parts of a mark (Ch 106) — the volume's hinge, and the answer to the inherited question

- **Halvard Sunn, forty-four, a Saltglass harbor notary, four years in Morrowfen, two rooms on a lease above a chandler's shop, a press that is not his, no second office.** He has said out loud that he does not know what the marks are and has not known for nine years, and **nobody may take that back.**
- **A Tide Reckoning mark is four parts, on the page, in his hands: a month out of an almanac, a tide phase out of the almanac, a harbour seal struck in wax from a brass matrix, and a translator.** He builds it out of four objects on the table in about ninety seconds, without touching it, and says that is the whole of what he can show anybody. **Three of the four parts are objects and one is a movement.**
- **The reason he will not swear to a hand, in nine sentences, and it is correct:** a mark that names a **day** can be checked against that day by anybody with a calendar; a mark that names a **month** can only be checked against a day by somebody with a **tide table**, and my months are not your months and my tides are not your tides, and there is one tide table in this basin and it is on the table in front of him. **A refusal to swear is not a denial, and he says that before anything else, because a no at that table costs the man who asked.**
- **The thing he would say and the thing he will not: a mark that names a month can go on any day inside that month. That is not a trick and it is not a trick of his. That is what a month is.** And the whole worth of a mark is that a person who has never been to Morrowfen and has never heard of Rainmarket can read it and rely on it, **and the price of that is that it can be read by that person and by nobody else, and he has never once been able to have both.**
- **The notebook's own answer to Volume 02's question, and it is the volume's institutional sentence:** *What is it for. Not what it is. **A mark decides where a paper can go.** The mark does not say the paper is good. It decides where the paper can go, and it decides it by being read by one man on the coast who has never heard of any of us.* He asks Marek what nine hundred marks are **for** and has never been able to answer it in nineteen years in the trade and four years in this room.
- **The slow dull door, and it stays the only one:** write to the harbour; the harbour keeps a list; the list says *yes*, or *no*, or *the name is not on the list*, in about six weeks, for about a pound. **The list will not tell anybody what the hand did on a particular day in a cellar four miles from a river.** Marek says he will do it in about a fortnight when he has somebody else's paper he cannot get into a room, and Sunn does not thank him and does not expect it, **and in Chapter 110 he has not done it and the reason is on the page.**
- **The four instruments the hand is on**, as stated in this chapter: a bundle of nine sheets and a schedule of fifty-two lines; a board over a shed door on this road; a haulier's contract for barley out of the second terrace in the growth month at four shillings; **and the inside of the lid of a crate of two hundred and eleven sheets on the fifteenth of the tenth month.** **The first draft had Marek assert that Sunn put the third one there himself; Chapter 101 establishes only that Duss paid four shillings to a man in two rooms above a chandler's shop and was never told, so the assertion was softened to a question, which is what gives Sunn something real to refuse.**

### A terrace at the door (Ch 108) — and what the batch got wrong first

- **Zofia Teran, forty-four, second terrace, keeps the terrace's tally in a school exercise book**, comes down four miles and eleven switchbacks with a cloth bundle containing a copy of the fourth column written out in her own hand because she could not carry the schedule. She is not the person behind the paper — her name is against her holding, not against the paper — and she has worked that out in nine days and has decided **not to name** the people in the class and **not to ask** any of them, and asks for it to be written down that she decided not to and not that she forgot. **The first draft had her count the class at three and name Tomek and a woman of fifty-one, which is Ch 97's Hartlow pair on the wrong terrace and is a number of persons inside a class, which Rell refuses four pages earlier.**
- **THE LINE DOES NOT GO IN, and the reason is not new and is not Marek's.** A person may enter a line about their own paper and nobody may enter a line about anybody else's, and the first question at that counter every morning is *is that your line, is that the person behind it, is it your words* — and the answer is nobody, correctly, and she has been carrying the answer down a road in a cloth for nine days. **And the fourth column is a class of people, and a class of people cannot be traced to a door, so there is nobody to name the counterweight to, so the fourth line stays empty, so the line does not go in at all.** The reader reads the pieces of it out anyway, reads the empty fourth line twice at the ordinary speed, and does not explain what he is doing.
- **THE FIRST DRAFT OF THIS CHAPTER HAD MAREK EXTEND THE RULE — deciding that a class goes in the same as a district — and that was wrong and was corrected, and the correction is the chapter.** The extension would have made the register a remedy, would have spent the volume's institutional beat in Movement I, and would have closed the hole that Chs 131–150 need. **No chapter of this volume may extend the register's rule, and the register's rule may not be made larger by a man with no office, and the reason is his own: a form has to be cut by a man who does not know whether it is right, and a rule made by a man with no standing on a morning is a rule with nobody's name at the foot of it, and a yard's method was spent on a nail in the seed month by exactly that reasoning.** Kade's exchange at the wall by the door is four lines and is not warm: *You could have made a ruling. Somebody was standing in this room who had been waiting all month to make one.* / *So somebody could. And there is no form on which any of us is anybody's record.* / *I know. I have read it in a shed off a seed room as well.*
- **Neris Kade comes up the road with her, is in the lane for an hour and forty minutes saying nothing, is named at the door, and gets a hand on nothing.** She is a second person and is not thanked. She was in a room off a seed room on the fourteenth of the ninth month and was named at the door there too, and neither chapter says so in words and the reader can see it.
- **The fifth line of the form stays empty and the sixth line stays empty for ever and no line was added to it.** The rota in the seed house has days on it and **nothing at the head of it** — see below.
- **The batch's one small institutional gain, and it is not a rule and it is not a ruling: Droll says that the reader has said a thing about a paper to the person the paper is about and not to the room, and asks that the yard notice it, and nobody writes it down, and the reader then enters the date at the foot of the wall list, which is the only thing he has ever entered on that board that was not a line somebody was asking for.** Zofia Teran goes up the road alone, in the dark, and nobody walks her up it, and the reader reads the fourth line out twice at the ordinary speed to an empty room, because the room does that.

### The trays, the lamp, and the rota (Ch 103) — locked

- **Hana Belic, thirty-four, named for the first time in this volume.** A member of the Elmsworth Seed Share in her own right, not through anybody, and she draws the eleventh tray, **and the eleventh tray is one of the eleven that came up as sand.** **She has worked sixty-one trays twice a day since the sixth of the ninth month, in the dark, by feel, because the seed house's lamp is the thirteenth of fourteen lines on the water office's list and is supplied in the third month and not before.** She has a cough in the small of her back and a physician wrote her out of four tray-days and did not write her out of the other seven.
- **The bench moves into the Gate shed, on a rota, from the fourth bell to the sixth, in eleven pieces, in the rain, with about nine people.** The arguments are all correct: the bench comes first and the lamp is a list nobody has sent anybody against; the register is a practice and a practice with a room in it is a practice, and a cooperative's work in a ward's room in six weeks is a thing a man in a good coat can point at; and honesty in a room is cheap and distance is not, because anything can be said in a shed and the trouble starts about a quarter of a mile away.
- **Marek proposes the fifth line and is refused, by the yard's own rule and for the yard's own reason, and the refusal is the batch's best institutional beat.** A hundred and twenty-two trays a day, which is sixty-one twice, is a person's hours and it is the only hours anybody in this basin has ever said out loud, and it does not go on the fifth line, because **a round that repeats is the third box on the settlement desk's form, and the yard refused the Compact's levy in the seed month for exactly that reason, and a figure that appears on a board the same way twice turns the board into a roll, and a shed where a person may ask what a piece of paper is becomes a place where the hours of a cooperative are kept, and that is a thing a man in a good coat can produce at anybody in nine months.** The yard would have fixed the empty fifth line and turned a register into a wage book.
- **And the rota on the seed house wall has days on it and nothing at the head of it.** Not the day, not the season, not the Share's name (that is on the bench book, in a different shed), **not a keeper's name, because there are sixty-one members and which one of the sixty-one is on the rota is a question the yard has no standing to ask and the Share has no form to ask it with.** Belic points at it herself: *It just goes — day, day, day. The days are the days the shed is open, and so the rota is not ours, it is the shed's. **We have built a rota that belongs to a shed.*** And then: *Leave it bare. Put the days on. I will come in when the rota says I am to and I will go out at the sixth bell and I will not be thanked for it, because the day it is on a board with a name at the top of it, somebody is going to work out that the trays are a resource and the days are mine.*
- **This is the fifth bare place in one basin in one year, and nothing proposes a heading for it.** The four are Ch 100's list — a sheet cut off a seed sack in a keeper's house in the growth month, a column on a board in a shed in Tallow Ward in the seed month, the column on the register's own wall list, and the column on a bench in a shed on the low-ground road. **The rota is the fifth and it is new in this volume and it is a rota, not a column, and nothing in it has a heading and nothing may be proposed for it.**
- **What it costs, named in the chapter:** two hours of a four-hour day with the lane taken, **no water at the Ropewalk standpipe on those mornings, and one of the two women who carries the yard's water now coming down at the second bell to get it up before the queue forms, which is a solution that puts the whole cost of the queue on one woman's back.** And the register runs forty minutes over on the first morning because a bench has turned up in a shed built for a seed bin, and the reader stops in the middle of a second line because the bench frame is in the way of the board, and says so out loud, and about nine people laugh.

### A brother, a slate, and a kitchen (Ch 109) — locked

- **Tomas refuses Marek's attempt to quiet the case, for the right reason, and the sentence is the volume's statement of its own problem:** *the whole of what has happened to us since the flood year is that men in rooms have decided which of my losses was worth writing down, and I have found out about four of them about a year after each, and the one I found out about soonest was the one you carried up a hill in your coat and asked nothing for, and I would have gone up that hill myself in an hour if you had handed it to me in the yard.* And: *you are eleven years late.*
- **What he wants, and it is the whole of the relationship beat in this volume:** *I want to be told when I am being used. Not a month afterwards. Not by a clerk in Bell Stair with a record in his hand. In the room. Before.* **Marek's realisation, in about nine seconds, standing in a cold yard, is that his brother is not asking him to be a different man and that there is no form in this kingdom in which a person may be told anything first, and that the register in a shed in Tallow Ward is the only place he has ever found where a man says a thing out loud in a room where it is written down, and that he has a duty there four hours a day and nobody has ever told him it is a duty to anybody else as well.**
- **Tomas goes up the road on the fifth as a bridgewright and not as a brother**, and his reason is a drain on the second terrace that has been holding a wall since the flood year and is going to go in the third month, and a road whose culvert has its mouth four feet too low, and the bill goes on the season works. *I have been on this road thirty-four years and I do not need a plan for a drain.* **He is not a favour and he is not a martyr and Marek goes because he is asked.**
- **Lio, nine, with a slate, asks what a Crown March is and is not satisfied by the answer**, and her sentence is the best child in the manuscript after Wenna Drel's: *you have said the word *a man* nine times in this kitchen and you have not said *a person* once.* And *You did not answer my question on the tail-board either. You said the date is for afterwards. That was the second time somebody has said a true thing and then gone inside.*
- **Tamsin is in the kitchen and is asked nothing about the mark or the house book, and the batch says so on the page** — *nobody in that kitchen said one word about a book* — and she does one ordinary thing and one flat line about bread. **The first draft had her deliver the volume's thesis, which the guardrail does not permit and the scene had not paid for, and it was cut.**
- **The first draft had Tomas claim that Marek's hand was written about in a Crown March minute with no name at the foot of it. Nothing in Ch 99 writes about Marek's hand, his name is nowhere in the minute because he is not a party to the work, and the line also brushed the Volume 16 forearm guardrail from an angle this batch must not touch. Corrected to the parcel carried up a hill in a coat.** Marek's forearm is not described, referred to or looked at anywhere in the ten chapters.

### A cargo going down (Ch 110) — the volume's first movement closes

- **The question is asked in plain speech by a woman on a river stair and nobody in the chapter knows it is a question.** Margit Sova, forty-one, cooks on the low ground, a basket and a list and no money, buying in the first week and waiting for the second. *Who is it that says a thing is all right.* And then, four minutes later, the second one: *And who says **the person with the seal** is the right person.*
- **And Marek answers it, and the answer is Volume 03's institutional sentence:** a person with a seal, standing in a room, at a desk, on a day, saying a thing is as it is on a page — always a person, never a district, never a class, never a number. And then: *Nobody says. **A signature does not say whether a thing is true. A signature says where a thing can go.*** **He tells her it is not his, that a notary said it to him in two rooms above a chandler's shop, and he uses the notary's name nowhere, and she notices that he has not used it.** *You have told me a man you know and not his name, which is a thing a man does when he is about to be somewhere in the morning and would rather be known as a reader of numbers.*
- **A barge goes down loaded with a coil of paper in a box that is not lashed well, and the mark on the roll is a month nobody on the bank can name, and it is going to Saltglass and will not come back.** The captain is Rennick, about thirty, eleven years on the stair, who has never had a certificate in a box on his deck and who takes the box without looking at the paper and gives the boy a shilling for holding the rope, **and that is how a crew stays a crew.** **Petar Duss is on the stair at the third bell with his rope, loading for the four o'clock up-road, and says out loud in front of about nine people that the mark cost four shillings and was not worth it.**
- **Marek has not written to the harbour**, because a letter costs a pound and takes six weeks, and he has two numbers on the back of his left hand in pencil and they are the only two figures in this basin that belong to him and neither of them is about anybody's future. The chapter's last beat is the coat off the nail and the road.

### What every character ends the batch with

- **Marek Vale: a clerk with no office who has read out two rates in two places in a month and been asked about it, who has one sentence of his own that he said once in a witness room in the growth month and cannot produce, who has said *no* to a Crown March granary official's direct question and meant it, who proposed a fix for the register's empty fifth line and was refused by the yard's own rule, who chose not to extend the register's rule and has said why out loud, and who has two numbers on the back of his hand.** He gets no office, no post, no pay, no form, no vote and no right of entry. He is going up a road in the morning with his brother.
- **Sabin Rell: right, and he signed it.** He keeps his post, he is not a villain, he has not been told anything, he is owed nothing, and he has handed a man the correct defence to the thing that is about to happen to him. He is censured in Movement V and `outline/ending.md` §4 owns his later life.
- **Halvard Sunn: right, and it costs him nothing to be right, and he will not swear.** He has lost nothing, he has said the volume's answer out loud to two men in a fortnight, and the only thing he wants is to be told what a mark is for.
- **Neris Kade: right twice, and unable to do anything about either, and she says so before he does.** She has been up a road, she has said the same flat no twice, and she has not been thanked and has not asked.
- **Tomas Vale: refused, for the right reason, and is going up the road as a bridgewright.** He wants to be told first, in the room, before.
- **Aldis Kern and the ward: the bench is in the shed and the yard did not want it, and the yard's own rule stopped the fix that would have made the register a wage book.** The yard is not grateful, not thanked, not forgiven and not a party, and it voted in four minutes in the seed month against a levy and has now refused a fix by the same rule.
- **Janko Vrabec: sitting up in his own yard with a lamp, and the ninth line of a list of nine empty behind him, and the one person on that road who is not going to be a story about a woman who came down a hill.**
- **Zofia Teran: went up the road alone, with her own handwriting in a cloth, and the fourth line read out as empty, and nothing was written down about her and nobody thanked her and she asked for nothing.**
- **Ovid Merrow: not in the batch.** His licence lapsed on the ninth of the ninth month, he keeps the money, he is not dissolved and not convicted, and **Ferrow's lapsed licence is dated correctly in Ch 107 and nothing else about him is touched.**

### Ledger Sight, Boundary Hold, Season Hand, and the guardrails across Chapters 101–110

**ZERO Ledger Sight panels in ten chapters. The budget was at most one with zero as the target and it is met at zero. No Boundary Hold is used, wished for, hinted at or mentioned. The founding standing hold is not referred to. No Season Hand is used, described or anticipated — it is Movement III's change, and nothing in the batch is a hand but a hand. Marek's forearm is not described, referred to or looked at anywhere in the ten chapters, and no second person decodes either entry in the vault book on the second floor. Tamsin is in Chapter 109 and is asked nothing. Pella Rusk is not in the batch, her surety cell is untouched, and Marek's unanswered answer in the schoolroom of the rains month is still unanswered. The Kallow roll and the name Kallow are not mentioned. The year scar is not mentioned. Zorka Vendl's discharge book, the Hollow Galleries, the unclaimed take-off, the reserve carry's origin and Grale's cut-order suspicion are all untouched. The Last Convocation, the First Account, the Founder's Clause, the crossing year, Edren Sable and A-0 are not mentioned once. The Saltglass Harbor Council is not named and is not implied; a harbor notary is not the Council. No Choir, no Crown of Continuation, no military body, no second lender and no Hollow Court cell.**

**There is no villain in this batch and none was manufactured.** Duss is right and wants it recorded. Kade is right twice. Rell is right and signs. Sunn is right and loses nothing. Lenge has not entered. Vesna Duss excludes herself before she is asked. **The only criminal available is a mark that names a month**, and it is not a criminal yet — it is a calendar.

**The romance does not advance past professional respect.** Kade and Marek are in the same scene twice, at most: Ch 102 (a bench, a morning, four lines, no warmth, no first names, she does not thank him) and Ch 108 (a shed, a door, four lines, no warmth, no first names, nobody thanked). **Volume 04 owns the first honest disagreement about who should bear a risk and the shared practical method, and this volume has taken neither.**

### Deliberate divergences and notes for the reviewer

- **Length, counted from the files with the title line included: 30,139 words across ten chapters, and the batch is OVER the 24,000–28,000 plan by 2,139.** The distribution is **Ch 101 3,443 · Ch 102 3,882 · Ch 103 3,203 · Ch 104 2,583 · Ch 105 2,881 · Ch 106 2,628 · Ch 107 3,293 · Ch 108 3,065 · Ch 109 2,554 · Ch 110 2,599.** The shortest is 2,554 and the longest is 3,882; no chapter is under 2,550 and two are above 3,200. **This is the batch's overrun and it is recorded rather than rounded down.** Against Volume 02's five batches, all counted the same way with the title line included, it is **smaller than batch 0004 (30,510) and batch 0003 (30,279) and larger than batch 0005 (29,874), batch 0002 (26,688) and batch 0001 (24,937)**, and it is therefore the third consecutive batch over the plan and the first batch of Volume 03 to be over it. **The figures were taken from the files after the review-repair pass and the review's own estimate of 26,200 was wrong by about four thousand words and estimated rather than counted; that is recorded here because this repository's length figures have now been wrong six times and every one of them was written before the count was taken.** The reason is not padding: Ch 102 carries a road, a bench, a keeper, a dying man and a refusal to be a story; Ch 107 carries a sheet, a schedule, a class written eleven times and a signature; Ch 108 carries a rule, a refusal and a woman who came four miles.
- **An independent review was run against this batch and returned thirty-two blocking findings and ten should-fix items, and all of them are fixed.** Two of the blocking findings were structural and are the ones a later writer must know about: **Chapter 108's first draft had Marek extend the register's rule, which would have broken the volume's central guardrail, and Chapter 102's first draft attributed Marek's own sentence from Chapter 79 to Neris Kade's fourth line.** Both are corrected above and both corrections are described in the sections they were made in. **The review's own word counts were estimates produced without a shell and are superseded by the counts above.**
- **Ten engines, checked and distinct:** a weighbridge and a queue at night · a bench and thirty-nine lots at dawn on an upland road · sixty-one trays in a dark shed and a bench carried up a lane in the rain · a cellar and nine crates under a chandler's yard · a river stair, a barge and a boatman · a man at a table with four parts of a mark built out of four objects · one sheet, a pen and a signature · a room with a rule in it and a woman who came four miles · a kitchen, a table, a slate and two brothers · a boat leaving at the fourth bell and a mark going out of the basin. **No chapter's load-bearing event is a document read out to a room. Ch 108 is the nearest thing to a trap and it is carried by the people standing in it.** There is no copy-room scene at a long table in this batch; Ch 104 is a cellar and Ch 106 is a table with four objects on it and nobody is reading anything.
- **Panel budget: ZERO Ledger Sight panels in ten chapters.** No Boundary Hold, no Season Hand. The founding standing hold is not used, offered, wished for or hinted at. Marek's forearm is not described, referred to or looked at. Tamsin is not asked anything.
- **Figures this batch creates and a later batch must not break:** **no price figure for barley is stated anywhere and none may be invented**; the rate's fall is *about a third in nine days*; **eleven hundred and something** the print run, unfixed; **two hundred and eleven** sheets in nine crates; **four** shillings for a mark; **thirty-nine** lots and **nine** failed; **four** terraces, **eleven** holdings in **eleven** names; **nine** words in the fourth column, written eleven times; **six** lines on the Hartlow roll, four women over fifty, one a child, and a season examiner on the sixth; **nine** lines on the season works, the ninth empty; **sixty-one** Share members; **sixty-one** trays; **one hundred and twenty-two** trays a day, which is the only figure in the batch about anybody's hours and is refused; **thirteen** and **fourteen** lines on the water office's list, the school the fourteenth; **eleven thousand and some hundreds** the advance, unfixed; **nine** days Teran carried the answer down a road; **four** days the rate has stood; **about eleven switchbacks** and a road that takes a day each way; **about nine days** between the request for the early sowing and the seal; **twelve months** between the seal and the sowing (the request is the second month of MR 412, the seal nine days later, the sowing the second month of MR 413 — *ten months* was in the first draft and was wrong); **nine hundred** sacks of barley on the down barge in Ch 110; **nine hundred** people in the basin, in Rell's own mouth in Ch 105, which is his estimate of his own district and not a Tallow figure; **nine and two** the price of flour, in Margit Sova's mouth in Ch 110 — *the flour is nine and two and not six* — and **it is a price of flour and not of barley, it is the town's own figure and not one Rell or Duss or Kuez says, and no price for barley exists anywhere in the volume**; **eighteen** people in the Gate shed and **about twenty** in it, and **eighty** in the lane. **The locked figures of Volume 02 are unchanged and none of them appears in a Rainmarket, upland, river or Bell Stair scene: 40,900, 2,400, 1,916, 300, 1,100, 40, 61, 52, 9, nine links, forty-one lines in forty-one hands, one hundred and forty, four hundred and six, fifty-two lines.** The only Tallow figure in the batch is the **about a hundred and forty people** at a tank (Ch 101, Tallow-adjacent, and it is the figure the yard's own grant was made in). **Ch 108's crowd is not a Tallow figure and the state files used to call it one: the chapter says eighty in the lane outside the Gate shed by the fourth bell, about twenty in the shed of the twenty-two it holds, and about eighteen in the room when Zofia Teran is read to. The figure 'about one hundred and ten in the Gate yard' that an earlier draft of this file asserted matches nothing on the page and is struck.** **No figure of Tallow's appears in a Rainmarket, upland, river or Bell Stair scene in the finished batch; the four hundred and six that was in Chapter 110 was removed by the review and Chapter 105's four hundred and six was removed before it was ever written.**
- **Two outline divergences to record rather than paper over.** (a) **Chapter 107 does not follow the card's "Ferrow says nothing for four pages." She speaks twice and the second speech is the best thing in the chapter, and it was written that way deliberately and is kept.** (b) **Chapter 110 does not end on a "sister's slate message" as the card proposed; it ends on the register opening, the empty fifth line, the bare column, the coat off the nail and the road, and the slate message is not in the chapter.** Both are taken as written. `outline/volume-03.md` and `outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0001.md` were not edited after drafting began and neither divergence is a repair.
- **Canon additions on the page that the outline does not carry, recorded here as proposed canon rather than invented silently:** (1) **a granary projection is entered in the year before the yield and the ten-year mean is not producible by the depot that certifies it**; (2) **a Crown March instrument is sealed in advance of signing and a counterseal is required on Morrowfen cargo, so a grant signed in Morrowfen is the ordinary case and not an exception**; (3) **the Trust seasonal advance form's fifth box is a return and the fourth box is stock, and about a thousand sheets of a six-year run are unaccounted for because a printer sells paper**; (4) **the first rule of a register of risk is a person may enter a line about their own paper, and a paper whose person behind it is not in the room is not entered at all, which is the reason a class counterweight is refused without a ruling.** **None of the four is a new world rule, none touches the missing year, the Last Convocation, the First Account, the Founder's Clause or A-0, and none of the four is mentioned once in Chapters 101–110 except as quoted above.**
- **A self-reported pass in this section is not evidence.** The findings above were produced by writing the ten chapters, running an independent review, and grepping them. **A reviewer should re-read before treating any of it as closed.**
- **`state/phase-ledger.json` is controller-owned and untouched. There is no separate batch-summary file in the repository layout; the batch summary is `state/current.md`.**

## BATCH 0001 SECOND REPAIR PASS — against `logs/next-0001.review.log`

An independent review of the completed batch returned **nine blocking findings and thirteen should-fix items, plus two operational notes.** All twenty-two prose and state findings are closed. The two operational notes are controller-owned and are recorded at the foot of this section rather than acted on. **The batch was not restarted and no chapter was rewritten from the top: every fix is local, and the good prose of all ten chapters stands.**

### The findings that changed canon on the page

1. **Chapter 102 named Marek as one of the four people Janko Vrabec had told he was going to die, six lines before Janko asks for it to be said to his face and Marek answers *you have said it to my face*.** **Marek is out of the four.** The chapter now says he found it out nine days ago from a seed merchant in Bell Stair who did not know he was carrying anybody's news, which is the point of the chapter and was being contradicted by the list. **Janko is still told to his face, in the yard, at the seventh bell, and Marek still does not ask him anything about it.**
2. **Chapter 102's date arithmetic was broken and the error had been promoted into this file.** The request for the early sowing is the second month of MR 412, the seal comes **about nine days** later, and the sowing is done in the second month of MR 413 — **twelve months** after the paper came back, not ten. **The figure locked in this file was *ten months*; it is now *twelve months*, and the bogus *twenty-two days between the request and the seal* (which matched nothing but Ottmar Kuez's twenty-two years at the bridge) is now *about nine days*.** Both were wrong in this file and both are corrected here as well as on the page, because the prose of Chs 1–110 is authoritative and this file had started to override it.
3. **Chapter 108 had Zofia Teran walk four miles and eleven switchbacks in two hours.** The volume says the road takes a day. **She now comes down on the twenty-seventh and sleeps on a bench on the floor of the seed house in the low ground, and Neris Kade comes down with her and sleeps in the low ground with her, and Kade is in the lane from the second bell.** Teran's own *a walk of two days* — which was always in the chapter — is now literally true: a day down and a day up. She goes up at the first bell in the morning, and the chapter's last paragraph is a whole day of daylight in front of her instead of eleven switchbacks before the dark.
4. **Chapter 110 asserted a promise that no scene ever showed being made, and dated it three weeks before an eleven-week gap.** **Sefa's request is now on the page, in her own mouth, and dated: she asked him on the nineteenth of the tenth month, on that stair, with Rell standing behind her and a satchel he did not open, and he has been there every morning since.** The *three weeks* is gone, and so is *I have stopped thanking you for it, because you are not doing it for me*, which is what the interval actually earned.
5. **Chapter 109 called Wenna Drel a boy of sixteen.** **She is a girl of sixteen, and she is a girl of sixteen everywhere else in one hundred and ten chapters**, including the sentence in `chapter-0073.md` that the first draft of this volume copied. **The state files never identified the figure at all, which is why the collision survived a review; they identify her now, in the locked-state list in `state/current.md` and in the repair note below.**
6. **Chapter 101's card requires Marek to ask the haulier who told him the road was open, and the haulier had already answered it six lines earlier**, so the question landed on nothing, and the card's *the office is not in this basin* only half-landed because the depot was named and never placed. **The repair takes the answer out of the lantern paragraph** — the man with the lantern now says only *he had been told* and *he did not know* — **and the question gets a second question behind it.** Duss's answer is the clerk with the good coat on the low ground, the twenty-first of the ninth month and *the rate is not the rate*; then Marek asks *which office cleared the road*, and Duss gives the finding the card wanted: **a depot up in the hills that he cannot name, in a basin where he knows every depot a cart can reach, and it is not one of them.** The clerk's name is the name; the office's is not. Nothing is gained for anybody.
7. **Chapter 101's card requires Oren Pike and he was not in the chapter**, and the stand-in for him was an unnamed man with four children, which is not Pike and is not true of him. **Pike is now on the weighbridge at the third watch, with his house's share of the forty carts, and he says the card's three things** — the fall is ordinary, the basin is fed, and a man who reads marks all night is a man with nothing to do — **and he is right, and Marek answers *you are right about all of that*, and Pike cannot produce one figure that is wrong.** The card's resistance and its *Marek cannot produce a single figure that is wrong* are both delivered now. **Pike's established facts are used and nothing is invented: thirty years on the floor, eleven names on a slate, no children.**
8. **Bold is new to this manuscript and this batch had 136 `**` markers in the prose**, including whole clauses inside dialogue and full sentences in narration, against zero in Chapters 1–100. **Every `**` has been stripped from all ten chapters. Single-asterisk italics are house style — 374 spans in Volume 01 and 206 in Volume 02 — and are untouched.** This is the whole of the change: the emphasis is now carried by clause position and by the sentence before it, which is how the rest of the manuscript does it.
9. **Chapter 108 had Neris Kade say the same sentence twice** — *I have read it in a room off a seed room as well as in this one* at the door and *I have read it in a shed off a seed room as well* four lines after the chapter's best line. **The repeat is cut and the guardrail's cap of four lines between them is now two speeches.** The first statement stands, where it is doing work.

### The should-fix items, closed

- **Ch 103's `one hundred and twenty-two tray-hours a day` was a unit error** — sixty-one trays twice a day is a hundred and twenty-two tray-workings and not hours — **and it was credited to a woman who had stood at the back of the yard and said nothing.** The figure is now **one hundred and twenty-two trays a day, thirty-seven days, in the dark**, said by the keeper of the bench, and the woman with the sand trays on her knees says the same thing in her own words an hour later and gets nothing for it. **Every state file that carried *tray-hours* has been corrected, and the created-figures list now reads *trays*, which is what it is.**
- **Ch 103 inverted the card's resistance.** The card is *Fisk will not have her bench in a room with a queue outside it* and the chapter had her demanding exactly that. **She now refuses it out loud, before anybody asks her**, and the yard overrules its own keeper inside a minute without counting, and the queue cost still lands afterward as an accepted consequence. The card's two correct positions are now two people saying *no* about different things in the same nine minutes.
- **Ch 106's four instruments against the outline's three.** The outline says *three marks in one hand*, the Ch 117 card says *the same hand on three instruments*, and **Ch 106 puts four on the table and Ch 104 supports the fourth.** The prose is self-consistent and supported by the chapter before it, so **the prose wins, as the batch-0002 prompt itself says the prose wins, and the count of four is now recorded here so that no one has to guess.** The four are: the bundle of nine sheets and the schedule of fifty-two lines; the board over the door of the shed on the low-ground road; the haulier's contract for barley out of the second terrace; and the inside of the lid of the crate of two hundred and eleven sheets. **`workspace/volume-03/batch-0002/PROMPT.md` has been given the fourth and the handoff note.**
- **Ch 110's *a board of five somewhere*.** The volume's board of five is the censuring panel in Crown March, whose papers are never shown. **The granary office's board at Verrow is a room of eleven men, which is what Ch 105 says, and that is what Ch 110 now says.**
- **Ch 107's *six weeks* for a form dated from the sixth of the ninth.** The figure is removed rather than corrected, because the batch does not count days across a month boundary anywhere and there is no correct answer to give. **Rell now says he has had the form since he came down the river**, which is true and is a better sentence.
- **Ch 105's haulier's contract was *four months ago* while Ch 101 and Ch 106 both place it in the growth month.** Same document, two ages. **Ch 105 now says the growth month.** Separately, **Ch 106's *I have been assuming those were yours for about a month* was a second ambiguity on the same clause, and is now *since about the time I started coming down to that stair*, which dates Marek's assumption instead of the paper.**
- **Ch 110's cargo clause mashed three objects into one** — a coil, a tarpaulin and a box — **and Rennick's *that box wants a lid and a nail* depended on the mash.** The barge now carries **about nine hundred sacks of barley and a box on the deck under a tarpaulin with a coil of paper in it, and the box was not lashed well**, and the tarpaulin comes off four feet where the box was and the lid is not on the box.
- **Ch 101 ran bells and watches in the same scene without fixing the relation.** The bridge is now counted in watches and not in bells, in one paragraph, near the top: **the bridge is worked all night and the town is not, there are four watches between the eleventh bell and the second one, and a cart that has come down in the dark is over the deck in about the third.** The card mixes them too and the card is not edited; the chapter wins and the mix is recorded here.
- **Titles differ from the cards and the next batch reads the cards.** **Ch 102 is *Eleven Switchbacks* and not *The Road and the Four Terraces*; Ch 107 is *The Twenty-Fourth* and not *The Clause, Signed in Advance*.** The written titles are now carried into `workspace/volume-03/batch-0002/PROMPT.md`.
- **Three created figures were on the page and not in the created-figures list, which claimed to be exhaustive:** **about nine hundred sacks** on the down barge, **nine hundred people in the basin** in Rell's own mouth, and **nine and two, the price of flour**, in Margit Sova's mouth. All three are now in the list with a note on why the flour price is not a barley price. **The list's *about one hundred and ten people in the Gate yard* matched nothing on the page and is struck**; the chapter says eighty in the lane, about twenty in the shed and about eighteen in the room.
- **Length is not cut.** The batch was over the plan before this pass and the review noted it without asking for a cut; cutting 2,000 words out of a finished batch to hit a number would break complete scenes. **The count is retaken from the files after this pass and recorded in `state/current.md`, and it is higher than it was, because this pass added a Pike scene, a Sefa scene and the two-question repair in Ch 101 and removed almost nothing.** The overage is disclosed, not rounded down.

### Two operational notes, recorded and not acted on

- **`state/phase-ledger.json` still reads `phase-000-bootstrap`, status `planned`, attempts 0**, after thirty commits and eleven completed phases. **It is controller-owned and this pass did not touch it.** Anything that reads it resumes at bootstrap.
- **`workspace/continuation/next-0001/` has no `.done` marker and sorts before `workspace/volume-03/batch-0002/`**, and the dispatcher takes the first `PROMPT.md` in sorted order that has no marker, so the next dispatch will re-run this batch instead of writing 111–120. **`scripts/novel_runner.sh` writes the marker when a phase closes and is controller-owned; this pass did not write a marker by hand and did not touch the script.** A reviewer should confirm the marker lands before the next dispatch.

### What did not change

**No chapter was restarted. No planned plot was moved.** Movement I still ends on the twenty-eighth of the tenth month with the register's hole the size of a terrace and a rota with nothing at the head of it, and the batch still crosses the year and still closes on a man with no office reading a number out loud on a river stair and then going up a road. There is still no villain, no false instrument, no fixed advance, no price for barley, no Ledger Sight panel, no Boundary Hold, no Season Hand, and no answer to the missing year. **The volume's ending is untouched.**

## VOLUME 03, BATCH 0002 (Chapters 111–120, Movement II — Who Signed It)

Prose is authoritative over the batch cards wherever they disagree. Chapters run from the seventh of the second month, MR 414, to the fourth of the growth month, with the season works made in the second month as the spine. No event is placed after the twentieth of the growth month. No other month's length is stated. No days are counted backwards across a month boundary.

### The batch's two discoveries, both institutional and neither a villain

- **A period can be put on any day inside it (Ch 111).** Sunn's pattern book holds four years of wax impressions ordered by his own months with the instruction's date beside each; about four in any month carry no day because the instruction carried none. **A mark is made out of an instruction, the instruction lives in a house nine days up the coast, it is kept until the tide and then burned, and about nine hundred marks a year leave no record of who produced them.** Marek writes to the harbour for a pound — *yes*, *no*, or *the name is not on the list*, in about six weeks — and the letter sits on a shelf for the tide of the ninth. The list is a door and not a rescue and is never used cheaply in this batch.
- **The same hand is a form, not a forger (Ch 117).** The crate lid's handwritten mark and the seasonal form's printed head sit side by side in the same four parts at the same distance from the edge. Sedge: the form's mark is a head set in type as furniture of the run, on every run longer than the printer's ledger goes back. Kelb: a printed mark and a written mark cannot be told apart by looking, because the written one was copied off the printed one and the paper does not remember which. **A standing authority on a printed form means a signature is a thing that can be produced without a person, and the machinery that copies a form is the same machinery that copies a hand.** The duplicate pulled on the taking press becomes a document only by Sedge's name at the foot — that is the moment a copy stops being a copy. **Nobody in Morrowfen is shown a Tide Reckoning mark that is wrong, because nobody in Morrowfen can read one.**

### What a projection is (Chs 112, 118 — the volume's third and fourth practice chapters)

- **From the inside, by the man who has to type it.** A projection is a granary's forward estimate entered in the year before the yield, because a book has to balance in the year it is balancing: a wrong number is arithmetic and is corrected in ink with two initials, and a blank says the office cannot do its job. **Petrus Lenge, thirty-three, six years at Verrow and four on the column, is the mechanism and not the villain, is right that a blank column is worse than a wrong one, and does not know what a class is.** In Ch 112 he carries last season's figure forward into the eleventh space for the terrace that has not returned; in Ch 118 at the second bell he writes nine districts out of a slate keyed by number and not by name, carries the third terrace forward a second year, rules the foot with no total. **Neither chapter restates Ch 105: Ch 105 is the definition given to Marek by the officer, Ch 112/118 is the same fact from inside by a man with a pen.** The ten-year mean is on a table in the first office, never copied in front of anybody, never produced, because the moment it can be produced it is an offer and an offer can be sued.

### The season works, the advance, and the set-off (Chs 113–114)

- **Nine lines, the ninth empty (Ch 113).** Made on the eleventh of the second month in a loft at Hartlow by four people and a knife. Line one cutting, two the drain going in the third month, three the shared threshing floor, four the road with the culvert mouth four feet too low, five a water, six and seven two store rooms, eight a seed store, nine the fourth-terrace seed store ruled and empty because Janko cut his own name out rather than write it and have it struck. **Tomas chalks his bill on the board in the margin against lines two and four — no date, no office — and it is a bill and not a favour, he is not a hero, and nobody thanks him.** He and Marek clear four feet of drain with a bar for an hour; only the lever under the lip is craft and Marek could not do it in a year.
- **The advance and the set-off (Ch 114).** Eleven thousand and some hundreds, already spent, served on the sixth of the third month at a holding gate with no desk, worked out on the back of a depot envelope on a flat stone. **A set-off takes seed eleven months before the seed wants to be in the ground, and there is no form in this kingdom for taking money back; the exact figure is never fixed in any mouth.** The party is the number, a holding cannot be present, the holder's name goes on the line because a set-off must have somebody on it, and a charge on a holding has no box. The notice goes on the seed house nail and forty people read it in a day.

### The exercise book, the list of names, and the board (Chs 115–116, 119)

- **Zofia Teran reads at the fourth bell (Ch 115).** A school exercise book with a door, a day, what was said, and who said it — the best record of the season anybody has and not a record, for five stated reasons, the fifth said twice. She names nine people with one thing said at each door, refuses *which nine*, and owns the garden-door entry about the road opened on purpose. **She says she will not be the woman who came down the hill, and nobody makes her — Janko's refusal in Ch 102 stands.** The chapter is carried by the yard in it: rope, child's coat, two men with a bar in a cut, a man owning his sentence and saying leave it.
- **The Chancellor's clerk comes for names (Ch 116).** Emmerich Tace, forty-four, off the Crown March post with a case he is told not to set on the trestle, wants a schedule of persons by name in a fortnight. **Chancellor Ysara Venn enters as a want and never as a body: she wants to know whether there is a room behind the paper to send a person to.** The list he makes is four names — the woman with the cloth, the examiner named at the door, the man with no office, then Petar Duss, who walks down the lane and owns his sentence aloud. **A list of names is unobtainable and the reason is not courage: forty lines on a wall are the people who asked questions, and there are four hundred and some people in those terraces and about nine on any paper.** Marek writes Duss's sentence that night as a private person with no office, from a conversation on a bridge, not evidence, not producible, tells nobody. Kern from the step: the yard refused twice on its own rule, cost its water on bench mornings, lost about nine people nobody can name — and is not thanked.
- **Harrow's board sits again (Ch 119).** The sixteenth of the rains month, nine in a room built for thirty, **the Trust's seat empty for the fifth time**, the Chair not voting in a money matter, no clerk, minutes in longhand at about four minutes a page, thanked by nobody. The four terms stand; Vendl declines money a third time in the first person. Asked whether an unreadable mark is a witness, the board writes that it was unable to find upon the instrument — **and the Chair adds the volume's institutional sentence in a minute that goes into a locked box in a drawer where nobody will be shown it: a mark that no officer can read is not a witness.** Kern takes term four in his own words at a cost: the yard's twice-refused rule is now in a Crown March record where a man in a good coat can produce it at the yard in nine months. The second use of term four in a volume is a cost, not a victory.

### Rell, right and wrong in one sentence (Ch 120)

- **The fourth of the growth month, the seed house with the lamp on.** Rell measures kept-back seed as *present* and not *sufficient*, sits on a sack, and says the halves himself first: right that the basin feeds three regions on a season granted in writing for good reasons and nobody stole a harvest; wrong that the ten-year mean is a measurement, because it is a number made out of ten numbers he read once and cannot produce, and the carried-forward third-terrace figure proves it. **Taking the advance back is correct, cheap, quick, and puts eleven thousand pounds of bad autumn onto four terraces — and the only reason it is not the answer is that the money is already in seed and labour and cannot be un-spent.** He writes the seed house and Hana Belic into his own book to send a letter with a price for a shilling that nobody must answer; it is not a favour and not a finding. **He is not censured here, does not resign, learns nothing about a mark, is not softened, is not a villain and not a martyr.** Marek holds back the sentence about the form and is not asked for it.

### Required character movement, recorded

- **Marek holds a thing back from Tomas and it costs him, and Tomas finds out (Ch 113).** He has carried Lenge's carried-forward figure since the sixth, waits for a form that cannot be improved on, and gives a technically accurate answer about where the head is set when asked which column and whose number. Tomas hears the evasion — *you have known that since the sixth* — and does not raise it again all season. He is not redeemed and not theatrically exposed; he was going to tell somebody on the road and did not.
- **Neris Kade is in the same scene as Marek at most twice, the second smaller and quieter.** Ch 118: she enters the projection office at the third bell with returns, certifies four sheets, says the third terrace sent her nothing either and her report goes nowhere, and leaves; no first names, no thanks, no warmth. Ch 119: she is present holding the four-figure sheet and speaks nothing. **Volume 04 still owns the first honest disagreement and the shared practical method; this batch takes neither.**
- **Sedge keeps his line and no number.** He is a third-grade clerk who has been in the building long enough that twice in it; no service length is given anywhere. **Kelb is a clerk other people ask things of now** — nine duplicates in a month — is not asked about the hold, and his left hand is a nine-year-old's and is not his. The pen that wrote the yard's four lines is never named.

### Budgets and guardrails, verified from the files after the fix pass

- **Length, counted with `wc -w` from the files with the title line included, after the fix pass: 25,606 words across ten chapters, inside the 24,000–28,000 plan.** Distribution: Ch 111 2,778 · Ch 112 2,710 · Ch 113 2,703 · Ch 114 2,200 · Ch 115 2,446 · Ch 116 3,176 · Ch 117 2,432 · Ch 118 2,177 · Ch 119 2,651 · Ch 120 2,333. Shortest 2,177, longest 3,176.
- **Kalendar is clean after the fix pass.** A case-insensitive sweep for the twelve real-world month names and seven weekdays returns nothing. Three uses of *October* were written and caught (two in Ch 112, one in Ch 118) and are now *the tenth month*. Every *March* in the batch is *Crown March* or the established *standard March practice*; the verb *march* never appears. *May* appears only as the lowercase verb. The growth month's twenty-day length is not restated; no other month's length is stated; no days are counted backwards across a month boundary. One cross-boundary quantification (*about a month and a half* in Ch 111) was removed.
- **Panel budget: ZERO Ledger Sight panels in ten chapters** (no `>` panels anywhere in 111–120). **No Boundary Hold and no Season Hand is used, wished for, hinted at or mentioned; the founding standing hold is not referred to. Marek's forearm is not described, referred to or looked at.** No second person decodes either vault-book entry. Tamsin is not asked anything. Pella Rusk's surety cell is untouched. Marek's unanswered schoolroom answer stays unanswered. The Kallow roll and district four are not mentioned. Wenna Drel is not in the batch and is not asked about anything.

- **No villain, no new faction, no conviction, no dissolution, no closed office.** Lenge is the mechanism and is right about a ledger. Rell is right and wrong and says which half is which. Sunn will not swear and does not unsay it, and no obligation is created on him. Duss is written down by a private person and becomes no witness against anybody. The register's rule is never extended, no bare column is named, the fifth line stays empty and the sixth stays empty for ever.
