## VOLUME 03, BATCH 0004 (Chapters 131–140, Movement IV — The Named Weight)

Prose is authoritative over the batch cards wherever they disagree. **Chapters run from the twelfth of the harvest month to the twentieth of the ninth month, MR 414, and the ten chapters are NOT in date order: 131 (12th harvest), 132 (14th), 133 (16th), 134 (18th), 135 (19th), 139 (20th), 136 (3rd–4th ninth), 137 (9th), 138 (11th), 140 (20th).** Ch 139 sits between Ch 135 and Ch 136 because `outline/volume-03.md` locks the new form to *the harvest month of MR 414*, and the form is cut **because of** the three terraces, which is the batch's causal spine. **No month length is stated anywhere in the batch and no days are counted backwards across a month boundary.** No real-world month name and no weekday appears in the ten chapters; every *March* in the batch is *Crown March*.

### The batch's three findings, in order, and they are all about a person

1. **A counterweight is not named by being written down. A counterweight is held (Ch 131).** Found in a yard, with a bar that does not sit flat, a padlock, a chain, a key on a nail and a door that has dropped a quarter of an inch in nine years. Milan Vray, the yard-man, refuses twice — once to be the room, once to be a man who explains a mechanism. The chain of hands is the argument: **a bar is a thing that can be lifted, so there is a padlock; a padlock is the reason there is a key; a key is the reason there is a keyholder; a keyholder is a person; a person can be asked.** And the refusal is correct for a reason that is the volume's own: a name on a list turns a store into a thing somebody is responsible for and a yard-man into an office, so **a counterweight is held by a person with a key on a nail and nowhere else, and there is no form in this kingdom in which anybody may be given that office.** A holding is not a household and a class is not a person, and a keyholder is neither of those things.
2. **The naming holds in one terrace, is refused in one, and cannot be done at all in one (Chs 132–135).** First terrace: told, before sealing, on a threshing floor, and **it holds** — and no one is thanked, converted, or made a supporter. Second terrace: refused, and the refusal is right. Third terrace: **unreached**, because the only line of the season works that works with a lock is the ninth and the ninth is empty. **Three households told or not told, one of them not told on purpose**: the four people on the third terrace are not told because there is no keyholder and because Marek does not go down the lane fork, and that decision is his and is made on purpose and is on the page.
3. **A rate is a charge on a thing that cannot be present anywhere, and a box with a class in it does not get a person in it (Chs 136, 138, 139, 140).** Ch 136 is the institutional beat and is a fact about a box. Ch 138 is the Share's own correct mechanism with no person in it. Ch 139 is the form. Ch 140 is the form used twice and not enough.

### The three terraces, and what each one is

- **First terrace (Ch 132, the fourteenth of the harvest month).** The **first time in this basin that anybody is told what a counterweight is before it is sealed.** It holds. **Mileva Vukic, thirty-eight, not a member of anything, and the household the counterweight already found** (brackish spring, a pear out in leaf twice, a boundary stone nine inches out, a heifer that has not come back in heat — Ch 124, none of it happened yet). The floor is the first terrace's own, not the shared floor, and nine people stop for two seconds and then go back to it. **She says no.** *What I am going to do is leave the jar on the rail… it is not acceptance, and it is not a favour to you.* And the thing he did not come for: **she will not say the paper did her spring, because if she says it she will have said it first, in nine years, to a man with a figure, and she will not have earned it and her daughter will have heard her.** He must not say it anywhere either. **Her one question: how long does it last, because she would like to know how long she has to be the woman with the jar.** *About eleven weeks… the woman who has been in a room about it says that is about how long anything of this shape survives being told once.* **Eleven weeks is the batch's clock and it is Vukic's word, not an office's.** She is not grateful, not a convert, has read nothing, and nobody is going to explain anything to her twice.
- **Second terrace (Ch 133, the sixteenth).** The refusal, and the plainest sentence in the batch: **"I was not in the room. You were in a ditch."** Grounds that cannot be argued with, in her order: (1) **he will not write the nine words in the exercise book**, because the day they are in a book in this valley somebody says *the second terrace said the nine words* in a room and the room owns it for ever; (2) the ninth of the growth month, the third bell, a lamp he did not light, nine rows on a shoulder above a drain, the ground a woman of thirty-one's and held in her mother's name, and he asked neither of them and one of them does not know it happened at all; (3) **the person he would have to ask is a woman of fifty-eight who holds the second terrace's store with a lock, and asking her is asking the woman, and asking the woman is asking Teran in front of nine people.** **CORRECTED AT THE REPAIR PASS: an earlier draft made Teran the mother of Stana Peric and said *my daughter's drill rows*, which put the fifty-eight-year-old holder of the eighth line in two places at once. Ch 133 now says the ground is a woman of thirty-one's, held in her mother's name, and identifies the mother as the mother of the woman whose ground it is. The eight-line holder is Stana Peric's mother, fifty-eight, and nobody in four terraces counts her, and that is unchanged from Ch 113 / 122 / 124 / 134.** Zofia Teran is forty-four and keeps the school exercise book and has refused twice before and is refusing a third time. **She refused to be the woman who came down the hill in Ch 102 and that stands and stays refused.** The season goes on anyway: nine rows in a shock carried off a shoulder on shoulders to a headland in about nine hours, and the barley does not know what was said in a yard. **Nobody says the word *failure*, and nobody treats the first terrace's holding as a success either.**
- **Third terrace (Ch 134, the eighteenth).** **Cannot be reached, and the reason is the outline's own: a counterweight has to be held by somebody, and the only line of the season works that works with a lock is the ninth, and the ninth is empty because Janko Vrabec cut his own name out of it in the second month rather than write it and have it struck.** **No name from the third terrace appears in Chapters 131–140 and nobody is sent there and nobody goes.** The chapter's load-bearing act is Marek **going down the ladder, out to the lane fork, standing at an unfinished stone wall for as long as it takes to count nine, and turning round.** The reason he gives is his own and not a rule: *a man with a season in him does not get to interview the people it came out of*, which he said in a lane in the growth month and said again and this time it did not feel like a rule. **He also refuses to point the season at the third terrace — the first time in four months that the thing he was carefully given is no use to him at all** — and says so out loud once, to Ruta Vrabec, in a loft, because a thing said in a yard to the wrong person is worth four things said in a room. **Ruta Vrabec is fifty-eight, one of the four women over fifty on the Hartlow roll, does the cooking, and Janko Vrabec eats at her table; she is not a keeper of anything and has never been asked to be.** The chapter's working fact at the end has nothing to do with the counterweight: **the kept-back seed of four terraces comes up a road in sacks in about a month, and the bar, the hasp and the padlock are on a door whose key is in a coat, and none of it can be entered, served, sworn to, or shown.**

### Janko Vrabec, in one page (Ch 135, the nineteenth of the harvest month)

- **Third refusal, differently, and he is permitted to do it in one page.** The refusal itself takes about a second and a half (**"No"**) and the sentence that carries it is **nine words: "A dying man is not a party to anything."** He had wanted to say it since the growth month and gives no second speech, and the narrator says the refusal is one page rather than explaining it.
- **The chapter is not a table and a speech.** Before the ask, **he fills cones from Hartlow's own stock for an hour with Ruta Vrabec at the other end of the bench, and is slow because he counts, and she says *You will be here till noon at that, and the count is not in the cone*.** Inside the speech, **he gets up, opens the seed room door, stands in it with his hand on the coat on the hook, does not take the key off the hook, and shuts it again.** Objects: the lamp with a very small wick in it, turned up a quarter turn and put back a quarter turn, which is the whole of what a man does with a lamp he will not leave at full and will not let go out; the chamois he is not using; the roll off its nail and a thumbnail down six lines.
- **Nothing comes of his illness except that it will not be in the room.** The illness enters only as the fact that a name in the ninth line would have to be struck in the eleventh month, and **there is no box in this kingdom for striking a man's name off a list.** So the ninth line would become a hole with a scrape through it, which is worse than an empty line, and the empty line at least was honest — and a dead man cannot be asked whether he will bear it, cannot say no, and cannot be told afterwards. *Three days of a road, and you have built a room with a person in it, and you have put a grave in it.*
- **He is not the ninth line's keyholder and is not made into one.** The key does not move hands, the ninth line is not filled, and Marek does not propose a keyholder.
- **Marek has a second question and does not ask it** — *is there anybody in this valley who could be asked instead of you* — because it was for him and not for Janko, and a man refused four times in five days does not get to put his own refusal inside somebody else's mouth, and **neither of them says one word about the not asking.**
- **The roll's count.** Six **lines**, not six women: four women over fifty, one a child, and the sixth a season examiner's name who is not a member and has never been up the road. **Two people on that paper will bear what the MR 412 paper bears — one of them is on the board and is fifty-eight, and the other is nineteen, is a person, and is not a line on the roll because he is a man and there is no form for him either.** The nineteen-year-old is **Tomek, Janko's son** (Ch 102), and the fifty-eight-year-old is **Ruta Vrabec**; Marek is told the count and told not to write it down. **The count is the only countable counterweight in the volume and it does not help: *I can count and I cannot ask. Those are two different trades and I only have one of them and I have had it for thirty-one years.*** **A keeper of thirty-one years; fifty-eight; a keeper of the Hartlow roll. Not a symbol, not a lesson, not an elegy, and not a door.**

### Ch 136 — the fourth box, and a filing clerk at Verrow

- **The volume's institutional beat and the whole of Ch 104's find coming back from the other end.** The Trust seasonal advance form has **four boxes and a fifth at the foot**; the **fourth box is the counterweight in type** and the **fifth at the foot is a return** (Ch 104). The finding: **the fourth box is stock.** It is the same on every sheet of the run, it is filled with a hand-entered district, and it is right every time, and **a clerk reads a form by the heads of its boxes and a head is a kind, and a kind is not a hole, and you do not go looking for a hole — you go looking for a figure that is wrong, and the fourth box does not have a figure that can be wrong.** So sixty-one readings is not sixty-one chances to notice, and the reason is not carelessness.
- **The small institutional fact that carries it: the fourth box is the first box with the person taken out of it.** A name is in the first box sixty-one times, four inches away, doing a different job, and nobody has ever moved a name from one to the other — not because a clerk decided not to, but because a name in a party box about a counterweight would assert that a person is a counterweight, and **no office in this kingdom has a box for that, and the man who put it in would be certifying a thing he cannot check and cannot read.**
- **DIVERGENCE FROM THE BATCH PROMPT, RECORDED NOT HIDDEN.** The prompt assigned **Josip Malnar, forty-nine**, as *the filing clerk at Verrow*. **That is not possible and the chapter does not do it: Chs 75, 77, 83 and 88 establish Malnar as the keeper of the Under-Row, forty-one acres on a middle terrace of the Red Uplands, with about two hundred old apple trees, a press and a windfall heap, who bought a claim on the low ground's flood quarters for four hundred and ten pounds and who has twice said in his own yard, unasked, that he has not read the schedule and does not want to.** A man who has not read a schedule cannot have read sixty of them, and the prose of Chapters 1–130 is authoritative over the prompt. **A new name is used: Damir Pribis, forty-nine, eleven years opening the basket at the Verrow depot, who describes himself as the man who opens the basket, is not a third-grade clerk, is not a villain, and believes Marek after four seconds of reflection and says so would like it entered.** **Malnar does not appear in Chapters 131–140 and no claim on him is altered.** A reviewer should treat Pribis as an addition, not a replacement, and should check that Malnar is untouched in Movements IV and V.
- **Sedge's third box fact is confirmed from inside, and the fifth box's hand and Grale's cut-order suspicion are untouched.** The pen is not named. The depot's figure, the ten-year mean, is still on a table in a room in that building and still cannot be produced. **The only other thing in the chapter takes nine seconds and confirms Ch 130: the coming season's projection column has two figures on one line, the ten-year and the truth with Lenge's initials against it** — and neither can be used, neither can leave the room, and Marek wanted a figure since the ninth of the second month and got the only one anybody in this basin has ever been given and can do nothing whatever with it. **Petrus Lenge does not appear and is not spoken to.**
- **The harbour letter is used and not used.** Asked whether a translator's name is ever checked at Verrow, Pribis says *we are not the harbour and we cannot read one and have never claimed to* — and **Marek does not tell him that this is the answer to a letter on a shelf above a chandler's shop since the seventh of the second month, and works out for the second time since the growth month that a thing not coming back is not a no, and does not go and look. NO NUMBER OF WEEKS IS STATED ANYWHERE IN Ch 136**: the letter is thirteen weeks old at Ch 126 and much older by the ninth month, and neither figure is a figure the calendar will carry.

### Wenna Drel (Ch 137, the ninth of the ninth month)

- **Sixteen, on her mother's line, not a member, a person, and not mentioned in Chapters 101–130.** **She is asked nothing in Chapter 137 by anybody including the narrator: the chapter contains no question mark.** The one thing in the volume nobody may ask her is **whether the balance on the fourth line from the end is hers**, and the arrangement is stated out loud by Mirren Fisk before Marek has got nine feet off the door — *not a rule and not a favour, and I am telling you so that nobody in this room has to be careful and so that nobody in this room gets praised for it later* — and **not because she is sixteen, but because a balance belongs to a ground, the ground is the woman's, the woman is a person, the girl is a person, and the one place the two get added is on the Share's own rule, which is not asked anything.**
- **The correct handling is a room that works around her and does not tiptoe, and it is played twice as an action with no commentary.** Petrik asks *Whose tray is the twenty-third for the last day* and Fisk answers at the ordinary volume, without turning round and without looking at the bench — *The fourth line from the end. It is the same as it was yesterday. Put the slate down and bring me the twine* — and Petrik puts the slate down, and the girl does not stop and does not look up, and three of the four people in the building would tell you about the twine. **Fisk does not lower her voice, does not send anybody out, does not move her, does not say *she is young* or *when she is older*, does not put her body between the girl and the question, and nobody turns round afterwards and looks at where she is sitting.** The sand on the floor is swept at the beginning of the day and not the end, and Fisk says so out loud **in front of Marek** so that he hears it from her rather than working it out and asking — the same move, twice in one chapter.
- **She does one ordinary piece of work and says one ordinary thing about it.** She fills cones at the bench in a work-cap with a name in the band, and at the sixth bell she says, without stopping and without looking up: *twenty-two are the same ones as yesterday and the twenty-three wants turning at the fourth bell, not the third, or you get the middle of it wet* — because the board has said the fourth for the first tray and the third for the rest since the second month and somebody has been copying it down. Petrik turns it at the fourth. **It is right.** That is the whole of what she says in that building that day and nobody thanks her for it.
- **She is not an oracle, is not asked anything, and says no true thing that fixes a plot. She is not Lio Vale, and Lio Vale is not in the chapter, and nobody writes on a slate.** **Her slate is on a trestle in the Gate yard in Tallow Ward and Bessok Tan has it in a drawer; it has not been written on since the seed month and nobody writes on it here.** Her mother is forty-one, ill since the flood year, did not come on the ninth, and **no name is invented for her and none may be.** **The caps are the chapter's instrument: sixty-one work-caps on a nail by the door, each with a name in the band in Fisk's hand from the seed month, because a cap is a thing a person wears and not a thing a person holds, and a cap with a name in the band is the only thing in this basin that looks exactly like an answer and is not one.** Her cap is on the bench; her mother's is on the nail behind it; nobody has moved either and nobody has asked.

### The Share's own rule, the rate, and the form (Chs 138–140)

- **Ch 138 is the volume's best example of a correct mechanism with no person in it, and neither Fisk nor the rule is turned into a fool or a villain.** **The Share's own rule, nineteen years old, not written by Fisk and not unwritable by her:** a lot of ours that does not come up becomes eleven days of labour divided across the members by trays, and if a member's own tray is the one that failed then it is her tray and her days; then a third of another member's kept-back, Ansgar Drel having carried his down from the top on his own back with nobody asking; then **the balance as a rate on the member's ground, and there is no form in this kingdom in which a rate on a member's ground goes, and it goes nowhere.** The eleven lines are the eleven whose trays came up as sand; eight other members owed days on a sheet of days and not on that, and Fisk has never been able to make that sound right to anybody and it is right.
- **The piece of sack: eleven lines, nothing at the head of it, nothing at the foot, no column of names, no total, each line a household's rate (Volume 02's object, in a keeper's house).** It went into the back of the bench book under the date and then home in an apron, because a sheet with nothing at the head of it does not stay in a building. **It has been in a drawer under a folded apron since the third day of the harvest month last year and Fisk has never taken it out of the front room in front of anybody** — **CORRECTED AT THE REPAIR PASS: a first draft had it two years old, which is not a figure the calendar carries. It is a year old and the eleven lines are this season's.** She shows it to Marek once, unprompted, and says *do not touch it, and there is no order on that and there is no reason for it*.
- **A rate is a charge on a ground, and the ground is a piece of the low ground with a roll number on it, and a number about a ground cannot be told anything or asked anything or be in a room — so a man puts a name in, because a charge has to have somebody on it or it is a note.** Fisk says this against Marek's own sentence from Ch 114, which is on a nail in a seed house four miles and eleven switchbacks up that road, and she has read it more than once and cannot improve it. **This is the exact mechanism Lenge described at a holding gate in the third month, and it is the same mechanism on a different scale, and neither is a fraud.**
- **The batch's new sentence, in a second mouth and only a second: *a counterweight is not a receipt*.** Stated by **Milan Vray** in Ch 131 and by **Mirren Fisk** in Ch 138, and in at most two mouths in the batch. A receipt is a thing you hand a man when he has handed you something; nothing in this basin gave anything to anybody, so there is nothing to receipt, so there is no paper to hand a woman saying *here is what you gave and here is what it cost*; and the only thing anybody can do is write a figure on a piece of sack, carry it home in an apron, and know that in about two years a woman will be standing where you are standing with a number she cannot pay in money and does not have in seed, and she will be told it is a rule and she will say yes because there is nothing else to say — **and that is how it was decided and by whom and on what day is nobody's business to say**, which is Fisk's own sentence from the growth month, restated inside her own mouth.
- **Kade's one co-presence in the batch, and the sixth in the volume, is in Ch 138 and it is a working one.** She comes in with a satchel and four sheets, puts the satchel on the table, and asks whether she may put her four counter questions to the piece of sack. **She does not pick it up.** *May I ask, is that your line* — and she stops, because there is nothing at the head of it to ask the question of. *That is not a fault in the question. That is the whole of what the question is, and I have been asking it for two years at about nine counters and I have never once had a thing in front of me that I could not put it to, and I have just met one.* She does not ask who wrote it or when, she does not take the sack, she says she is going up the street to certify four sheets at the ordinary speed and good morning, and **nobody thanks her and Marek does not thank her and there is no note of it anywhere.** No first names, no warmth, nothing built, no method shared, no Venn list, no shared practical method. **Volume 04 still owns the first honest disagreement about who should bear a risk and the shared practical method, and this batch takes neither.**
- **Ch 139 — the form, cut in the copy room in the harvest month as `outline/volume-03.md` locks it.** **Five ruled lines, no boxes, and no box for the ground.** Cut by Sedge, in the same copy room and the same manner as the register's form, in the afternoon of the twentieth of the harvest month, with a carpenter's pencil, a straight-edge, a knife and a piece of window glass worn round at one end that nobody else in that room may touch. **Lines not boxes is deliberate and defended: a box is a thing a person can be put in, a line is a thing a person has to write in, and every bad instrument he has ever filed got filed in a box with a kind on it and the kind is chosen by whoever cuts the form.** The four heads are: the thing in the person's own words; who holds the other end and since when; **the return in kind, in kind, and a figure is not a return in kind and a figure is not going in this line**; and anything anybody else has to say, which is very often nothing. **The fifth line has nothing at the head of it and neither Sedge nor Marek ever names it, and that is the batch's whole institutional point: a head is a kind, and if the kind is *ground* then Sedge has filed a charge against a class, done with a pencil and a straight-edge in a copy room with a lamp at his elbow, and nobody will ever be able to say who did it because there will be no name on it. The way he does not do it is by having no word there at all, and he has not been sure about it for a day and a half, and that is how he knows it is the right one and also how he knows it is the wrong one and he cannot separate the two and neither can Marek.** The spacing is the technique: the fifth line is ruled in four seconds, evenly, with the same straight-edge from the same edge of the sheet, because **if the fourth is tighter or the fifth is looser he has told the room which line he thought was the important one, and the only man qualified to say which is important is the person who fills it in, and he is not in this room and has not read it yet.** **It is not the yard's form and does not go near it, the yard's form has six lines and Sedge is not cutting a seventh, and this is a different form and may never be added to that one.**
- **Sedge's locked line, its third use, and it costs him.** *A form has to be cut by a man who does not know whether it is right.* **It comes out flat, and he stops, and the chapter says why: a man who has said a thing three times has stopped being able to say it fresh, and a man who has stopped being able to say a thing fresh is a man who ought not to be cutting anything at all, and he is cutting one, and he would like it said out loud in the room by somebody, because if nobody says it then it is a thing he has noticed on his own and he has never in his life been able to trust a thing he has noticed on his own.** The three uses are Ch 84 (a yard, a hundred and forty people, the best thing he has ever said), Ch 117 (a printer's shop, a hand flat on a margin) and Ch 139 (a copy room, a lamp, a man in a wet coat). **In Ch 140 he refuses to put it at the bottom of a piece of paper, because a line read out twice at the ordinary speed in the Gate shed is not a thing a man said in a room any more — it is a thing the room owns, and in about four years a man will produce it and say that the yard thought so.** **Sedge is given no service length anywhere in the batch; his own line is *I have been in this building long enough that twice in it*, and it is used in Chs 139 and 140.**
- **Ch 140 — the form used twice, two failures of scale, and a refusal to sign a finding.** **Two households, two attempts, at different hours on purpose, and both women stay.** The two failures are **different in kind and neither is anybody's fault**:
  - **Ilma Tresch, forty-one — the ground's scale.** She did the eleven days on ground that had already failed; a physician wrote her out of four of them and then, in the same afternoon, of the rest of them once; the certificate went to the clinic board as a cost and **the district's answer to a cost is a rate.** Her balance is the days she was written out of and it is all there is in it. She asks *Whose name goes against it*, is told she does, and says *Then that is right… so write it.* **Then she names the failure herself: there is going to be a rate on the low ground in the fourth month as well as on her lot, and the two sizes do not meet anywhere, so there will be two charges on one piece of dirt and nobody alive will ever be shown both of them at once, including her.** *That is not an objection. I am refusing nothing. I am telling you the size of it* — and she wants it minuted that she said the rule was right first.
  - **Hana Belic, thirty-four — the rule's scale.** She drew the eleventh tray and had worked it twice a day in the dark since the sixth of the ninth month; a physician wrote her out of **four tray-days in the harvest month and not of the other seven**, and then, in the growth month, a physician came back to a cough in the small of her back and wrote her out of **the evening trays for eleven days**; both certificates went to the board and the board entered them as costs. **Her balance is the eleven days and she did none of them and has not hidden that.** She asks the only question that matters — **who did the work that was not done** — and then says it herself: the trays are the Share's, the rule divides a failed lot across the members by trays, the days those eleven trays would have been are the days she was written out of, and those days went into a book at the clinic as a cost, and **there is no line on that paper for the Share, and none for the rule, and none for the certificate, and none for eleven trays.** *So there is one woman on here and one figure, and the woman did the trays and the figure is hers, and the eleven days are nobody's, and nobody's is what a Share is when a thing has to be put on a piece of paper.* She will say yes before she is asked and wants it noticed that saying yes to a form is not the same as saying the form is right.
  - **CORRECTION OF A PRE-EXISTING INCONSISTENCY, MADE IN THE PROSE AND NOT SILENTLY.** Ch 103 has Belic written out of **four tray-days and not of the other seven**; Ch 127 has a physician write her out of **the evening trays for eleven days**. These are read in this batch as **two certificates in two months — a first one in the harvest month of MR 413 and a second one on the nineteenth of the growth month of MR 414 — and both are on the page in Ch 140.** A later chapter may not collapse them into one certificate or into one number. **Ch 140 does not state a figure for Tresch's balance**, because Ch 73 gives a certificate of four days and a second certificate of the remaining days once and the arithmetic does not close, and a rate's figure is the Share's and is not on the paper.
  - **Sedge refuses to sign a finding, in three moves, and will not cut one:** a finding is a form and its head would be a kind — *a rate on a member's ground does not scale* — with a space in it where a person is supposed to be; **the man who cuts a form is the man who ought to be told it is not right, and being told is not the same as signing, and signing is what a man does to a form to make it into a document, and he has already made this one into a document twice this morning and it has cost two women a morning each**; and he will not put his own line at the bottom of a piece of paper to be read out twice at the ordinary speed. *Take your two sheets. Do not let anybody head them.* **Nobody thanks him and nobody is granted an office.**
- **The form was not produced, adopted, extended, or used a third time, and no finding was recorded.** The two filled sheets go face down under the slate. **The batch produces no procedure, no form, no rule, and no precedent, and the rate is going on that ground in the fourth month anyway — correctly, out of a rule nineteen years old, by a woman who has walked to every kitchen in this district and has told two of them the size of it and has been thanked by neither.**

### Marek, in this batch, and the retreat

- **His arc is a retreat from the thing that is actually his.** He has spent a season on one holding; Movement IV is about going to a whole terrace and saying *this is what is going to land on you*, **and the batch's finding is that this is not a bigger version of the same act. Saying a thing to nine people is not nine times saying it to one, the difference is not a matter of degree, and there is no hand in it at all.** He goes to three terraces and does not point once.
- **Season Hand is not used anywhere in Chapters 131–140, and its refusal is on the page twice:** in Ch 134 he could name a name on the third terrace and does not, and in Ch 139 he puts two fingers on the fifth line and there is nothing there to point anything at. **Nothing above Season Hand. No Boundary Hold is used, offered, wished for or hinted at, and the founding standing hold is spent and is not mentioned. The words *Ledger Sight*, *Boundary Hold*, *standing hold* and *Season Hand* do not appear in any of the ten chapters.**
- **He gains no office, no post, no pay, no vote, no right of entry, and no way of producing the yard's own refusal in any room.** The flat board with four ruled sheets stays under the trestle and is not produced; the certified duplicate under the trestle is not produced; **he is refused by the yard again in no chapter of this batch, because he does not go to the yard, and the four questions are used only as a thing he counts out on a trestle and is told are no use to him.** He never tells a room the yard's own method and nobody produces it in a room.
- **The three costs are all still on him and none is visible to a room:** a season of knowing a thing that is not his; a piece of somebody else's counterweight; two and a half days of a bank. He does not lose another and he does not recover any of them. **The physician's mark is on a clinic sheet in a bag in Rainmarket and neither he nor she has referred to it since Ch 127, and nobody decodes it, and nobody may.** **Marek's forearm is not described, referred to or looked at in any of the ten chapters and the word does not appear.**
- **The harbour letter is not paid, not opened, and not a way out.** Noted once, in Ch 136, and not looked for. **Petar Duss gets one line in Ch 140** — he will be in a room in the hills before the year turns and nobody has told Marek anything about it and Marek is not going with him. **He is still a factor's opponent and not a witness against anybody and has not become one.** Movement V owns him.

### Three terraces, one lock, one key, and the batch's shape

- **Ch 131 is the chapter that fails on purpose and it is carried by a bar, a wedge, a chain, a padlock, a keyway, a key on a nail and a table.** **Milan Vray, the yard-man, is the mouth of the batch's central finding and is not a mechanism-explainer: he stops Marek inside four minutes and says *I do not need to know what it is. I have moved seed in this yard for nineteen years and I have never once been asked what anything weighs against what.***
- **The engines of the ten, one each, none of which is a document read out to a room:** a bar that does not sit flat, a lock, a key on a nail, and a man who will not put his name in a line · a threshing floor, a jar on a rail, a spring, sheaves carried to a headland, and nine people who are not going to be told anything twice · a shock of barley, a shoulder above a new drain, a book off a shelf that stays shut, and a woman who says no in the plainest sentence in the batch · a store door, a keyway, a piece of chalk held against an empty line, a lane fork, an unfinished wall, and a man who turns round · a lamp with a very small wick and a tray of cones and about nine words · a wire basket, sixty-one sheets, a filing clerk's hands, and a box that is always the same · a swept floor, sixty-one trays, a cap with a name in the band, a chalk on a bare board, a broom, and one ordinary sentence about a tray · eleven lines on a piece of sack out of a drawer, a keeper's house, and a rate with nobody's name on it · a pencil, a straight-edge, a piece of window glass worn round at one end, five ruled lines and the moment a man realises what the missing box is for · two women at a bench, a form used twice, and a man's refusal to sign a finding. **No corridor. No copy-room scene at a long table. No chapter opens with a date and a feeling.**
- **Nine of the ten chapters have their load-bearing event be a physical action, a disagreement between two people who are both right, or a thing discovered in an object.** The tenth, Ch 135, is a refusal delivered at a table, and is carried by the cones, the wick, the seed room door and the roll.
- **Nothing was fixed, resolved, entered, recorded, named, or made permanent.** The naming holds in one terrace, is refused in one, and cannot be done at all in one. **It is not a rule, not a practice anybody agreed to, and nobody may make it look permanent: Vukic's own figure is *about eleven weeks* and she says she will know by the season when it has stopped, and the batch is written so that about a month of that is gone at Ch 138 and he has been up that road once and has not been back, and the second telling has not happened and he has not the least idea what a man says the second time.** **The register is a practice and is not made into a remedy: the four questions are the only instrument the batch puts on a table and they are useless for this shape, and the chapter that says so is 131 and the man who says it is a yard-man who is not a clerk.**

### Budgets and guardrails, verified from the files after the repair pass

- **Length, counted with `wc -w` from the files with the title line included, after the repair pass: 25,925 words across ten chapters, inside the 24,000–28,000 plan.** Distribution: Ch 131 2,741 · Ch 132 2,812 · Ch 133 2,450 · Ch 134 2,688 · Ch 135 2,219 · Ch 136 2,487 · Ch 137 2,569 · Ch 138 2,804 · Ch 139 2,493 · Ch 140 2,662. Shortest 2,219, longest 2,812. **A first draft came in at 23,808, which is below the floor, and four chapters were given real material rather than padding: Vukic's spring and an hour and a half at the rail in Ch 132, the cones and a second refused question in Ch 135, a hand on a store door in Ch 134, and the near-miss and the sand in Ch 137.** **Take the count from the files and not from this line.**
- **Kalendar is clean.** A case-insensitive sweep for the twelve real-world month names and the seven weekdays was run on the finished ten files and **returned nothing**; six instances of *Friday* in Ch 133 and one of *Thursday* in Ch 140 were written in the first draft and were replaced with ordinal and rota language, and a first attempt at that replacement used *Tuesday*, which was caught in the second sweep and is gone. Every *March* in the batch is *Crown March*. **No month length is stated, no event is placed after the twentieth of the growth month, and no days are counted backwards across a month boundary.** The batch's only cross-month arithmetic is a month and eleven weeks' worth of an eleven-week term, which is **not** stated as a subtraction.
- **Panel budget: ZERO Ledger Sight panels in ten chapters.** `grep -c "^>"` returns 0 in every file. **Marek's forearm is not described, referred to or looked at, and the word does not appear** — a first draft described the piece of sack as *a forearm long*, which was cut at the repair pass even though it was the sack and not Marek, because the guardrail is on the word.
- **No villain and none manufactured.** Vray is right and refuses twice. Vukic is right and refuses to say yes and refuses to say the paper did her spring. Teran is right and refuses. Ruta Vrabec is right. Janko Vrabec is right three times. Fisk is right and sharp and not calm. Sedge is right and says he does not know. Pribis is right and unhelpful and says so. Tresch, Belic and Petrik are right. **Nobody is shown to be withholding anything they could have said in March, nobody is convicted, nothing is dissolved, no office is closed, and no new faction enters.**
- **No bare column is named or headed.** The rota, the bench book, the oiled board, the piece of sack, the wall list, the register's fifth and sixth lines and the new form's fifth line all stay bare. **The register's rule is not extended, the register is not made into a remedy, and nobody in the batch says it has become one. A register is not a seed.** Petrik is not told to head the board in Ch 140 and does not head it.
- **No locked figure of Volume 03 has been changed and none appears outside its own scene.** The advance is not stated in this batch. No price for anything in the basin is stated. The low ground's rate is not stated. The register's levy, the Tallow figures and the Rainmarket figures do not appear. The Hartlow count of two and the two names on that paper are given once, in Janko's mouth, in a Hartlow scene, and are not to be turned into a number for the other three terraces.
- **Romance: one Kade-and-Marek co-presence in this batch (Ch 138), which is the volume's sixth against `outline/volume-03.md`'s guide of two.** Recorded, not concealed, and the running overage is already flagged in `state/current.md`. **The batch is compliant with its own instruction, which permitted at most one further co-presence in Movement IV.** The scene is inside the tone limit: no first names, no warmth, no thanks, nothing built, no method shared, she does not hold the form or the sack, she leaves, and nobody thanks her and Marek does not thank her.
- **Every verification sentence above was produced by writing the ten chapters, running a repair pass, and grepping the files.** A reviewer should re-read before treating any of it as closed — that is the rule this repository learned the expensive way in Volume 01 batch 0005.

### Repair pass — a full independent review, and what it found

A `novel-reviewer` subagent read Chapters 131–140 against Chs 102, 104, 107, 113, 115, 120, 122–130 and against the outline, the continuity file and the state files, and returned four BLOCKING, nine HIGH, thirteen MEDIUM and seven LOW findings. **No chapter was restarted, no card was moved, and no planned plot changed.** What was fixed, and confirmed by grep afterwards:

- **BLOCKING.** (1) **Ch 138's eleven-week clock was arithmetically impossible** — the fourteenth of the harvest month to the eleventh of the ninth month is a month, not eleven weeks, and "the eleven weeks was not up" was false. Rewritten: about a month of an eleven-week term is gone, the term is not up, and he has not been back. **The same error was in Ch 139**, which had him going back "in about a week" and which misattributed to Vukic the opposite of what she said in Ch 132; both are fixed. (2) **Ch 133 called the drill rows *my daughter's*** and so made Zofia Teran the mother of Stana Peric and put the fifty-eight-year-old holder of the eighth line in two places. Rewritten: the ground is a woman of thirty-one's, held in her mother's name, and the mother is identified as the mother of the woman whose ground it is. (3) **Ch 133 had a first-person *I* in a third-person paragraph** and a day count that did not match Chs 132/133. Both fixed. (4) **Ch 140 silently took a side in a pre-existing conflict between Ch 103 and Ch 127 about how many days Belic was written out of.** Resolved in the prose by making them **two certificates in two months** and putting both on the page, and by removing the uncheckable figure from Tresch's balance.
- **HIGH.** (5) **Ch 131's closing paragraph was Ch 129's closing paragraph**, with an ungrammatical phrase in it. Rewritten as a working fact about a man who keeps a nail. (6) **Verrow was "eleven days up the river"** against a night and a day on the water; corrected. (7) **"Four hundred yards" was used for distances that are days apart**, in ten places across six chapters; all are corrected or removed, and Hartlow is four miles above a stone bridge as Chs 102/123/130 have it. (8) **Ch 136 said the harbour letter was eleven weeks old**; no number of weeks is now stated there at all. (9) **Three pieces of meta prose addressed to the reader and to the volume** — *in this volume* in Ch 133, *the part a reader is likely to miss* and *this is the entire finding* in Ch 137 — all cut. (10) **Ch 137's closing inventory had two factual errors** and restated the batch. Replaced with a working fact. (11) **Ch 138 had the piece of sack two years old** when it is a year. Corrected. (12) **Ch 139 misattributed to Vukic what she said in Ch 132.** Corrected. (13) **Ch 131 and Ch 135 ran the shape batch 0002's review named** — a man explaining a mechanism at length. Ch 131's restatement was cut to one line and its ending rebuilt; Ch 135's longest speech was cut by about a third and a physical act was put inside it, and the cones were put in front of the ask.
- **MEDIUM and LOW, all fixed.** Five chapters that closed on a recap now close on a working fact. Four openings were reworded so the batch does not open nine times on the same construction. **The batch's `about nine` was reduced from forty-seven instances to thirty-three.** Ch 132's narrator no longer awards itself the chapter's insight. Ch 134 no longer restates Ch 113's argument in the narrator's voice. Ch 137's Petrik clause and its service years are fixed. Ch 138's "three weeks" in a wet coat is fixed. Ch 139's bell-time circularity is fixed and his count of his own three repetitions now follows. Ch 140's uncheckable lot arithmetic is removed and the trays are the unit. Ch 136's fourth box no longer carries a number of holdings, which belongs to the granary schedule and not to the Trust form, and its three return volumes now reconcile. Ch 135's lamp date is fixed. Ch 133's unparseable opening line and a narrator editorial are cut.
- **Checked and deliberately NOT changed, recorded so the work is not repeated.** (a) **The word *held* and *hold* is used throughout Chs 131 and 139 in the ordinary sense of a counterweight being kept, and that is the volume's finding.** No Boundary Hold is used, offered, wished for, hinted at or mentioned anywhere in the batch, and the two words are one semantic step apart and must not be read as the power. (b) **A new named character, Damir Pribis, forty-nine, is not in `outline/volume-03.md`'s locked names and not in the name-trap list**, because the prompt's assignment of Malnar to that role contradicts Chs 75, 77, 83 and 88; see the divergence note above. He is a small addition and a later chapter must not confuse Pribis with Malnar, who owns the Under-Row. (c) **Nothing about the missing year, the Last Convocation, the First Account, the Founder's Clause, the crossing year, Edren Sable or A-0 is mentioned in any of the ten chapters, and nobody in Morrowfen knows who A-0 is.** (d) **The Saltglass Harbor Council is not named and is not implied**; Pribis's *we are not the harbour* is a clerk in a granary depot and a harbor notary is not the Council. (e) **Chapter 137 has no question mark in it at all**, which is the mechanical guarantee that Wenna Drel is asked nothing by anybody including the narrator, and that is the check a reviewer should re-run first.
